The Blood in the Fold

The Blood in the Fold

A novel of the Queen’s peace

Part One — What Peace Promised

Chapter One — The Receiving Yard

The woman had brought her husband’s good boots. She put them on Lyra’s table, one beside the other, with a pair of woollen stockings stuffed down the shafts.

“He’ll want these.”

Lyra moved the ink.

Outside, somebody was teaching the men in the yard a song about a captain’s horse. The singer had reached the same line three times without finding a rhyme, and each failure had brought him more assistance. Through the open shutters came the smell of damp straw and the hot, sweet breath of the malt-house next door. Its wall had kept the morning cold. Lyra had been rubbing her hands beneath the table when the woman came in.

“Is he here?” she asked.

“He’s against that wall. The one doing nothing.”

There were several. Lyra stood far enough from the window to see past the edge of the shutter. Men sat on bundles, on the rails enclosing the horse trough, on a broken cart which someone had turned over to make a bench. Three were playing at dice in a shallow wooden bowl. A fourth watched with the injured dignity of a man whose own money had gone into it.

The woman pointed.

“That one. Matthis.”

He was sitting by himself under a patched awning, holding a strap between his knees. His left boot had opened across the toes. He had worked the strap through it and was trying to buckle it without bending his foot.

“He could come inside.”

“He’s been inside. He was told to wait.”

“By me?”

“By whoever was here before you.”

The stool beneath Lyra’s table had been warmed by another clerk that morning. He had gone to find the return which the captain said he had already supplied, leaving her his knife, a crust, and a warning about the woman with the boots.

Lyra drew a sheet towards her.

“Your name?”

“Perrine.”

“And what were you told?”

“That he can leave when he’s paid.”

“Does he wish to leave before then?”

“I should think he wishes to leave last week. There’s work waiting for him, if we get there before the carter takes somebody else.”

The singing stopped. A man shouted an answer which made the others laugh, and Matthis looked up from his foot. Perrine saw him and raised a hand. He smiled at her through the window.

She turned back before the smile had gone from her face.

“We came in the cart,” she said. “I asked the man to keep him a place on the way home. He’s got hides. He wants somebody who can keep hold of a horse when it smells them.”

“Where is he going?”

“South.”

“How far?”

“As far as we need.”

Lyra waited.

Perrine put a finger inside one of the boots and pulled up the stocking. She smoothed its folded heel.

“Across the river. We’re from Istovar. I didn’t think it was important.”

“It may be why they want him paid first.”

“Then they’d best pay him.”

Lyra wrote Matthis and left space for the rest. She could hear the other clerk coming down the passage. His gait had a quick little drag where the sole of one shoe caught the flags; she had learned it during the week she had spent asking him for things. He came in carrying two rolls and a square of bread with cheese pressed into it.

“Still here?” he said to Perrine.

“Yes.”

He gave Lyra the rolls.

“The first is the order. The second is what the captain sent. You’ll see the difficulty.” He took his knife from beside the inkwell and cut the bread across. “I found these in the upper press. Somebody has put the river matters under roads.”

“They came by road,” Perrine said.

He looked at her. Then he gave Lyra half the bread.

She unrolled the order. It had been read enough for the outer edge to curl back as soon as she released it. A guarantee covered men retained for the passage of stores after the settlement: wages until their release, provisions on the return journey, and payment through a receiving officer appointed by the Crown. The officer had been named in the margin. His name had been struck out and another entered above it.

The captain’s return was a list of men, days, and sums. Matthis appeared halfway down. Beside his name a different hand had written departed.

“When did your husband reach Valora?”

“Yesterday, with the rest.”

She showed Perrine the entry. Perrine bent over it without touching the sheet.

“He went to fetch me.”

“From where?”

“The hides. I was at the gate.”

“How long was he gone?”

“Long enough to carry my bag.”

The clerk swallowed his bread.

“Has the captain put him back?”

“He’s outside,” Perrine said.

“In the return.”

“He can come in.”

“I mean—”

“I know what you mean.” She picked up the boots. “I’ll get him.”

She went before Lyra could ask her to wait. The clerk watched her cross the yard. Matthis struggled upright, failed to fasten the strap, and followed her with his foot held awkwardly inside the split leather.

“Seven like that,” the clerk said. “Out to find a brother. Out to sell a knife. Out to pray, according to one, though the captain saw him come back from the wine stall. They’ll all be entitled again by supper.”

“What happens at supper?”

“The victualler counts them.”

“And yesterday?”

“He’d made the count before they went.”

Lyra put the two rolls beside each other.

“So they were fed as present and marked as gone.”

“The captain didn’t know if they meant to return.”

“He could have asked.”

“He could.” The clerk brushed crumbs off his coat. “He might have had to listen to the answer.”

Captain Roul arrived with Matthis and a young man carrying a bundle of belts. He took off his cap before he entered, exposing a strip of forehead much paler than his face, and held it against his chest while Perrine made Matthis sit.

“I was told there was a question about his discharge.”

“There are seven questions,” Lyra said.

“I’ll answer for mine.”

“They’re all yours.”

He came to the table. Behind him Perrine knelt and cut away the strap with the clerk’s knife. Matthis objected when she took hold of the boot. She ignored him until he caught her wrist.

“Wait.”

“Then stop pushing.”

“I’m not pushing.”

“You’re making it harder.”

“Perrine.”

She looked at his foot. Her hands became careful. The captain kept his eyes on the return.

Lyra asked him when he had last seen the seven men named as departed. He answered for five. Of the others, one had been asleep under the cart when the list was made; the other was the man carrying the belts.

“What were you doing?” she asked him.

“Looking for these.”

“His sword-belt was taken while he slept,” the captain said.

The young man lifted the bundle. “I found three of ours at the pawnbroker’s. I thought if I brought the whole lot—”

“Were you intending to leave the company?”

“No.” He hesitated. “I’m intending to now.”

The captain held out his hand for the belts. The young man passed them over one at a time, unwilling to lose sight of the buckles. Roul laid them on the end of the table.

“Correct the return,” Lyra said.

“I can’t swear to days after a man goes.”

“You can swear to the morning when you saw him asleep under that cart.”

“And tonight?”

“If he is released today, you won’t be paying him for tonight.”

“With what?”

She moved the order towards him.

He read the struck-out name in the margin and gave a short breath through his nose.

“That man had the chest. The new one says he has the office. I’d be grateful if they could meet.”

The clerk lowered his eyes to his bread. Lyra read the correction again. The new officer had acknowledged his appointment three days earlier. There was no acknowledgement of the money.

“Where is the chest?”

“I’ve been asking since we reached the walls.”

“And you kept the men because—”

“Because if I send them off with another promise, half will never collect it. Some can’t write. Some won’t get across the river again for a harvest’s wages. The old fellow by the horse trough thinks he’s been paid because a sergeant gave him a silver piece out of his own pocket.” Roul turned his cap in his hands. “I brought them here to be paid.”

“And he’s got to be at the south gate before noon,” Perrine said.

Matthis had his new boots on. The stockings were pulled high under his breeches, hiding the rubbed skin above the ankle. He stood to try them, then sat again before anybody remarked on it.

Lyra went to the window. The men had begun another song, one they knew. Beyond them, above the low buildings of the yard, the palace roofs lay in sunlight. She could get to Pellam before noon if he had returned from the audience. If he had not, she could wait in the passage while Matthis’s carter left.

She turned to the copying clerk.

“Will you take a message?”

“Where?”

“To the chancellor. Ask which officer holds the funds for this return. Say the men are here and some have transport waiting. I need a direction today.”

He looked at the two rolls.

“Shall I take those?”

“Copies.”

“They’ll ask for the original.”

“Tell them where it is.”

He reached for a fresh sheet. Lyra took it from him.

“I’ll copy. You eat.”

Elyas came through the yard while she was still writing.

She knew his voice before she saw him. One of the men had called him over to look at a hand, and he was telling the man that he was poorly qualified to give an opinion. The answer came back that a prince ought to know whether a hand was worth keeping. Elyas said he had never had enough to spare one.

Perrine was at the door. She moved aside for him, then stopped moving when she understood who he was.

“My lord.”

“Sir will do.”

“Will it?” she said.

He glanced at Lyra. She was trying not to smile over the wet page.

“It generally has.”

“Then, sir, can you get my husband out of here?”

He listened. That took time. Perrine began with the cart, Roul with the guarantee, and Matthis with an explanation of why he had left the yard. Elyas made them return to each in turn. Lyra finished the copy and gave it to the clerk, who wrapped the last of his bread and went.

“What would release them?” Elyas asked her.

“The captain can release them now.”

“And their money?”

“The claim remains. Whether they’ll be able to collect it from home is what he’s worried about.”

Roul nodded.

“Quite,” Elyas said. “And I can’t find a chest you’ve mislaid by promising to find it.”

“I haven’t mislaid it, sir.”

“I beg your pardon. Somebody has.”

He looked towards the yard. Matthis had begun rubbing his old boot with a piece of cloth, preparing to put it in Perrine’s bag.

“Let them choose,” Elyas said. “The ones who stay are fed under the order. The ones who go take a copy of what they’re owed. Put my name on it. If the Crown won’t pay, I will.”

Roul looked up sharply.

Lyra laid down her pen.

“Have you read the sum?”

“No.”

“Read it.”

He took the return. She watched his eyes move down the page. At the bottom he stopped and looked back at the first names.

The room had become still enough for her to hear Perrine winding the worn strap round the old boots.

“All right,” he said.

“No, Elyas. What is all right?”

“The sum.”

“You’re guaranteeing that sum to these men. Not whatever another officer adds to it tomorrow.”

“Yes.”

“And when may they ask you for it?”

He glanced at Roul. “A month?”

“Too long for some.”

“A fortnight, then.”

Lyra drew the return back. In the space beneath the last name she wrote the undertaking, identifying the men and the amount. Her pen caught where the paper had been folded. She lifted it and began the word again beside the rough place.

Elyas signed.

Perrine watched him do it. “Where do we find you?”

He gave her the address of the house where he and Mara were staying.

“We’ll be south of the river,” she said. “You know that.”

“I know.”

“You’ll not tell us then to ask somebody here?”

“You may ask me.”

She waited until Lyra had read the terms aloud, then looked at Matthis. He was already standing, with his old boots under his arm.

Roul went outside and called his men together. The one with the belts began distributing them, charging each owner a share of what he had paid. A dispute rose immediately about whether a buckle had been scratched before it went missing. Matthis and Perrine left through the little gate. She walked quickly; after a few steps she slowed, and he put his hand beneath her elbow as though the difficulty were hers.

Elyas followed them with his eyes.

“You might have asked me first,” Lyra said.

“You’d have said wait.”

“I would have asked how much you had.”

He looked back at the table.

“How much time do I have to find out?”

“A fortnight.”

He laughed, a little ruefully. She did not join him. He drew a stool over and sat.

“Will you make me a copy?”

“Two. One for you, one for Mara.”

“That is unkind.”

“She’ll ask better questions.”

“She usually does.”

Lyra took another sheet. Through the window she could see Roul counting men who were still deciding whether to stay. He started again whenever somebody moved from one group to the other.

“Why were you here?” she asked.

“The man at the gate sent for me. Two of these fellows served on the Scar. He thought I might know which captain ought to answer for them.”

“And do you?”

“I do now.”

She gave him the copy when it had dried.

On his way out he helped the man with the swollen hand lift his bundle onto the cart. The man objected to the way he held it. Elyas changed his grip.

Lyra turned back to the order. She copied the name of the officer who had surrendered the office and the name of the man who had accepted it. Beneath them she left a space for the person who could tell her where the money was.

Chapter Two — The South Road

Mara read the undertaking while he held a bowl for her.

There were strips of linen in it, boiled and cooling in water. She had been carrying it out to the yard when he came home, and had given it to him without interrupting her account of a woman at the station who would insist on standing to stir a pot after being told to stay off a damaged foot. The station had fewer beds each week. The linen came home with Mara because the copper there had cracked and nobody would pay to have it mended for a house that was closing. At the mention of damaged feet he had begun to tell her about Matthis. He had got as far as the guarantee before she asked for the paper.

A drop of water ran from her wrist to the corner. She wiped her hand on her apron and read on.

Berent stood in the doorway eating an apple. A bruise the colour of an old plum lay along his thumb. He had tried to catch a falling saddle that morning by its stirrup, and Elyas had been obliged to explain why this was a poor way of saving leather.

“How much have we?” Mara asked.

“In Valora?”

She looked at him over the page.

“Yes,” he said. “I’ll find out.”

“If you have to pay this, we cannot hire the cart.” She looked at the sum again. “And we owe the woman for these.” She nodded towards the bowl. “And the lodgings.”

“The lodgings are paid.”

“To the end of the month.”

“Yes.”

“It’s nearly the end of the month.”

Berent took another bite of apple. Elyas turned towards him.

“Have you somewhere to be?”

“No, sir.”

“Find somewhere.”

Mara gave the paper back.

“He can stay. Take the bowl out, Berent. It goes on the bench in the shade. Don’t wring anything out.”

Berent put his apple down and carried the bowl out with both hands.

Mara watched him clear the threshold.

“You could have brought them here to speak to me.”

“The cart was leaving.”

“Then you could have sent me word. I might have known somebody going the same way.”

He had expected a quarrel about money. He found he had been preparing an answer to it all the way home, and that the answer was of no use.

“There wasn’t much time.”

“There was enough time to find you.”

He put the paper on the table. Their dinner lay beside it under a cloth: bread, a bowl of soft cheese, onions with soil still caught among the roots. The room was too small for the chest they had brought up from the carrier’s yard. They had pushed it beneath the table, where he struck it with his shin whenever he forgot.

“I’m sorry,” he said.

“I know you are. I’m not asking you to be.” She reached past him for the knife. “The station closes at the end of the month. The house has been standing empty since the pear came into leaf. We’re meant to be leaving next week. If those men come after us, who tells them where we’ve gone?”

“I’ll leave an address.”

“With someone who can read it.”

“Yes.”

“And if you’re not there when they arrive?”

He sat on the edge of the chair.

“Then they wait for me.”

“Where?”

Berent came in with the empty place in his hands where the bowl had been. He looked from one to the other, found his apple, and left again.

Mara cut an onion in half. She kept the larger piece and put the other beside Elyas’s plate.

“You can eat,” she said. “I’m not going to improve if you look hungry.”

He went back to the receiving yard that afternoon.

The malt-house was letting out hot water into the gutter. Two boys had dammed it with broken tiles and were floating nutshells through the breach. Elyas waited for a cart to pass before crossing. The driver saluted with his whip. The nearer boy asked if he was a knight.

“Yes.”

“Where’s your horse?”

“In a stable.”

“My uncle’s a knight.”

“Is he?”

“No,” said the other boy.

Elyas stepped over their dam. The first boy called after him that his uncle had a horse, anyway.

Roul sat under the awning with a cup between his knees. Most of the yard had emptied. The overturned cart remained, occupied by the man with the injured hand and a companion carefully cleaning dirt out of the seams of his own boots.

“They went?” Elyas asked.

“Most.”

“Matthis?”

“Your clerk got them away.”

“She isn’t my clerk.”

Roul looked towards the window. It stood open, but the room beyond was empty.

“She’d be a useful one.”

“She has work already.”

Elyas sat on a sack beside him. Through a gap in the awning a narrow bar of sun fell on Roul’s cup. It had been repaired at the handle with wire.

“May I ask what you’re waiting for?”

“My return.”

“You gave it to her.”

“The one that lets me go home.”

Roul took up the cup, found it empty, and set it down again.

“I need someone to accept the men. I can count them when I leave the camp. I can bring them here. Until somebody accepts them, I’ve still got them.”

“They’re gone.”

“Yes, sir.”

“And you’re here.”

“Yes.”

Elyas looked at the two men on the cart.

“Does the queen know?”

Roul’s eyes moved towards him. For a moment he seemed to be considering the safest answer.

“I’ve written to the receiving officer.”

“I meant about all this.”

“I don’t write to the queen.”

Elyas leaned forward, elbows on his knees. The man cleaning his boots had begun on the sole. He used a bent nail, drawing out bits of straw and gravel and flicking them into the dust.

“You could have asked before you marked them gone,” Elyas said.

“I could.”

“They might have answered.”

“They would have answered that they meant to come back.”

“And you didn’t believe them?”

“Two of them went the night before we marched. I gave their money to their corporal to send home. At the next town they came to collect it.” Roul looked at the cup. “They hadn’t gone home. They’d been drunk behind a baker’s. One was a good man. The other was a good man when he could find somebody worse to compare himself with.”

“Did the corporal get it back?”

“From his wife. She’d used it.”

“And now?”

“He won’t speak to me. His wife will.”

The man on the cart laughed without looking up. Roul turned his head.

“Carry on, Bennet. You’ve only the other foot.”

Elyas took a coin from his purse and gave it to the boy passing the gate with a pitcher. He bought two cups of watered ale and waited while the boy rinsed Roul’s cup. The captain drank half before setting it down.

“The queen promised,” Roul said. “I heard the order read. It wasn’t somebody saying we’d be looked after. It named the days. I told them.”

Elyas had heard such readings. Men standing in mud, caps off, faces raised towards a dry man under a shelter. He remembered watching for the moment when belief passed through them. Sometimes it was a cheer. Sometimes a man only looked at his neighbour and lifted his shoulders a little, as though the pack had become bearable.

“We’ll get your return accepted,” he said.

Roul rubbed a finger along the wire round the handle.

“Thank you.”

This time Elyas heard Mara before he went further. Who tells them where we’ve gone?

“Where will you be tomorrow?”

“Here.”

“Till when?”

“Till somebody gives me leave.”

Lyra found him at the gate an hour later.

She was walking fast, carrying a leather folder under her arm. The copying clerk came behind her with a small chest slung between himself and a porter. Neither was enjoying it. The porter wanted to stop before the threshold; the clerk wanted to be inside before they did. They put it down halfway through and stood looking at one another.

“Someone found it,” Elyas said.

“Someone found a different one.” Lyra waited for the men to lift again. “The first has reached a storehouse outside the west gate. Its officer thinks he’s waiting for a direction to bring it in. The receiving officer thinks he’s refused to bring it in. Pellam has sent a man with both directions.”

“Then what’s this?”

“An advance. Against the sum already authorised.”

“From whom?”

“The treasury. Fayd found the acknowledgement of the transfer. It was with his river correspondence.”

Elyas watched the chest disappear into the room. The captain had risen from beneath the awning.

“He found it quickly.”

“He was asked quickly.”

She was flushed from the walk. A strand of hair had come loose beside her cheek; when she pushed it back he saw ink on the heel of her hand.

“You’re cross with me.”

“I was cross with you this morning. This afternoon I’ve been cross with three other people. You’ll have to wait.”

“Can I help?”

“Yes. Tell the captain to bring the men who are still here. I need them to hear how the rest will be paid.”

He went to Roul. Behind him, Lyra called to the clerk to keep the door clear.

They counted the money twice. The first count was short. The second found the missing coins in a smaller bag which had fallen beneath the chest’s lining. The man with the bad hand began laughing and could not stop for some time.

Lyra read the direction. Men still in Valora would receive their money in the yard. Those who had left would be paid through named receiving houses on their routes, on production of the copies she had given them. The officer responsible would acknowledge each payment to the chancery. Any sum uncollected at the end of the term remained due.

Bennet asked whether the paper had to be whole. His copy had torn while he was trying to make room for it in his boot.

“Both pieces?” Lyra asked.

He produced them.

“Yes. Keep them together.”

He put them on the table, side by side, and laid the bent nail across the join.

Roul signed for the men present. When his own discharge was put before him he read it from the first line to the last. Elyas watched his mouth move at one point. He stopped, went back, and read the sentence again.

“What is it?”

“Nothing.” Roul put the sheet down. “It’s right.”

He signed.

When the men had gone outside with their money, Lyra showed Elyas the list of receiving houses. She had left Matthis’s entry unassigned.

“Where exactly are they going?”

He looked at the blank beside the name.

“South. Into Istovar.”

“I know that.”

“The cart had hides.”

“So did two others.”

He remembered Perrine asking where they would find him. He had offered her an address and never thought to ask for hers.

“I’ll go to the gate.”

“The keeper may remember which carter.”

“I’ll ask.”

“Ask where he was bound. Not which way he went.”

She gave him a slip bearing the particulars they knew. He read it before putting it away.

At the south gate he found a man who remembered Perrine because she had quarrelled with the carter over the space allowed for her bag. Matthis had lifted a bundle of hides to make room. The keeper knew the carter’s name and the village where he usually slept, and thought he would be there by nightfall if his off horse held up.

Elyas wrote both down. His letters looked clumsy on the little board the man lent him. He had to ask for the name again.

Outside the gate the road ran between fields where the wheat had risen above the ditches. A woman and two children were working along the edge of one, pulling weeds into baskets. Farther off, a team had stopped at a turn. One horse shook its head against the flies while the man at the plough adjusted something Elyas could not see.

He could send Berent after the cart in the morning. It would give the boy a useful journey and a household to remember. He could tell Mara where the message was going before he sent it.

On his way back he bought onions to replace the ones they had eaten. The woman selling them asked whether he wanted a string or a basket.

“How many in a string?”

She lifted one for him to see.

“That many,” she said.

He bought the string. She threw in a small loose onion whose outer skin had split.

“For your trouble,” she said, without much sympathy.

Mara was in the yard when he returned, taking the dry linen off the bench. He held up the onions. She looked at them, then at him.

“Have we acquired more people?”

“No.”

“Good. Put those in the kitchen.”

He did. Then he came back to tell her where Matthis and Perrine would be paid.

Berent left before it was light.

He had asked to go. Elyas had meant to give him the errand as a favour and found it already claimed, with the horse saddled and a heel of bread in the boy’s coat. Mara had written out the particulars on a clean slip: Matthis’s name, Perrine’s, the carter’s village, the receiving house nearest their road and the day from which it would pay. Beneath it she had added the name of a woman who kept a bakehouse in that village and would give a stranger a bench for an hour if he asked for her by name.

“How do you know her?” Elyas asked.

“She had a son at the station.”

“Did he live?”

“He lives in her bakehouse and complains about the ovens.”

Berent read the slip twice and put it inside his shirt. Elyas watched him mount, then caught the bridle before he could turn out of the yard.

“If they’ve gone on, don’t chase them into Istovar.”

“How far is into?”

“Past the first river.”

“And if they’re just past it?”

“Then you will have found a very good reason to come home.”

Berent grinned, which Elyas took for agreement, and was wrong. He was gone three days. He came back with the horse lame in the near fore and an account that took most of supper, because every part of it required some earlier part to be understood.

The carter had slept where the gatekeeper said. The off horse had not held up. Matthis had spent the night walking beside it with a wet cloth tied round its hock while the carter drank and Perrine sat on the hides and told both men what she thought of the arrangement. Berent had found them in the morning, a mile short of the river, the carter asleep and Matthis feeding the horse from his own hat.

“Did you give them the paper?”

“I read it to them.” Berent tore bread in half. “She made me read it twice. Then she took it and read it herself.”

“She can read?” Mara asked.

“Better than me. She said so.”

“Was she right?”

Berent considered. “She was quicker.”

He had ridden back as far as the receiving house with them, because the carter now needed to get over the river before his horse failed altogether, and Perrine would not let Matthis walk the last miles to Istovar with his old boots in a bag and the new ones rubbing. At the receiving house the keeper had looked at the copy, said he had heard nothing about it, and put it on a shelf.

“On a shelf,” Elyas said.

“That’s what I said.”

“What did he say?”

“That the shelf was where things went until they were sent for.” Berent looked at Mara. “So I asked him to write down that he had it. Lyra said to. The day and his name and what it was for.”

“Did he?”

“He said he’d never been asked.”

“And?”

“Perrine asked him.”

Mara laughed. Berent tried to keep eating, but that set Elyas off, and the boy had to put down his bread.

“What did she say?” Mara asked.

Berent swallowed. “Asked whether he needed a shelf to put the pen on first.”

Elyas laughed until he had to wipe his eyes. He wanted to meet Perrine.

For eleven days nothing happened that he could see.

He went to the receiving yard twice. The first time Roul was gone, released under his accepted return; the man with the bad hand had gone with him, and a woman was sweeping the space beneath the awning with the thoroughness of someone who had been told to make it look as though no one had ever waited there. The second time the room where Lyra had worked was locked. A clerk in the passage told him the business of the Scar returns had been moved upstairs, and that the lady who had been sitting there went now to the chancellor’s rooms in the mornings and somewhere else in the afternoons.

“Where?”

The clerk looked at him with an expression Elyas had seen on servants at Tarlington when asked where the queen had gone.

“Elsewhere, my lord.”

He counted money instead. It was a poor substitute and he did it badly. The sum he had signed for was more than he had in Valora and less than he had altogether; the rest was in Istovar, in rents which came in when they came, and in a horse he had meant to keep. Mara watched him lay it out on the table one evening, the coins in their little stacks, and asked what he intended to do if the Crown did not pay.

“Sell the grey.”

“You love the grey.”

“I promised.”

“You promised a sum. You didn’t promise the grey.” She moved one stack a finger’s width to the left, so that it stood by itself. “That is the station’s. Don’t touch it.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

“You were looking at it.”

He put the coins back into their purse. On the twelfth day he stopped going to the receiving yard, not because he had given up but because Mara said a man who kept visiting a place to see whether a promise had been kept was telling everyone there that he doubted it.

On the fourteenth a boy came to the door with a scrap of paper folded small and sealed with a thumbprint of candle wax. He had been paid in advance and wanted no answer. Elyas broke the wax standing in the passage.

The writing was not Perrine’s. It was a clerk’s hand, tidy and uninterested, and it said that the receiving house at the river had paid Matthis in full on production of his copy, and had entered the payment on the day, and that the direction concerning the other men had been received. Beneath it, in a different ink and a larger, less certain hand, somebody had added:

You need not sell anything. Tell the woman who wrote it down that the shelf keeper knows her name now. P.

He read it to Mara in the kitchen. She was shelling beans into her apron and did not stop.

“So you were right,” she said.

“The money came.”

“You told them they could go and they would be paid. They went. They were paid.”

“Lyra found the money.”

“Yes.” She tipped the beans into a bowl. “You were both right. It will be very tiresome for everybody.”

He took the scrap upstairs and put it with the undertaking in the chest beneath the table. The next day he walked to the chancery and waited in a passage for most of the morning to give Lyra the second half of the message. When she came out she was carrying three rolls against her chest and had ink on her chin, and she listened to the whole of it without putting the rolls down.

“He wrote it in the book?”

“On the day.”

She was quiet for a moment.

“Good,” she said. Then, with an effort he could see: “You were right to let them go.”

“You were right to make me read the sum.”

“I know.”

He waited for her to smile. She shifted the rolls to her other arm instead and looked past him down the passage, at a door that had just opened.

“I have to be somewhere,” she said. “Thank Perrine for me, if you write.”

“I don’t know where to write.”

“The receiving house. The man with the shelf.” Now she did smile, briefly. “He knows her name.”

Chapter Three — Where an Answer Stops

Fayd’s room was being painted.

The smell drove them into the passage, where a servant had put two chairs beside a window and abandoned a third with a broken seat. Fayd offered Lyra the better chair and remained standing because the other was occupied by papers.

“The receiving yard,” he said. “How many places did you ask?”

“Before you?”

“Altogether.”

She told him. He wrote them down, asked which had answered in person, and crossed one out when she said its reply had merely redirected her to the first.

“Would you do this for the other receiving houses?”

“Find where the money is?”

“Find where an answer stops.”

“Pellam has officers.”

“He has written to them.”

“And you want to know whether what they wrote back is true.”

“I want to know where to send the next chest.”

A painter came down the passage carrying a pot. Fayd moved a chair to let him pass. The man asked whether the window in the room should be left open overnight. Fayd said yes, then asked whether rain would spoil the work. The painter looked out at the clear sky before answering.

When he had gone, Lyra said, “I won’t give you everyone who complained.”

“I didn’t ask for them.”

“You may need the name of an officer. You don’t need the woman who brought the boots.”

“Will withholding her name prevent an answer?”

“No.”

“Then withhold it.”

He took the upper sheet from the chair and gave it to her. Three houses, the authority for the inquiry, a sum for expenses. She could finish before the end of the month. She read it again while he waited.

“I report to Pellam as well.”

“Of course.”

“And if your office is where it stopped?”

“Tell me before he does.”

She looked up. He smiled, with enough fatigue in it to make the request sound reasonable.

Pellam read the authority standing at his desk, with his spectacles pushed up onto his forehead where they did him no good.

“He asked you himself.”

“In the passage. His room is being painted.”

“I know. I can smell it from here.” He put the sheet down and pulled the spectacles back into place. “You will copy everything you send him to me.”

“Yes.”

“Not afterwards. With it.”

“Yes.”

He looked at her over the frames. Pellam had taken her into the chancery during the war because she could read a return and find the place where it disagreed with itself, and because a man he trusted had asked him to. He had kept her because she did not mind being disliked by the officers whose returns disagreed with themselves. He had never, so far as she knew, asked her to like Fayd.

“You needn’t go yourself,” he said. “There are riders.”

“Riders bring back what they were asked for.”

“That is generally what one wants.”

“The receiving yard sent back what it was asked for. It took seven days to discover that nobody had asked where the chest was.”

Pellam sighed. It was his way of agreeing to something he would later have to explain.

“Take Jehan. He needs to learn which end of a horse to avoid.”

Jehan was the copying clerk who had given her half his bread in the receiving yard. He rode as though the saddle had been designed by someone who disliked him, and he talked, when he was frightened, about food. By the first milestone Lyra knew what his mother put in a pie, what his mother’s sister put in a better one, and why neither of them would speak to the woman at the end of their street, who used lard.

The first house was the storehouse outside the west gate, where the missing chest had waited for a direction while the receiving officer waited for it to arrive. The officer there was a broad, careful man named Hamon who kept his keys on a cord round his neck and touched them whenever a question worried him. He showed Lyra the order he had received and the order he had been waiting for. The first told him to hold the chest until a proper direction came. The second, when it came, had named the old receiving officer.

“Whose name was struck out,” Lyra said.

“Not on mine.”

He showed her. On his copy the old name stood clean. Nobody had told the man at the storehouse that the office had changed hands, and so when the new officer sent for the money Hamon had refused him, correctly, because the man was not the one named.

“Did you tell anybody why you refused?”

“I told the man who came.”

“What did he do?”

“Went away.” Hamon touched the keys. “I thought he would come back with the right paper. He came back with another man who shouted.”

“And then?”

“Then I thought I had better keep the chest very carefully indeed.”

Jehan laughed and turned it into a cough. Lyra wrote down the dates. Hamon watched her pen as though it might be about to record something against him, and she read him what she had written before she left, so that he would know it had not.

The second house was a day’s ride south, at the river crossing where the Istovar road came down to the water. It was a stone building with a new roof and an old door, and the man who kept it had put a bench outside for people to wait on and a board above the bench on which he wrote, in chalk, what he could pay that day and to whom. The board said Nothing when they arrived. Two women were sitting beneath it, one nursing a child, the other mending a sock with a needle she kept losing.

The keeper had a shelf.

He showed it to Lyra with some pride. Every direction he had received since the peace lay on it, weighted with a stone, in the order of their arrival. A dozen copies of men’s claims lay on it too, each tied with a thread. He knew which was which without reading them.

“Why do none of these say paid?”

“Because I haven’t been told I may.”

“You have the direction for the Scar men.”

“I have a direction.” He took it down. “It says pay on production of the copy, through the receiving officer appointed by the Crown. It doesn’t say who that is.”

She read it. The direction was sound. It had been written by someone who knew exactly who the receiving officer was and had assumed everybody else would know too. The keeper had written twice to ask, once to the old officer and once to the treasury. The treasury had answered that the question should go to the receiving officer.

“And the old officer?”

“Never answered.”

“Did your letter reach him?”

He shrugged. “It reached the man who carries letters.”

Lyra sat on the bench beside the woman with the sock. The woman moved her mending to make room and said, without looking up, that she had been coming every fourth day since the spring and would come on the fifth if the fourth ever grew tiresome.

“Whose claim?”

“My brother’s. He’s dead.” She found the needle. “He’s owed his days to the end of the month he died in. They told us at the muster. So I come.”

Lyra did not ask her name. She asked instead when the brother’s company had been released, and under which captain, and wrote down the answers. When the woman had finished the heel she held it up to the light, found a thin place she had missed, and began on it without complaint.

That night, in the inn by the crossing, Jehan asked why she had not taken the woman’s name.

“Because she didn’t give it.”

“You could have asked.”

“And then I would have it.”

He considered this over a bowl of beans, which he had declared inferior to his aunt’s before tasting them and then eaten with every sign of contentment.

“Somebody will ask you who complained.”

“Somebody will ask me where the money stopped. That isn’t the same question, though it’s often asked by the same man.”

He looked at her with the particular caution of a young clerk who has begun to suspect he is being taught something.

“Is that for Master Fayd?”

“It’s for whoever asks.”

In the morning she went back to the keeper and asked him for the letter he had sent to the old officer, or his copy of it. He had a copy. It was addressed with great care, and it had been sent not to the receiving office at all but to the office which had paid the river crossings during the war, because the old officer had been paid through it and the keeper had always sent him things there. That office had been folded into Fayd’s at the peace, along with its correspondence and its clerks.

She sat with the copy on her knee for some time.

The third house needed no ride. It was in Valora, at the end of the street of the tanners, and its failure was of a different kind. The receiving officer there had money, had directions, had the right name, and paid. He also paid a man who had come twice under two names, and he had not paid a woman who had come once with a copy that had been rained on, because he could not read the sum. Lyra spent a morning with him and his book. By noon he had found the man who had come twice and gone very pale. By the afternoon he had written to the woman whose copy was wet and asked her to bring the pieces.

“I shall have to report myself,” he said.

“You shall have to report the payment.”

“Is there a difference?”

“Yes,” Lyra said. “The report is about the money.”

He looked at her as though she had offered him a chair he did not deserve. She wrote the return in a way that set down the double payment, the date, the sum, and the steps he had taken, and did not set down the word negligent, which she had heard two chancery clerks use of him in a passage a week before.

She wrote the return for Fayd at her own table, in the room above the seamstress’s shop.

It was a narrow room with a good window, a bed behind a hanging, and a table that rocked unless she put a folded paper beneath its shortest leg. On the table lay Gyllan’s whetstone. She had never sharpened anything on it. She used it to hold down the corner of whatever she was writing, and sometimes, when a sentence would not come, she turned it over in her hand until the grey stone warmed.

Below, the seamstress was quarrelling with a customer about the length of a sleeve. The customer wanted it to reach his knuckles. The seamstress wanted to know whether he intended to write with it.

Lyra set out what she had found.

At the tanners’ street the answer had not stopped at all; it had gone wrong in the other direction. She named Hamon, because his conduct had been correct and would need to be defended. She named the keeper at the river and gave him credit for the copy. She named the officer at the tanners’ street, because the payment was his. She did not name the woman with the sock or the woman with the wet copy, and she said so, in a sentence at the end: Claimants who spoke to me did so to assist the inquiry and are not identified. Their claims can be found by company and date.

Then she wrote the same thing again for Pellam, and a third time, shorter, for herself.

When she had finished she took Fayd’s copy and folded it with the written side inward. She had learned it from Gyllan on the road in Acasia, where he had kept every useful paper folded that way and said that a man who could read upside down deserved at least to have to try.

Fayd read it in his room, which now smelled of paint only faintly and had one wall a colour she privately thought of as underdone pastry.

He read the river section twice. Then he rang for a clerk and sent him to find the river correspondence for the spring, and they sat without speaking while the man was gone. Fayd turned a pen over between his fingers. Lyra watched the new wall and wondered whether he had chosen it.

The clerk came back with a bundle. The keeper’s letter was in it, opened, read, and filed under the river crossing it had come from.

Fayd looked at it for a moment.

“Somebody here read this and put it away because it was about a crossing.”

“Yes.”

“And the officer it was meant for never heard of it.”

“He was relieved of the office a week after it arrived. He may not have heard of much.”

Fayd set the letter down beside her return. “You told me first.”

“You asked me to.”

“Pellam will have this within the hour.”

“It’s already on his desk. I left it on my way here.”

For a moment she thought he would be angry. His face did something small and then settled, and when he spoke it was in the tone of a man noting the weather.

“That is within the terms.”

“Yes.”

“The claimants.” He tapped the last sentence. “You’ve kept them out.”

“You said I might.”

“I did.” He read the sentence again. “Is there any claimant whose name would change what I do next?”

She thought of the woman on the bench with her needle, coming every fourth day.

“No.”

“Then leave it.” He wrote something on the corner of her return and turned it for her to read: Send direction to river house today. Name the officer. “Will that do?”

“It will do for the river.”

“And the rest?”

“The rest is Pellam’s.”

He smiled at that, properly this time.

On the way out she passed the open door of an audience room. Petitioners were leaving. A clerk was showing them out, and beyond him, near the window, the queen stood with her head bent to hear an older man who was holding his hat in both hands.

Lyra did not stop. She had a paper to deliver to the tanners’ street before the office there closed, and she had promised the officer she would bring it herself, so that he would not have to wonder what it said.

Chapter Four — Outside the Baker’s

She saw the mare before she saw the rider.

It had its nose through the baker’s open shutter, investigating the loaves with the unhurried interest of an animal that expected to be forgiven. A tall man with a stick under his arm was reaching past its neck to rescue a loaf, and an old woman with a basket had caught her sleeve on a nail in the shop wall and was swearing at it in a steady, practised voice. Between the woman and the horse, holding the reins as if she had forgotten they were in her hand, stood Riarhin.

Lyra stopped at the mouth of the lane.

She had the officer’s paper in her hand, folded, and she was aware of it the way one is aware of a cup that might spill. One of her hairpins had begun to slip. She caught it without looking away.

“You’re late,” she said.

Riarhin turned. For a moment she did nothing at all, and Lyra saw her face do what faces do when a thing expected for a long time arrives in an ordinary street.

“I didn’t give you a day.”

“Your letter said before the end of summer.”

“It’s summer.”

“It is almost supper.”

Then she had her arms round Riarhin and the reins had gone somewhere and the mare had taken advantage of it. There was road dust in the seam of Riarhin’s coat. She smelled of horse and of a road that had been dry for a long time. Lyra held on longer than she had meant to, because she had spent the spring expecting to find her changed and could not at once find what had changed, and it seemed necessary to keep hold until she did.

She let go and looked at the man with the loaf.

“You can walk.”

“Very well,” said Na’ar.

“Your letter said—”

“Her letter.” He pointed at Riarhin with the bread. “I walked when she wrote it. She made a great deal of noise about the manner.”

“He fell over a saddle,” Riarhin said.

“It was on the ground.”

“That was why.”

Lyra laughed. The baker leaned out through the shutter and asked who meant to pay for the loaf, and Na’ar held it up and asked whether he meant this one, which had been very nearly eaten, and the baker said then they would find it fresh. Riarhin paid. While she was counting coins Lyra took the paper out of her sleeve, where she had put it for safety, and checked the direction. Riarhin’s eyes went to it. Lyra turned the written side inward without thinking and saw Riarhin see her do it.

“I must leave this first,” she said. “Then I can take you to Mara.”

“We can find her.”

“You can wait here for ten minutes.”

“Your work?”

“Yes.”

“What sort?”

“The sort I must deliver before the office closes.”

The old woman, having freed her sleeve, caught Lyra by the arm to complain about the nail. Lyra listened, because the woman plainly needed somebody to, and because it gave her a moment in which not to explain to Riarhin what kind of work involved a folded paper and a closing office and a man at the end of the tanners’ street who would be sitting at his desk now with his hands flat on it, waiting to learn what she had written about him.

“Ten minutes,” Riarhin said.

“Please don’t go north in them.”

It took her eleven. The officer read the paper in front of her, as she had known he would, and at the end he put it down and pressed it flat with both hands and said, “Thank you,” in a voice that made her want to leave quickly. She came back through the lane at a pace that was not quite running and found them where she had left them. Na’ar had eaten a quarter of the loaf. Riarhin was scratching the mare under its jaw and watching the lane.

They walked to Mara’s together.

Mara was not at home. She was in a lodging-house by the east gate, in the ground-floor room the station had rented for its stores, on her knees beside a chest, in a room so full of blankets that the air in it had gone woolly. The blankets belonged to a station that had closed three weeks before. The keeper’s son had promised to take them away and had then found work carrying stone, and his father had come instead with a handcart that would hold perhaps a quarter of them. The woman who owned the room wanted it empty by morning for her sister. The father wanted to be home before dark. Mara wanted everybody to stop explaining to her what they wanted.

She looked up over the chest, saw Riarhin, and said, “Oh.”

Then she stood too fast and struck her shin and swore, and crossed the room, and Lyra put the loaf down on the only clear surface and stepped back to let them have it.

She had known they would be glad. She had not known how it would feel to stand beside the door watching two women she loved hold each other, and discover that she was not jealous of either of them, and was a little surprised not to be.

“Where is the other key?” she asked the room at large, when it seemed they might go on standing there.

“Marten has it,” said the woman beside the chest.

“Where is Marten?”

“Carrying stone.”

They opened the blankets instead. Riarhin found that they were dry above and damp along the bottom. Na’ar went out to the cart and came back with cord and hung a line between two hooks. The old man in the chair with arms objected that his son had told him to fetch everything, and Mara told him he would fetch what would fit and his son could come after work for the rest.

By the time Elyas came in they were eating under a low tent of damp wool, and there was no room for his sword on the wall.

Lyra watched him see Riarhin.

It began in his face and made him younger for a moment, and then his mouth changed, and he came in carefully, moving a stool out of his way. He did not say her name. He put both hands on her shoulders.

“You took your time.”

“You’ve married.”

“That took less.”

“Elyas,” Mara said, and he turned to her, still holding Riarhin, and smiled. Lyra saw Riarhin watch him turn. There was something in her face then that Lyra could not read and did not try to; it went away almost at once, and Riarhin said she congratulated them, and Mara asked, “Both of us?” and Riarhin said she was considering.

The stew had caught on the bottom. Mara said so before anyone could taste it. Na’ar said he had eaten worse and named two occasions in enough detail that Elyas asked him to stop.

Lyra wanted to know about the sea.

“It moves,” said Na’ar.

“All of it?”

“All of it.”

Riarhin told them about a bird belonging to a man called Varek and a sailor who had tried to persuade Na’ar that one side of the ship moved less than the other. She left out the first night of sickness until Na’ar supplied it himself, accurately and with a complaint about the cook’s idea of broth. Mara leaned back laughing. A drop of water fell from the blanket above onto Elyas’s collar. He moved his chair. Mara put out a foot and steadied it without looking.

Lyra noticed that Riarhin noticed it.

The light in the window was going. Lyra looked at it twice. She had letters to read before morning and an early lesson at the University. The blankets would be dry soon. She could stay until then, and find another reason.

“Where next?” Elyas asked.

“North.”

“Ubral?”

Riarhin nodded. Mara glanced at Elyas, and Riarhin said, “Rocke. A place called Glenruan, if the man who told me was sober enough to remember it,” and Elyas asked whether he had been, and she said he had remembered what she owed him.

Lyra waited for her to say how long. She did not. She talked instead about the people she had travelled with in the south, and in the middle of it she said a name, Sada, and went on to the next thing a little too quickly, and came back to the name twice more before the end of the evening as though it were a stone in her shoe.

Lyra caught Mara’s eye across the table. Mara’s face said nothing whatever, which was how Lyra knew she had heard it too.

Before the bread was finished Riarhin took a folded paper out of her coat and laid it beside her plate. It had a seal on it that had been chipped in travelling. Elyas turned it to read it without touching the wax.

“A gift?”

“For the queen. I’m to put it in her keeping and send an acknowledgement south.”

“What is it?” Mara asked.

“A small thing.”

“That isn’t an answer.”

“It’s the answer that fits it.”

Lyra watched the paper go back into the coat. Riarhin’s hand stayed a moment on the place where it lay, pressed flat against her side, and then came away.

The queen’s messenger came before the blankets were dry.

He was a young man in good boots who looked relieved when Elyas came to the door, and he held out a folded instruction and said the queen would receive the bearer of the Taharkan gift after the morning petitions. Elyas read it and read further and handed it to Riarhin without a word.

Three evenings. At the queen’s request. A line about a companion. A line about rooms.

“I have a room,” Riarhin said.

“No,” said Mara. “You have blankets.”

The messenger smiled before he remembered not to.

Lyra walked home through streets where the shutters were coming down. Three evenings. She had seen men wait three months for half an hour of Veralyn’s attention and go home with less than they came for. She tried to be pleased and found that she was, on Riarhin’s behalf, and a little frightened on her own, and could not have said of what.

At her door she found that she still had half the loaf. Na’ar must have put it in her basket. She ate it standing at the window, looking down at the empty street, and went to bed without reading the letters.

She had one morning.

Riarhin had the queen’s audience on the first day, and the queen’s supper on the first evening, and the fitting of a borrowed gown in between, which she described afterwards with the bewilderment of a woman who had been measured by strangers for a garment she had not asked for. On the second day she had Mara. Lyra had the chancery. On the third morning, very early, before either of them had anything, Lyra knocked on the door of the rooms the queen had given Riarhin in the palace and found her already dressed and sitting on the bed with a pen in her hand, not writing.

“You said you’d be awake,” Lyra said.

“I was.”

“Come and be awake somewhere else.”

There was a garden behind the University. It had been planted by a man who had since died and was kept by another who grew everything, according to Master Ridow, in the wrong place. Ridow went there each morning before his first pupil to be offended by it. Lyra was his first pupil. She had been studying Kahfagian with him since the winter, three mornings a week, from a grammar in two volumes that he would not let her take away because it was his and old and, he said, the only copy north of the sea that had not been corrected by a fool.

They found him in front of a young tree, disputing it.

The gardener claimed it had come as a pear. Ridow maintained that the shape of its leaf disposed of that claim. Neither man had seen fruit. The gardener invited him to wait until autumn; Ridow answered that waiting did not alter a tree’s nature, and then remembered that he had guests.

“This is your traveller?”

“Riarhin,” Lyra said.

He bowed, caught his sleeve on the end of a stake, and freed it with considerable dignity.

The garden smelled of wet earth under sun-warmed leaves. A narrow channel carried water beside the walk. Riarhin crouched to look at something growing against its edge. Ridow began to explain it, found that she knew another name for it, and asked where she had learned that one. She told him. He asked what the people there did with it. She told him that too, and he took a little book from his sleeve and wrote it down, and for some time Lyra stood in the early sun and listened to a conversation in which nobody needed her to find anything.

Lyra had planned this in the night: the garden, the hour, Ridow being Ridow. She wanted to give Riarhin something of her own.

At the far wall there was a bench. They sat. Riarhin stretched out her legs. A girl carried cut stems past them and left the smell of bruised green things behind her.

“How long have you been learning?” Riarhin asked.

“Since the winter.”

“Why Kahfagian?”

Lyra had an answer about the southern trade, about petitions that came to the chancery in that language and went unread for weeks because nobody could manage more than the greeting. It was true. She said instead, “Because you went south.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“I thought if you wrote to me from somewhere they speak it, I should like to be able to read the name of the town.”

“I wrote from a boat.”

“I noticed.”

Riarhin laughed. It went on longer than the remark deserved, and at the end of it she put her head back against the wall and closed her eyes.

“Tell me about Sada,” Lyra said.

Riarhin did not open her eyes. “There’s very little to tell.”

“Then it won’t take long.”

“She’s occupied. She has people depending on her.”

“Does she know you’re trying to sound unconcerned?”

Now Riarhin opened her eyes. She looked at the sky over the wall, where a pair of swifts were cutting back and forth after something too small to see.

“She has complained of other things.”

Lyra laughed, and Riarhin, after a moment, did too.

They were quiet for a while. On the other side of the garden Ridow had got hold of the branch he wished the gardener to examine and was attempting to lift it into the light. The branch was winning.

“I could come north,” Lyra said.

Riarhin turned her head.

“For a visit. When you’ve found somewhere to send a letter that reaches you.”

“Yes.”

“You needn’t sound surprised.”

“I was imagining it.”

That pleased her enough that she had to look away. The chancery bell rang across the roofs, the first of the morning, and she stood up, because she was never late, and because if she sat there any longer she would ask how long Ubral was likely to take and receive the answer she already knew.

Ridow walked them to the gate. At the gate he took Lyra aside and said, in the tone he used for a verb she had conjugated wrongly, that her friend knew more about plants than any three members of the faculty and ought to be persuaded to stay.

“I know,” Lyra said.

“Then persuade her.”

“She has a journey.”

Ridow looked at Riarhin, who was waiting in the lane with her hands behind her back, reading the notices pinned to the University wall with an expression of polite disbelief.

“Everyone has a journey,” he said. “Some people come back from them.”

The good week began, as far as Lyra could afterwards tell, on the day of the audience.

It was not a thing anyone announced. A widow who had been petitioning since the winter for her husband’s back pay was told on the Tuesday that they would be paid on the Friday, and on the Friday they were. The chest from the western storehouse came in. The river house received its direction and began chalking names on its board. A dispute about a mill-race which had outlasted two officers was settled in an afternoon because the queen, passing through the room where it was being heard, stopped and asked the two men why they could not share the water on alternate days, and they looked at each other, and could not think why not.

People said it was the queen.

Lyra heard it in the chancery passages and in the street below her window and from the seamstress, who had it from a customer whose husband’s pay had arrived after four months. The queen had heard them. The queen had been told, and had done something. It was true. Lyra had also written the return that sent the direction to the river house, and Hamon had kept the chest very carefully indeed, and Pellam had signed four orders in one morning without complaining about any of them, which she had never seen him do. None of that was said in the street. She found she did not mind. It was pleasant, for once, to have done a thing and have it go right.

On the fourth evening, on her way out of the chancery, she passed the open door of an audience room. The petitions were over. A woman sat on a bench near the wall with a little boy asleep against her. The queen had bent to hear what she was saying, and as she bent something swung forward from her throat on a narrow black ribbon: a small case of old brass worked into leaves, with green glass set in it, catching the late light.

Lyra had never seen it. She knew it at once. It was exactly the size of the place where Riarhin’s hand had rested against her coat.

The woman began to cry. Veralyn put a hand on her arm and called a clerk. Lyra stood aside to let him pass, and then, because she did not want to be seen standing there, went on down the passage and out into the evening, where it had begun, very lightly, to rain.

Riarhin left two days later, at dawn, while the bread carts were coming in. Lyra was not there. She had meant to be. She had set out her shoes the night before. In the morning there was a letter under her door in Riarhin’s hand, which said that she had not wanted anyone to stand in the cold on her account, and that she would write from somewhere with a name Lyra could read.

Chapter Five — The Pear Tree

The branch struck Elyas across the face as he lifted Mara through the gate.

She made a sound against his shoulder. At first he thought he had hurt her. Then she began laughing.

“Put me down.”

“The threshold.”

“This is the garden.”

“I can see that.”

“So could the tree.”

He set her down. He had not carried her in the spring. They had come then in rain behind a cart with a chair that would not go through the door, and Berent had held two legs of it for most of an afternoon while Elyas tried the other door and found it narrower, and by the time the chair was in the kitchen neither of them had remembered that there was supposed to be a ceremony about arriving. He had meant to make up for it. The pear tree had other views. Mara removed a leaf from his hair and took the basket he had abandoned on the wall, and they went in separately, which spared the doorframe and disappointed Berent, who had been waiting inside with the expression of a man hoping to witness a second attempt.

The house smelled shut. They had been in Valora since midsummer. Nelle, the woman from the village who kept the house for them, had aired the rooms, but the kitchen chimney still gave back a cold, damp breath, and a bowl left on the upper shelf held the tiny bodies of flies. Where the storm had split the pear in the spring there was a pale scar the size of a plate, and beneath it on the path the stump of the half Mara had sawn away, still bleeding a little resin into the gravel.

“It’s put out more,” Elyas said, looking up.

“It has a great deal to say for itself,” said Mara. “None of it pears.”

She began opening things. Elyas carried the chest upstairs. Berent followed with a bundle which caught on the turn.

“Sideways,” Elyas said.

“I am sideways.”

“The bundle.”

They set it down on the landing and rested. From below came Mara’s voice asking who had thought to put soap beside onions.

“That was me,” Berent said.

“Your courage is needed downstairs.”

“No, sir. Yours is.”

In the morning a woman came to the gate with a child on her hip and asked whether the lady from the station was home.

Mara went out to her with her sleeves still pushed up from the kitchen. Elyas watched from the window. The woman talked for some time, with the child pulling at her ear. Mara put a hand on the child’s head, then on its neck beneath the jaw, and then took it from the woman altogether and carried it to the light by the pear tree and looked into its mouth while it screamed.

She came back in without it.

“Throat,” she said. “Not the bad kind. She’ll be back tomorrow and tell me it’s worse, and it won’t be.”

“How did she know you were home?”

“Nelle told her we were coming. Half the lane knows by now.” She washed her hands in the basin and dried them on her apron and looked at him. “You thought I would sit in the parlour.”

“We haven’t a parlour.”

“That was going to be my answer.”

By the end of the week there had been four more. A man with a burn on his forearm who had tried to lift a pot off the fire with his sleeve. A girl who wanted to know whether a certain tea would bring on her courses, and would not say why she wanted to know, and went away with a different tea and a talking-to that Elyas heard only the end of through the kitchen door. An old man whom nothing was wrong with except that his wife had died at Candlemas and he had nobody to tell about his knee. Mara told Elyas she did not intend to open a second station in their kitchen. He said he had not suggested it. She said she knew, and that was why she was telling him before he did.

His brother’s steward arrived before they had finished unpacking.

He came with two servants and a cart containing a bed, four chairs, a hanging, and a chest of linen. The bed belonged to a guest room Harald no longer intended to use. The steward said this twice while watching the men manoeuvre it through the gate.

“The duchess thought you might be glad of it.”

“We have a bed,” Mara said.

“A proper one.”

She looked towards the stairs, then back at him.

“Ours has been adequate.”

One of the servants coughed. Elyas put his hand on the gatepost and looked into the road until he was certain of his face.

“The linen is generous,” Mara continued. “Please thank her. The bed won’t turn on our landing. That man will hurt himself if he tries.”

The man had already been considering the door. He straightened gratefully.

“The carpenter could alter—”

“We need a table,” she said. “If there’s one to spare. And two sound chairs. Those two, perhaps.”

She pointed. The steward looked at Elyas.

“My wife knows what fits,” Elyas said.

Afterwards, while the cart was being turned, the steward asked him to walk a little way down the road. The duchess also wished it known that there was a woman in her household, a widow of good family, who had kept the linen at the ducal house for twenty years and was now without a place. She could come to the prince’s house. She would see to things.

“What things?”

The steward made a small gesture that included the house, the garden, the half-sawn stump, and by implication the woman in the kitchen who had just refused a bed.

“It is a great deal for one pair of hands, my lord. And the lady has her—” he searched for the word— “her callers.”

“Ask the lady,” Elyas said.

“The duchess thought it would come better from you.”

Elyas looked back at the gate. Mara was carrying the two chairs in, one in each hand, and the servant was following with the table and an expression of great uncertainty about who was now in charge of him.

“It would not,” he said.

The steward went back and asked. Elyas did not hear what Mara said. He saw the steward’s face while she said it, and then saw her put her hand briefly on the man’s arm, as she would have done with any patient who had received news he did not like, and send him back to the cart with, Elyas learned later, the name of a household in the next valley that needed exactly such a woman and could pay her.

“She will do very well there,” Mara said at supper. “They have eleven children and nobody who can count sheets.”

“The duchess meant it kindly.”

“The duchess meant I should have somebody in my kitchen who would tell her what I do in it.” She tore bread. “That may also be kind. I don’t want it.”

The steward had also asked him, on the road, to come to dinner. Harald wanted him. There were gentlemen from the south, and a matter concerning watch service, and it would help if his brother were present.

“Will there be a place for Mara?”

“A family matter, my lord.”

“Then you will require two places.”

The steward studied the gate behind them.

“I shall tell his grace.”

“Tell him we’ll come on Thursday.”

At dinner Harald had a sore tooth.

He had refused to have it drawn because the man recommended to him smelled of wine; he had refused another because the first spoke ill of him. A third was expected from Valora. Until then Harald ate on one side and spoke with the careful anger of a man obliged to be courteous while something inside his head struck him repeatedly with a hammer.

Mara was seated beside an elderly cousin who asked where she had been brought up. Elyas heard the answer, then the cousin’s surprised request for the name of a woman she had known there. By the fish they were discussing a marriage that had disappointed everyone except its participants. He left them to it.

Across the table a southern gentleman described a watch whose men failed to appear. He was Sire Gaubert of the Lesser Ford, whom Elyas had last seen in the war looking for a horse he had lost and blaming three different men for it. The levy was old, the villages poor, the road dangerous. Each statement came with a gesture of his knife.

“They say they have supplied their service.”

“Have they?” Elyas asked.

“They sent men to carry stone.”

“For the watchtower?”

“Yes.”

“Under whose direction?”

The knife stopped moving.

“Mine.”

Harald put down his cup. Elyas recognised the look: his brother had wanted help, but help that concluded before anybody important was embarrassed.

“The work counts,” Elyas said. “Find out for how long. Then ask for what remains.”

“The road doesn’t cease to require guarding.”

“No. But you cannot have the same man’s back under a stone and his eyes on a ford.”

Gaubert looked at him with the beginnings of a smile. “You were always good at saying that sort of thing, Elyas. In the war you said it about my horses.”

“I was right about your horses.”

“You were.” Gaubert laughed and raised his cup to him across the table, and two of the other southern men laughed with him, and one of them said something about the Scar and the night they had eaten the colonel’s dinner by mistake, and for a moment the end of the table was a camp.

Elyas saw Harald’s face.

His brother had watched the guests turn, all together, towards Elyas. Now he sat looking at the fire. Gaubert had called the duke your grace all evening. He had called Elyas by his name before the soup was cleared.

“We might ask the queen for men,” Harald said.

Everyone turned towards him. He touched his jaw, winced, and went on.

“She has companies to spare. She wants the approaches kept. Let her keep them.”

Gaubert began to explain why royal men would be unwelcome. Harald heard him with an increasing satisfaction which seemed almost to relieve the tooth. Elyas waited until the objections had become particular: billets, grazing, who could arrest a man, whose officer would answer for a stolen pig.

“Let me go down,” he said. “I’ll see what has been supplied. If there is a gap, we can ask for men on terms the villages have seen.”

Harald looked tired again.

“You always want to go and look.”

“Yes.”

“Then go.” He pushed his plate away. “And take Gaubert’s horse, if he still has it. He can walk behind you and explain what went wrong.”

The table laughed, gratefully. Gaubert laughed loudest. Elyas watched his brother receive it and saw that it had not helped, or not enough.

After dinner Harald asked him to walk in the gallery.

It was cold there. The windows had been glazed in their grandfather’s time with glass that made the courtyard below look as though it lay under water. Harald walked with one hand pressed against his cheek.

“You needn’t have told him he was wrong about the horses.”

“He brought them up.”

“He brought them up because you were there.” Harald stopped at a window. “They write to you, you know. Gaubert. Sornay. That fool Rousset, even, though he’d sooner eat his own hand than admit it. They write to me about their rights and to you about their troubles.”

“I don’t answer them as duke.”

“You don’t need to. They know who answers.”

Elyas stood beside him. Below, a groom was leading a horse across the court, and the old glass stretched and shrank them both as they went.

“What do you want me to do?”

“Go south. See what the watch needs. Write to Tylan for men and make the terms something those villages will swallow.” Harald took his hand away from his jaw and looked at it, as if the pain might have come off on his palm. “And when you write, write that you are acting for me.”

“I am acting for you.”

“Write it.”

“I will.”

Harald nodded. He seemed about to say something more, and Elyas waited, and in the end all his brother said was that the man from Valora had better be able to draw a tooth without breaking it, because he did not think he could endure another one of these dinners with it in.

On the way home Mara told him about the cousin’s niece, who had married a baker and was believed to be miserable because she had become stout.

“Is she?” Elyas asked.

“Her husband keeps sending sugared things. The family has decided he is consoling her.”

“Perhaps he is.”

“Perhaps she likes them.”

They rode for a while in the dusk. Behind them Berent was explaining to the groom why a horse which had stumbled once ought not to be judged by the incident. The groom thought the same might be said of the rider, but declined to apply it in this case.

“Harald asked me to go south,” Elyas said.

“I heard.”

“You were talking about bakers.”

“I can do two things.” She looked across at him. “Do you want to?”

He thought about it. The question deserved thinking about, and he found that the answer was simple, and that he was glad of it. The watch needed doing. Tylan would send men if the terms were good, and Elyas knew what good terms looked like in those villages because he had broken bread in half of them. The queen wanted the roads kept, and so did the villages, and so, once his tooth was drawn, would Harald. For once they all wanted the same thing, and he was the man who could walk between them.

“Yes,” he said.

“Then go.” She rode on a few paces. “How long?”

“Ten days. Perhaps a fortnight.”

“Write to tell me which, when you know.”

“I will.”

“And before you offer anybody our stable, remember that there’s no sound roof at the back.”

“I hadn’t thought of the stable.”

“I know.”

He reached across the space between their horses. She gave him her hand for three steps, then took it back to adjust her rein. Their gate came into view, and above it the pear branch, black against the last light.

That night he wrote to Tylan. He began At the duke’s request, and read it, and let it stand. He described the villages, the stone, the ford, the men who would be needed and the men who could be spared. He proposed terms: the company to be billeted in the tower and not in houses; grazing paid at the rate the villages already charged each other; any man of the company who took anything to answer before his own officer and the village reeve together. He added that he would stay with the company for its first month, if the queen agreed, so that the villages would have someone to complain to whom they already knew.

Mara read it over his shoulder before he sealed it.

“The pig,” she said.

“What pig?”

“The one somebody’s officer will have to answer for. Put in who pays for it.”

He put it in.

Part Two — The Useful Years

Chapter Six — Terms the Villages Had Seen

The tower at the Lesser Ford had been finished to the height of a man’s chest.

Above that there was scaffolding, and a heap of dressed stone under a sheet of sacking, and a boy of about sixteen sitting on the heap eating an onion as if it were an apple. When he saw riders on the road he put the onion in his shirt and picked up a stone and carried it three paces to the wall, where he set it down very carefully in the wrong place.

“Gilot,” said Sire Gaubert, dismounting. “That is not where it goes.”

“No, sire.”

“Then why did you put it there?”

The boy looked at the stone and then at Elyas, whom he had evidently decided was the more important of the two men and therefore the more dangerous.

“So you’d see I was working.”

Elyas liked him at once.

There were four villages on the approach to the ford and they had supplied, between them, eleven men to carry stone since the spring. Gaubert had counted this as their levy of service for the watch. The villages had counted it the same way. The difficulty was that stone-carrying had filled the days, and there had been nobody left for the nights, and in the nights men had come down from the Acasian side twice and taken sheep.

Elyas walked the ford with the reeves of all four villages. It took the whole of the first day, because each reeve had to show him where his own village’s ground ran down to the water and explain why the next village’s ground was not really theirs. By evening he knew where the ford could be crossed in summer, where it could be crossed after rain, and where it could be crossed only by a man who did not mind the possibility of drowning. He also knew which reeve disliked which, and why, and that one of them had a daughter married into Acasia and did not want this discussed.

“Will the queen’s men take our houses?” asked the reeve of the nearest village, a square woman called Ermengarde, whose hands had been scalded at some point and healed shiny across the backs.

“No. They’ll sleep in the tower.”

“There isn’t a tower.”

“There will be.”

“And until then?”

Elyas had not thought about until then. He looked at the chest-high wall and the scaffolding and the boy sitting on it, and said that until then they would sleep in tents on the common by the tower, and pay for the grazing, at the rate the villages already charged each other.

Ermengarde looked at him for a long moment.

“You know what we charge each other?”

“Sire Gaubert told me.”

“He told you what we charge him.” She sniffed. “Half that.”

He wrote it down. Gaubert, beside him, opened his mouth and closed it again.

The company came in the second week.

Captain Wace brought it himself. He had served with Elyas through the last year of the war, on the Scar and after, and had come out of it with his left arm stiff from the elbow and a new appointment in the queen’s regular foot that he described, when Elyas asked, as being paid on the day stated for doing the same thing as before. There were sixty men. Most were young. Their coats were cut to one pattern and several had already been altered at the cuffs by men who were particular about their cuffs.

They marched in at noon, and the four villages came out to look at them the way people look at weather coming.

Elyas had told Wace what he had promised. Wace had read it and said it was a great deal to promise on behalf of sixty men who had not yet been told. Then he stood them on the common and told them, in a voice that carried to the last house in Ermengarde’s village, what the terms were: tents on the common; grazing at the village rate, paid weekly; any man who took so much as an egg to answer for it before Wace and the village reeve together, and pay for the egg, and then pay for Wace’s time.
