A man in the second rank laughed. Wace looked at him until he stopped.

“It was the egg,” the man said.

“You think it’s funny.”

“A little, sir.”

“Good. When you take one, I’ll be sure to make it funny for the whole village.”

The villagers heard that too.

The first egg was taken on the fourth day. It was taken by a soldier called Tassin, who was nineteen and had been hungry since he was born, from a hen that had wandered onto the common and laid in the grass by the tents, so that there was a respectable argument about whose egg it had been. Elyas sat on a bench outside Ermengarde’s house while the argument was made. Wace sat beside him. Ermengarde sat on a stool opposite with the hen’s owner, a girl of perhaps nine, standing at her knee.

“It was in our grass,” Tassin said.

“It’s our hen,” said the girl.

“It was our grass.”

“It’s the common,” said Ermengarde. “It isn’t anyone’s grass. It’s everyone’s.”

“Then the egg—”

“Is the hen’s,” said Ermengarde, “and the hen is Aude’s.”

Tassin looked at Wace for help and received none. He paid for the egg, a copper, into the child’s hand. Then Wace made him carry water for Aude’s mother for a week, and the child followed him to the well every morning to make sure he did it properly. By the end of the week she was telling him how to hold the yoke. By the end of the month he was eating at Aude’s mother’s table on Sundays, and the other men of the company were making jokes about it that Wace pretended not to hear.

“That went well,” Elyas said.

“It went well because it was an egg,” said Wace. “Wait till it’s a girl.”

The pay came on the day.

It came in a small iron-bound chest in a cart with four riders, and the officer who brought it had a list and read every name on it aloud on the common, and each man stepped forward and was counted into his hand what the list said. Elyas watched the villagers watch this. He saw a woman at the edge of the common put her hand over her mouth, and he did not know why until Ermengarde said, beside him, that in the old duke’s time the watch had been paid in promises and flour, and the flour had been short.

“And the promises?”

“Long.”

After the pay there was drill. Wace had brought a sergeant who had been through the new instruction at Valora, and the sergeant took the company through movements Elyas had never seen: how to form across a road and open to let a cart through without losing the line; how to fall back to a set point and turn; how to send two men forward and bring them back by a signal. It was good work. It would have been useful on the Scar. Elyas said so.

“And here?” asked Wace.

Elyas looked at the ford. Below the tower the river ran brown and fast over the stones.

“Here they’ll need to know that when the water’s that colour you don’t send two men forward. You don’t send anyone. It’s rising and it will come up behind you.”

“The sergeant doesn’t know that.”

“No.”

“Neither did I, until you said it.”

Elyas spent the next three evenings walking the ford again, this time with Wace and the sergeant and Ermengarde, who knew more about the water than any of them and made the sergeant write it down in his little book in her own words. The sergeant objected to one of her words. She told him to write it anyway. He did.

Gilot enlisted at the end of the month.

He did it on the day of the second pay. Elyas saw him at the edge of the common, watching the men step forward to have their names read, and he saw the boy’s face, and knew. He went over.

“Your mother’s alone on the holding.”

“She has my brother.”

“Your brother is eleven.”

“He’s strong.”

“Is it harvest in six weeks?”

Gilot looked at the ground. He had the onion again, or another one; it made a round shape in his shirt.

“It’s pay on the day, sir. Every month. I carry Sire Gaubert’s stone for nothing and it counts as my mother’s service, and her roof’s been leaking over the bed since before my father died, and Sire Gaubert says he’ll mend it when the tower’s done, and the tower won’t be done for two years.” He took a breath. “And they feed you.”

Elyas had nothing to say to that which was not a reason for the boy to wait six weeks. He said it anyway. Gilot listened politely and then went and stood in front of Wace, and Wace asked him his age, and he said seventeen, and Wace looked at him the way he had looked at the man who laughed at the egg and wrote down seventeen.

Gilot’s mother came to the common that evening. She was a small woman called Hodierne, with a face like a closed door, and she walked past the tents and past Wace and past her son, who stood up when he saw her and then did not know what to do, and stopped in front of Elyas.

“You brought them.”

“Sire Gaubert asked for them.”

“You brought them.”

He had. He did not deny it.

“Who cuts my barley?”

“I’ll find someone.”

“Who? You?” She looked him up and down with an expression that said she had seen princes cut barley before and did not think much of it. “You’ll be gone in a month.”

“I’ll find someone before I go.”

She turned and looked at her son. He was standing with his hands at his sides, and the onion had fallen out of his shirt and was lying in the grass at his feet, and he had not noticed.

“Pick that up,” she said. “Waste.”

He picked it up. She went home.

Elyas found her two men from Ermengarde’s village for the harvest and paid them himself. Hodierne said neither knew which end of her field flooded or where the stones lay. She let them cut the barley anyway.

He went home at the end of the month, as he had said he would. Harald had had his tooth drawn and was in a better temper, and received his report at table, and read the terms Elyas had made with the villages, and did not ask why the grazing was half what Gaubert had said. The queen had written approving them. Tylan had written separately, asking for the sergeant’s notebook to be copied and sent to Valora with Ermengarde’s words left in.

Mara asked him whether the villages were content.

He thought about it.

“They were paid on the day,” he said. “I think they’re waiting to see if it happens again.”

It happened again.

It happened the next month and the one after, and in the spring Elyas rode down to the Lesser Ford to see how the tower had got on. It had got on. It stood to the height of three men with a roof of new timber, and the company had moved into it out of the tents, and there was a vegetable patch behind it which Tassin had dug under the direction of Aude’s mother. The sheep had not been taken since midsummer.

He went up to Hodierne’s holding on his way home, without quite deciding to.

She was on the roof.

She was sitting astride the ridge with a bundle of straw in her lap, and there was a man beside her with a leggett and a mouthful of spars whom Elyas did not know, and between them they were laying new thatch over the end of the house where the bed was. She saw him at the gate and did not come down.

“It’s being mended,” he called up.

“It is.”

“Sire Gaubert?”

She laughed, a short sound, and pointed the straw at the thatcher.

“Gilot’s pay. Came through the river house on the day stated, with his mark on it and a clerk’s writing saying what it was for. Twice now.” She shifted her weight on the ridge. “Gaubert can mend his own roof.”

Elyas stood at the gate and found he was smiling.

“And the barley?”

“Rotted in the low corner. Your two men didn’t know about the low corner.” She laid the straw where the thatcher showed her. “I told them. They didn’t listen. Gilot would have listened.”

“I’m sorry.”

“I didn’t ask for sorry.” She looked down at him over the eaves. “He writes. He can’t spell. He says the food is very good and he has learned to swim. He was never afraid of the water; I don’t know why they had to teach him.”

“They teach everyone.”

“Then they waste their time on some.” She turned back to the roof. “Go home, my lord. You’re standing in my light.”

He went home. On the way he passed the tower, and Wace came out to meet him, and they stood together at the edge of the ford and watched the water, which was clear that day, and low, and running quietly over the stones.

Chapter Seven — A Place at the Table

It was nearly a year before Lyra saw Perrine’s handwriting again, and then it was on a letter carried into the chancery by her son.

The year had been full. The receiving houses had been given officers who answered letters, and then officers who answered them within a month, and then, after a quarrel with the treasury that Pellam won by the simple method of outlasting everybody in it, a rule that no direction for payment should leave Valora without the name of the man who was to make it. Lyra wrote the rule. She was not credited with it, and did not expect to be; she was given instead the task of seeing it kept, which meant a desk of her own in the room above Pellam’s, a boy to carry for her, and a number of officers across three duchies who knew her name and had opinions about it.

She had also acquired, without meaning to, a second kind of work. People began to come to her who were not sent. A widow whose husband’s back pay had been paid to his brother. A carter who had carried stores for the army in the last winter and could not find anyone who would admit to having hired him. A woman from the tanners’ street whose wet copy had been read, in the end, and who came back with a pot of honey and a cousin who had a problem with a lease. Lyra did what she could for them in the chancery’s time when their troubles were the chancery’s, and in her own when they were not. Some of them paid her. Most of them brought things. The seamstress below said that if it went on she would have to charge Lyra for the stair.

The examination room had once been a dining hall. Grease showed through the fresh limewash above the old hearth, and the new benches had been made too high for anybody who wished to put both feet flat on the floor. Lyra sat at the end of one, reading the recommendation a young man had brought her.

There were six lines about his father’s service and none about his own.

“Can you read this?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“Then you know the difficulty.”

“My father was told it would do.”

His name was Lucan. He had Matthis’s long hands and Perrine’s way of lifting his chin when he expected a refusal. She had not recognised him two days before, when he stood up from the bench in the chancery passage and asked the clerk for the lady who had written down his father’s pay. She had recognised him only after he gave his mother’s name. Perrine’s letter was in a different pocket, wrapped to keep it clean. He had not offered that one until Lyra asked.

It said the boy could reckon, knew horses, and had a good head when he chose to use it. The last phrase had been added below the signature.

“Did you choose to come?”

“Yes.”

“What do you want to do?”

“Learn.”

“What?”

He looked round the hall. Boys with better coats waited beside men who knew when to bow. At the far end an instructor was setting wooden blocks on a table. Beyond the open windows came the clash of practice weapons and a voice telling someone that his opponent would not pause to admire how far he had drawn his arm back.

“How to do it properly,” Lucan said.

The rule required a recommendation from an officer of recognised standing. Matthis had served under a captain who could attest to Matthis; he could say little about the son. Lucan had ridden north at his own expense because the recruiting clerk had told him the new school would test men who came forward.

The instructor could see the mistake. He could not admit every hopeful traveller on the strength of it.

“Then test him before you admit him,” Lyra said.

“He must first be entered.”

“As a candidate.”

“Which requires—”

“I have read it.”

A door opened behind them. Tylan came in with a man whose fingers were stained blue from marking plans. They stopped beside the blocks. The instructor rose. Lyra remained seated a moment too long because she was watching Lucan try to make himself smaller.

Tylan heard the explanation. He took the recommendation, read it, and asked Lucan how many horses he had cared for.

“Six at home. More at the fair.”

“Sound ones?”

“Mostly.”

“What do you do with the others?”

“That depends who’s selling.”

The blue-fingered man laughed. Lucan flushed.

“I meant—”

“I know what you meant,” Tylan said. “So does everybody here. You will sit the examination.”

The instructor opened his mouth.

“Provisionally. If he cannot read the question or add what it requires, send him home with his fare. We invited applications. We can afford to discover whether we have received one.”

He gave Lyra the letter back.

“Will you stand for his identity?”

“Yes.”

“For his ability?”

“No.”

“Good.”

Lucan failed the first exercise.

He had counted every animal and allowed too little water. The instructor pointed to the empty space where the reserve should have been, then made him carry two full buckets across the yard. He returned with wet boots and a different understanding of how much a bucket contained.

Lyra waited under an arcade with the other applicants’ relations. A father beside her kept getting up to look through the door. A young woman told him that if his son could not answer the question without being watched, he would scarcely improve with an audience. The father sat down. Five minutes later she got up herself.

At noon bread was brought out. Lyra shared hers with the young woman, whose brother had refused breakfast from nerves. They talked about lodgings, then clothes, then the ridiculous price asked for a ribbon in a shop near the palace. The woman had bought it anyway. She showed Lyra the edge beneath her sleeve.

“It is a good colour,” Lyra admitted.

“For that price it ought to flatter my grandchildren.”

Her name was Isaut. She kept a glover’s shop with her mother two streets from the palace, and she had closed it for the day, which she said had cost her more than the ribbon. Her brother wanted to be an officer because their father had been one, briefly, for four days at the end of the war, before a horse fell on him. The brother had been nine then. He had been talking about it ever since.

“Does he know anything about horses?”

“He knows they fall on people.” Isaut looked at the door. “That’s something, I suppose.”

Lyra laughed, and found that she was enjoying herself. She had not sat in the sun and talked to a stranger about ribbons since before the war. Isaut asked what she did, and Lyra told her she was a clerk, and Isaut said that was a pity, because she had hoped Lyra was somebody’s mistress and could tell her whether the palace was as dull as everybody said.

“Duller.”

“Oh, good. I was afraid I was missing something.”

Lucan emerged last. His face gave nothing away until he saw Lyra still there.

“They’ll take me for the term.”

She offered him the remaining bread. He ate it too quickly to answer her next question, and had to begin again after swallowing.

“The horses helped.”

“And the water?”

“I won’t forget the water.”

She met Tylan on the way out. He was standing beside a boy whose recommendation bore an impressive seal. The boy had passed. His father wanted him excused the common sleeping room.

“He has never shared with strangers.”

“He will become acquainted with them.”

“He is delicate.”

“Then the physician can examine him.”

The father lowered his voice. Tylan did not lower his.

“If he is unfit, we will not take him. If he is fit, he will sleep with his fellows.”

The father went red, then white. He looked round for someone to be angry with who was not the Marshal, and found Lyra, and Lucan standing beside her with his bread.

“I see how it is,” he said. “Carters’ sons sleep with carters’ sons, and if a gentleman’s son objects he’s told to become acquainted.”

“My father was a soldier,” said Lucan.

“I didn’t address you.”

“No, sir. You addressed my father.”

Lyra put her hand on Lucan’s arm. The father looked at the hand and then at her.

“And you, madam. Who sent you? Whose son is this, that the chancery comes with him?”

“His mother’s,” Lyra said. “She wrote to me because I once wrote down his father’s pay. That’s all I’m here for.”

“That’s a great deal, it seems, to get a boy in.”

“He isn’t in. He’s been allowed to try.”

The father looked at Tylan, who had not moved and had not stopped listening, and at the boy with the impressive seal, who was staring at his own shoes as if he wished he were wearing somebody else’s. Then he took his son by the shoulder and walked him away down the arcade. At the end of it the boy looked back once, at Lucan. Lyra could not tell what was in the look. She thought it might have been envy.

“He’ll sleep in the common room,” Tylan said, to nobody in particular. “His father will write to me about it twice, and to the queen once, and by midwinter the boy will be the best of that intake at the lance and will never forgive any of us for it.”

“How do you know?”

“Because he comes every intake,” said Tylan. “Sometimes his father writes three times.”

Lyra carried Perrine’s letter home and wrote her own beneath it. Lucan would need another shirt, she said, and something to mend his stockings with. He had been accepted for instruction. Nothing further was promised yet.

She let the page dry while she washed. Then she added that he had looked happy when he came out.

Isaut’s brother was taken too. Isaut came to the chancery a week later with a pair of gloves for Lyra, fawn-coloured, with a little green stitching at the wrist, and would not hear of payment.

“You gave me half your bread,” she said. “And you told me the palace was dull. I’ve been telling everyone. It’s made me very popular.”

After that Lyra went to the glover’s shop on the evenings when she could not bear her own room. Isaut’s mother fed her and asked no questions about her work, which was restful, and asked a great many questions about her clothes, which was not. Isaut had opinions about everyone who came into the shop and delivered them the moment the door closed. It was the first place in Valora where nobody wanted anything from her except her company, and she did not mention it to anyone at all.

In the spring Tylan sent for her.

He did not send for her to the chancery or to the palace. He sent a boy with a note to the academy, where she was not, and the boy found her by asking at the glover’s shop, which she disliked. The note said that a candidate for the next intake had brought a recommendation from a captain of good standing, and that the captain’s company had been at the Scar, and the candidate’s name did not appear on any return from the Scar that the Marshal’s clerks could find. Would she look?

She looked. It took two days. The candidate had been at the Scar. He had been there as a servant to the captain’s brother, carrying his baggage and his wine, and the captain had written in all honesty that the young man had served at the Scar and conducted himself well there.

She brought Tylan the returns. He read them in the practice yard, standing, while two instructors shouted at a line of young men who were trying to form across a road.

“So it’s true,” he said.

“Every word.”

“And means nothing.”

“It means he carried wine well under difficult conditions.”

Tylan almost smiled. He handed the returns back.

“I’ll see him. Examine him like the rest. If he’s good he can come in on that and not on his captain’s brother’s wine.” He watched the line reform, badly, and the instructor go down it with his stick, correcting feet. “And I’ll write to the captain. Every recommendation he sends for the next year comes to me first.”

“He’ll be angry.”

“He’ll be more careful.” Tylan turned to her. “Next time, can you tell me before I have to ask?”

“How would I know?”

“You found this in two days.”

“You told me where to look.”

He considered that.

“Then I’ll tell you where to look,” he said, “and you’ll tell me what’s there. Will that do?”

It would do. When she next knocked at the academy, he received her. Lucan finished his term and was entered for further instruction. On the day the list was posted he came to the chancery in his new coat and stood in her doorway until she looked up.

Chapter Eight — Above the Ford

The raiders came over in the grey before dawn and took forty head of cattle from the common below Ermengarde’s village.

Elyas was at the tower. He had come down the week before to see the second autumn’s pay made and stayed because Wace had a fever and would not admit it. The horn woke him. By the time he reached the roof Wace was already there in his shirt, shouting down to the sergeant, and below them in the half-light the company was coming out of the tower door and forming across the road in two ranks, exactly as it had been taught, while the villagers ran past them in the other direction with children and bundles and one old man carrying a hen under each arm.

“Let them through,” Wace was shouting. “Open. Open, damn you, let them through and close behind.”

The line opened. The villagers went through. It closed. Elyas watched it from above and felt something in his chest that he had not felt since the war, and did not altogether like.

“They’ve got the cattle,” he said. “Not the village.”

“The cattle are the village,” said Wace, “in about a month.”

Elyas took the horsemen.

There were fourteen: his own household, four of Gaubert’s riders who had been billeted at the ford for the harvest, Berent, and a young man called Renier who was Gaubert’s nephew and had come down to learn the watch because his uncle said he needed to learn something. Renier rode his father’s horse, a tall bay with a white stocking that was worth more than anything else at the ford, and he rode it as though he knew that. Wace kept the foot at the crossing. The raiders had gone south, driving the cattle up the valley towards the Acasian track.

They followed the trail through the morning. Twice they came close enough to see the rearmost cattle and the men behind them, and twice the raiders turned off the valley floor into ground that the horses did not like. By noon they had recovered eleven head that had strayed or been abandoned, and sent them back with one of Gaubert’s men. By early afternoon they had reached a ridge above the Acasian side of the valley, and below it, a mile off, the rest of the herd was being driven through a narrow place between two hills, and there were perhaps a dozen men with it, and there was light enough left to think they could take it back.

Elyas had meant to stop at the ridge.

He knew he had meant to. He remembered, afterwards, the exact moment of meaning it: the horse blowing under him, the herd below, the thought that the ridge was far enough and the ford was behind them and the water had been brown that morning and he did not like the colour. He remembered deciding to go on, too. He could not afterwards find the place where the one had turned into the other.

“Down,” he said. “Quickly. We’ll take them in the narrows.”

They did not take them in the narrows.

The raiders had seen them come over the ridge and did not wait. They left the cattle, which scattered, and turned, and there were more of them than a dozen, because others came out of the trees on the far hillside where they had been waiting. The first arrows came over as Elyas’s men reached the valley floor. Nobody fell. They went back up the slope at a pace that the horses did not like either, and at the top Elyas turned to count and found them all, and began to breathe.

The man ahead of him looked back twice before he fell.

The first time, Elyas thought he was checking who followed. The second, he saw the blood soaking down the arm. Then the bay took a bad step and the rider went sideways into the ditch, disappearing so completely that the next two men rode past him.

“Hold!” Elyas shouted.

His own horse fought the rein. The light had gone flat. Rain moved through the trees in long grey strips.

Berent dismounted beside the ditch. Elyas ordered two men back to watch the bend, then slid down to the fallen rider. Renier was conscious. He kept trying to reach his horse.

“Leave him,” Elyas said.

“My father’s.”

“We’ll fetch him.”

The bay stood twenty paces off, trembling. Its bridle hung caught in a thorn bush. Berent went to it while Elyas and another man lifted the rider.

The wound was high in the arm. Blood soaked the cloth as fast as they bound it. Elyas tightened the binding, and Renier swore at him in a sudden fierce voice which reassured him more than the colour of his face.

“Better,” Elyas said.

“No.”

“Louder, at least.”

They put him across his father’s saddle and turned back.

The ford had become a moving brown width full of things that did not belong in water.

A branch went past turning slowly, one end lifting like an arm. Beyond it the far bank seemed close enough for a good horse to reach. Elyas watched a stone vanish where there had been a narrow line of white water that morning. He called the men back from the approach.

“We crossed this morning,” said one of Gaubert’s riders, a broad young man called Joscelin.

“Yes.”

“It can’t have risen so much.”

“It has.”

He could see the tower on the far side, a mile downstream, with a light in its upper window. He could see the line of the company, very small, still across the road. They would be watching the water too. The sergeant had it written down in his book, in Ermengarde’s words: when it runs that colour, it runs for two days.

Renier had begun shivering. Berent stood beside him, holding the bay. Upstream the banks grew close beneath trees, and Berent was looking there.

“Deeper,” he said.

“You’ve tried it?”

“I ate apples there.”

Joscelin stared at him.

“Last autumn,” Berent said. “When I was carrying for Captain Wace. There’s an orchard above the ford, on this side. A track behind it. It comes round to the old stone crossing, higher up, where the river’s narrow.”

Elyas looked at him. “How far?”

“An hour. Less if we lead them through the first part.”

“Why lead?”

“A wall. There’s a gap in it.”

Behind them a horn sounded, faint through the rain. The two men watching the road rode in together. There were riders coming. They could not say how many.

“Show us,” Elyas said.

The gap was too narrow for a saddled horse.

Berent looked at it with the affronted expression of a man who had been betrayed by a thing he remembered. It had been wide enough for a boy on foot with his arms full of apples. It was not wide enough for a troop of horsemen and a wounded man in the rain. He began taking stones from the top. Elyas sent the sound men to help, kept two watching the lane, and held Renier against his shoulder while the bay waited.

The orchard’s owner came out of the trees carrying a billhook.

“What are you doing?”

“Getting through,” Berent said.

“You asked leave to take apples.”

“And you said yes.”

“I didn’t say yes to this.”

Elyas turned far enough for the man to see Renier. The billhook lowered.

“The gate is at the top.”

“How far?”

“Too far for him.” The man leaned the tool against the wall. “Move that one. It’s holding the others.”

His name, Elyas learned a day later, was Cormin. He had lived above the ford for thirty years and had seen raiders come over twice and the water come up more times than he could count, and he did not panic. He moved stones with the soldiers, telling them which ones would bring the rest down, and when the gap was nearly wide enough he took the bay’s bridle from Berent without asking and said that he would take the wounded boy up through the trees himself, because he knew the way in the dark and they did not.

“Go with him,” Elyas told two of the men. “Berent, get them through. I’ll hold the lane.”

He went back down with the watchers. Through the rain he could see the bend in the road, and nothing on it yet, and then something.

He did not see what happened at the gap. He learned it afterwards, in pieces, and put the pieces together in the order they must have gone, and was never sure he had them right.

The first horses went through. The fourth did not. It was Joscelin’s, a big grey mare, and she balked at the wet stones and then went at them too hard, and her near fore went down between two of them and did not come up. Elyas heard the sound from the lane. He did not know then what it was.

She went down in the gap. She lay across it with her leg broken below the knee, trying to rise, and there was no way past her for anything larger than a man. There were eight horsemen still on the near side, and a lane behind them, and riders on the lane.

Joscelin would not leave her.

He had knelt at her head and got his arms round her neck and was talking to her, and when Berent told him to get up he did not hear, and when Berent took him by the collar he fought. Berent was younger than he was and a head shorter. He did not try again. He took his knife out of his belt and went round Joscelin and knelt on the mare’s neck where she lay in the gap and killed her. Then he and two of the others put ropes on her and dragged her clear, with Joscelin still on his knees in the wet grass shouting at him, and they got the rest of the horses through.

By the time Elyas came up from the lane the gap was open and there was blood all down the stones. Berent was standing beside it with the reins of the last horse in his hand. He had not gone through.

Down the slope the two watchers fired, then began retreating between the trees. A rider appeared below them and checked at the broken wall. Elyas raised his sword. The other man looked at what he would have to follow him over, at the mare lying on the grass with her throat open, looked past Elyas into the trees, and wheeled away.

They did not wait to discover whether he had gone for help.

At the stone crossing Berent slipped on the wet approach. He went down on one knee with both reins in his hand. The horses crowded him. He stayed there until the nearer one stepped back, then rose without letting either go.

Elyas watched him reach the other side. Only when he was across himself did his hands begin to shake.

Renier lived until morning.

He died while Elyas was outside speaking to the surgeon Wace had sent up from the tower, and Elyas hated the few steps between the door and the bed. Somebody had put a dry cloth beneath the young man’s head. His father arrived before noon, wearing a coat fastened wrongly at the throat. Elyas gave him the bay and told him where his son had fallen. The father listened, asked which arm, and then asked to be left alone.

Berent waited in the passage. His knee had swollen.

“You should have it seen,” Elyas said.

“I have.”

“Again.”

They sat on a bench. On the opposite wall hung a painted saint whose face had been rubbed pale by years of smoke. Somebody had put fresh flowers below it. Water from the stems had marked the plaster.

“We went too far,” Elyas said.

Berent watched a drop move down the wall.

“Yes.”

“I should have stopped at the ridge.”

“Yes, sir.”

The answer hurt. He was glad of it.

After a while Berent said, “Joscelin won’t speak to me.”

“No.”

“I didn’t ask him.”

“No.” Elyas looked at the saint. “Would you do it again?”

Berent was silent for so long that Elyas thought he would not answer. When he did, his voice was steady and very tired.

“I’d do it quicker.”

Later Elyas wrote the report. He put the decision to pursue beyond the ridge beside his own name, and the route through the orchard beside Berent’s, and the mare beside Berent’s too, because Berent asked him to and because it was true. When he came to the orchard owner he had to send a man to ask, because none of them had remembered to learn it.

Wace read the report at the tower with his fever still on him and a blanket round his shoulders.

“The line held,” he said. “Did you put that in?”

“I put it in.”

“They opened and let the village through and closed again, in the dark, with arrows coming, and not one of them ran. That’s the drill.” He coughed. “That’s Valora. They’ve never held a line in their lives before this year.”

“And the water?”

Wace looked out of the window at the ford, still brown, still rising.

“That’s Ermengarde,” he said. “And your boy with the apples.”

Elyas sent a copy of the report to Tylan, with a note at the bottom asking that the sergeant’s book be read to every intake, and that someone find out whether it would be possible, at the academy, to make the young men walk a ford with the people who lived beside it before they were sent to hold one.

Tylan wrote back within the fortnight. It would be possible. It would be done. He added, in his own hand, that he was sorry about Gaubert’s nephew, and that he had known the father slightly, and that he did not think the ridge had been an easy place to stop.

Elyas read that line several times.

Cormin wanted his wall rebuilt before the sheep were brought down.

Elyas went back with men and stone. Berent came despite the knee. They worked until Cormin stopped correcting them, which took most of the afternoon. Joscelin did not come. Near the end of the day he appeared at the top of the orchard on foot and stood under the trees watching Berent set the last stones in the gap, and then went away without speaking.

A month later he sent Berent a bridle. It had been his mare’s. There was no note with it. Berent hung it on the wall of the stable and did not use it, and when Mara asked him why he kept a bridle for a horse he did not have, he told her it was being kept for somebody.

Chapter Nine — The Seat by the Window

The letter had come to Lyra’s desk in the chancery with a direction on the outside that had been written three times, each time a little larger, as though the writer had not trusted the first two to be read.

It was from a woman called Hawise, in a village on the western edge of the Scar country, and it was about her brother. Lyra found him in the returns before she had finished the letter. Tibaut, son of Gace, of that village, entered with his company at the muster before the last battle on the Scar. Entered as present at the start of the day. Not entered at the end of it, in any list: not among the dead, not among the wounded, not among the men brought in afterwards from the field or the river or the farms where some had crawled to be found days later. There was a small mark beside his name in the company’s return, made by a clerk who had not known what else to write, and the mark meant not accounted for.

There were other such marks in the Scar returns. Seven remained in Elyas’s own account. Lyra had gone through them that winter, checking each against the later lists from the surgeons and the villages. Tibaut’s had remained.

Elyas was in Valora that month for the spring muster. She sent him a note. Jaennis, hearing from somebody that the prince was looking for a quiet room to go through Scar returns with a chancery clerk, sent a note of her own offering one.

Jaennis had put them in a room with a view of the garden and no convenient way to leave unnoticed.

Lyra arrived first. There was a plate of little cakes beside the ink, and a servant who knew Elyas was expected, and a fire which would have been too warm had she not opened the window. The papers she had brought concerned men still missing after the Scar. She moved the cakes before putting them down.

Elyas came carrying a letter from Ouen. He had been smiling at something in it. The smile went as he saw the returns.

“Another?”

“A sister. She wants to marry.”

“Which man?”

She showed him. He read the name twice, then sat.

“I remember where he stood.”

“Can you say he died there?”

“No.”

“That’s the difficulty.”

The sister’s father had died over the winter. Her brother’s claim to their little holding remained unsettled. The man she meant to marry would take the lease if it could be made to her. Until then she could keep working the land under a temporary arrangement, paying a charge each time it was renewed. Nobody disputed that she was the person doing the work. Nobody would declare her brother dead.

“He isn’t coming back,” Elyas said.

Lyra looked at the return.

“Will you sign that?”

His hands lay on either side of the sheet. Through the window came the sound of a rake dragged over gravel.

“I can sign that I do not expect him.”

“That is already here.”

“Then what can I add?”

“Nothing about his death. Something about her tenancy.”

He read the request again. The sister had asked for the house as well as the ground. She had written its particulars herself, in letters that grew larger towards the end as though she feared running out of courage before paper.

“Let her have it,” he said.

“And if he returns?”

“He has his sister.”

“She may have a husband and children by then. The house may have a new roof they paid for. He may arrive with nothing.”

“So we leave her in this?”

“No. We ask for an agreement that tells them both what happens.”

He pushed his chair back. She had heard that scrape in meetings before, when men thought she had made a simple matter difficult for the pleasure of holding it. Hearing it from Elyas hurt more than she expected.

“You think I want another form.”

“I think she has waited long enough.”

“So do I.”

“Then write what will get her the house.”

She did. She wrote that the sister should hold the property on secure terms without being required to swear her brother dead; that his return should give him a right to maintenance and a hearing, rather than an immediate power to put her out; that improvements made meanwhile should be heard in that settlement. Then she put the pen down.

“His lord must agree. So must she. It may not suit the man she means to marry.”

“Why wouldn’t it?”

“Because he wants a house.”

Elyas looked towards the garden.

“I’ll write to the lord.”

“Ask what power he has to grant it. Don’t promise for him.”

Elyas looked back. After a moment he pulled the chair in.

“Give me another sheet.”

They worked until the cakes had become lunch. Jaennis came in when Elyas was making his third attempt to explain the arrangement without insulting its recipient.

“Have you eaten?”

“Your cakes,” Lyra said.

“Those were for someone else.”

Elyas looked at the empty plate. Jaennis let him consider it before she smiled.

“Who is giving you trouble?”

“A man who hasn’t answered yet.”

“You have always been ambitious.”

She sat in the chair nearest the window. Outside, Vaelrie was talking to a young woman who had taken a ribbon out of her hair to demonstrate something with it. Jaennis watched them while Elyas explained the request.

“I know his aunt,” she said. Then, turning to Lyra: “How is Oudine’s knee?”

Lyra looked at her. Oudine was the seamstress. Lyra did not think she had ever said her name aloud in the palace.

“Better,” she said. “Since the weather turned.”

“She made Vaelrie’s riding hem last winter. She told Vaelrie she lets her upper room to a woman from the chancery who reads until the candle’s gone and never complains about the noise of the treadle.” Jaennis smiled. “I’ve been meaning to tell you how much that sounded like you.”

It was an ordinary explanation. Lyra accepted it, and found a moment later that she was still holding it, turning it over, the way she turned Gyllan’s whetstone when a sentence would not come. Valora was small at that height, and women who made riding hems talked to the women who wore them. She put it down.

“We need his agreement,” Elyas was saying. “The lord’s. Not his aunt’s.”

“His aunt can put the letter where he reads it,” said Jaennis.

Lyra glanced at Elyas. He was already reaching for the unfinished page.

“Not that one,” she said. “Finish it first.”

Jaennis’s smile widened.

The subject of Veralyn’s marriage came to them later that afternoon, through a door left open while they waited to be admitted.

The speaker was Lord Ferrand, who kept the treasury and had spent the winter keeping it from running dry. Lyra knew his voice from the other side of Pellam’s door, where it was usually explaining that something could not be paid for. It was explaining now that the southern court had offered three thousand measures of grain on credit for the coming winter, to be delivered to the northern garrisons before the passes closed, and that the offer had been made, in so many words, in expectation of a closer kinship between the two houses. If the queen married the Marshal, the prince whose brother had been proposed would not take it as an insult. He would take it as an answer. The grain would be reconsidered. The northern garrisons would eat short in the month after midwinter, when there was nothing else to buy.

“I have the figures, your grace. I can show you the garrison returns.”

“I have read the garrison returns.”

“Then you know what February costs.”

There was a pause. Lyra heard paper move.

“It shall matter not,” Veralyn said.

“Your grace, I beg you to consider what may follow.”

“It shall matter not.”

“Your grace, with respect, it will matter a great deal to a man in a garrison in February—”

“Then we shall buy the grain,” said the queen, “and he shall eat it, and it shall matter not who I married.”

Berent stood beside Elyas with the clean coat Mara had made him wear. His eyes remained carefully on the opposite wall. Lyra could see that he was listening to every word.

When they entered, the queen was removing a ring which had caught in a thread at her cuff. She gave it to Tylan, who freed the thread and returned the ring without interrupting his account of a muster. The southern envoy watched them as though they had already signed something he had hoped to prevent.

Ferrand bowed himself out past them a little later with his papers under his arm. He was a grey, careful man who had lost two sons in the war, and he did not look at the Marshal as he went. He looked at Lyra, whom he knew from the chancery, and gave her the smallest nod, and she saw that he was afraid. He had the summer to find three thousand measures of grain somewhere else.

Afterwards they walked down through the lower court together, she and Elyas and Berent, and the court was different.

It took her a little time to see how. It was the same court: the same passages, the same petitioners on the same benches, a man arguing with a guard about a dog. But people were standing in different places. The men who had spent the spring near the southern envoy’s rooms were standing near the Marshal’s. A woman Lyra knew slightly, who had been wearing the southern fashion in sleeves since Candlemas, was wearing her old ones. Two clerks from the treasury who had been going everywhere together for a month were walking twenty paces apart.

“She’s decided,” Lyra said.

“She decided years ago,” said Elyas. “She’s told him.”

“You knew?”

“I grew up with them.” He looked pleased, and a little sad, in a way she did not understand. “He’ll be good for her. He’ll say no to her. Hardly anyone does.”

“Ferrand did.”

“Ferrand said February. That isn’t no. That’s a price.” He walked on a few paces. “And she’ll pay it, and she’ll be right to, because the spring muster is paid from the Crown now and not from somebody’s dowry, and the northern garrisons will know that their pay doesn’t depend on whether a prince in the south is pleased with his brother’s marriage. Tylan told me in the winter. Every man at the Lesser Ford was paid on the day because the Crown paid it. Not a treaty. Not a match.”

Lyra thought about Ferrand’s figures, and the month after midwinter, and the pause in which paper had moved.

“And if the grain can’t be found?”

“It will be found,” said Elyas. “She’ll see to it.”

In Acasia he had doubted everything, including the direction of north. Lyra listened to his certainty and tried to be glad.

At the gate Berent asked whether there would be a feast, and whether knights in the prince’s household would be expected at it, and whether they would be expected to dance. Elyas said yes, probably, and certainly. Berent went pale.

Lyra walked home the long way, past the glover’s shop, and bought plums from a barrow at the corner because they were the first of the year, and ate two of them standing in Isaut’s doorway while Isaut told her who had been wearing southern sleeves and who would now be having them altered, and how much she intended to charge.

Chapter Ten — Marshal and Consort

Ferrand found the grain in nineteen days.

He did not find it where he had been looking. He had written to every house in the south that had ever sold grain to the Crown and received from each of them a courteous letter about the difficulties of the season. Then a merchant from the Amaranthine coast whom nobody in Valora had heard of put into the river mouth with a hold full of barley that his buyer in the west had refused on a quarrel about the price, and sent word upriver that he would sell the lot at a loss rather than carry it home. The two thousand two hundred measures cost less than the southern credit would have, and reached the northern garrisons before the passes closed.

People said it was the queen.

Lyra heard Ferrand say it himself, in Pellam’s room, with the merchant’s bill in his hand and an expression of great weariness. “She said we would buy the grain,” he said, “and we have bought the grain.” He did not sound as though he believed it had been her doing, or anyone’s. He sounded like a man who had been braced for a blow that had not come and could not yet stand straight.

The wedding was in the first week of summer.

It was held in the chapel of the old palace, which was too small, so that most of the court stood in the passages and the yard and heard it through the doors. Lyra stood in the yard with the chancery. She could see nothing, and she heard the promises only because the priest had a voice that had been trained for larger buildings. The queen’s voice was clear. Tylan’s was lower, and he stumbled once, on a word Lyra could not catch, and somebody near the door laughed and was hushed, and then there was a pause in which Lyra imagined the queen looking at him, and he said the word again, correctly, and the whole yard let out its breath.

Afterwards the herald named him. Tylan, Marshal of the Queen’s Armies, and Queen Consort. A man beside Lyra repeated the second title under his breath, trying its unfamiliar shape, as if it were a coin from another country.

“Consort,” he said. “Not king.”

“No,” said Lyra.

“Is that usual?”

“It is now.”

There were long tables in the great hall and more in the lower hall, and more again in the yard for those who could not be fitted into either, and the chancery was put in the lower hall between the treasury and the kitchens. This was generally held to be the best place. The food reached them hot. The wine reached them early and often, because the treasury clerks had made friends with the kitchen during the negotiations about the barley, and the kitchen had long memories.

Jehan sat beside her and described each dish before it arrived, having visited the kitchen that morning under pretence of delivering a message. He was right about the eels. He was wrong about the sauce, and took it hard.

“It was green this morning.”

“Perhaps it ripened,” said Lyra.

Across the table Ferrand was eating very slowly and not speaking. A treasury clerk beside him was telling a long account of a man who had tried to pay a tax in live geese. Ferrand let him finish, and at the end said, “And did we accept the geese?” and the clerk said yes, and Ferrand said, “Good,” and went on eating. Lyra thought it was the first joke she had ever heard him make, and was not sure he knew he had made it.

She found Lucan standing behind an officer who could not remember his name.

He had grown into the coat. There was a new confidence in the way he waited, and he was furious about being made to carry a cup. The cadets of the first intake had been told off to wait on the officers’ table as an honour, and had discovered that the honour consisted of carrying cups. Lyra asked whether he would prefer the officer to go thirsty. Lucan looked at the cup, then at the man talking with his hands at the far end of the table.

“Briefly.”

She laughed. He grinned.

“Isaut’s brother is on the other side,” he said. “He dropped a jug. The major said he had the makings of a cavalry officer.”

“Is that good?”

“Nobody knows.”

Later there was dancing.

It began in the great hall and spread, as the evening went on and the wine went round, down the stairs and into the lower hall and out into the yard, where somebody had found a fiddler and somebody else had found a drum, and the two had not agreed on anything but were both loud. Lyra accepted a man from the treasury who trod on her foot, declined him the second time, and accepted Jehan, who danced as he rode, as though the floor had been laid by an enemy. She laughed so much in the middle of it that she had to stop, and he stood looking at her with his hands out, offended, and then began laughing too.

She went out into the passage to breathe.

Elyas stood there with Mara, both laughing over an account she refused to repeat for Lyra because, she said, it concerned somebody still in the room.

“Berent,” Lyra said.

“I didn’t say that.”

“He danced.”

“He danced,” said Elyas, “with the queen’s mother.”

Lyra stared at him.

“She asked him,” Mara said. “He had been standing by the wall since the eels, looking as if he would sooner be at the Lesser Ford with the water coming up. She crossed the whole hall. Everyone watched her do it. She stopped in front of him and said, You are the young man with the orchard, and he said, Yes, madam, and she said, Then you will know how to find your way through a crowd, and held out her hand.”

“And did he?”

“He found his way through two of them,” said Elyas, “and into a third, which was the Marshal’s cousin, who has a temper. After that she let him go.”

“She was kind to him,” Mara said.

Lyra thought of the queen’s mother as she had seen her in passing: a tall, straight woman with a face that gave nothing away and a way of standing slightly apart from her daughter in any room, as though the distance between them had been measured and agreed. She would not have thought kindness was among the things the woman did in public.

“Was it kind?”

“It was deliberate,” said Mara. “It can be both.”

From the hall came a cheer.

They went to the doorway to look. The queen had taken Tylan’s hand to lead him out, and the room had cleared for them, and they stood in the middle of it for a moment not dancing, while the musicians fumbled for the right tune. Veralyn said something. Tylan answered. She laughed, and he looked at the floor, and then at her, with the patient, faintly embarrassed expression of a man who had known her since they were both children and was being asked to do something foolish in front of three hundred people and would do it because she asked.

They danced. They were not good at it. Veralyn led, which was not the custom, and Tylan let her, which made it worse, and halfway through they collided and she laughed so hard she had to stop, and the room laughed with her, and it was the best moment of the day. Lyra felt it pass through everybody in the doorway, the lightness of it.

Margery stood near the dais. She was watching the queen, and she was smiling, and when the dance ended and Veralyn came off the floor flushed and breathless the queen went to her first, before anyone, and put a hand on her arm and said something close to her ear. Margery laughed. Tylan had gone to get the queen a cup. He came back with two and gave the second to Margery without being asked.

Jaennis stood close enough to say something which made Veralyn laugh and Tylan look away towards the candles.

Lyra watched that. It was quite ordinary: a woman at a wedding making a joke to the bride. But she saw Jaennis arrive at the joke a moment after the others had finished laughing at something else, and she saw the small adjustment in Jaennis’s face when she found the three of them already turned towards each other, a circle closed without her. It lasted as long as it took to see it. Then Jaennis said her line and was inside the circle, and Veralyn’s hand was on her arm too, and Tylan was looking at the candles, and everyone was laughing.

Beside Lyra, Mara said quietly, “She wanted to be first.”

Lyra did not ask whom she meant.

Much later, when the fiddler in the yard had given up and the drum had not, Lyra found Elyas sitting on the steps outside the lower hall with his coat off and a cup on the step beside him, watching the last of the dancers.

“Where’s Mara?”

“Finding Berent. He’s gone somewhere to die of shame.”

Lyra sat down beside him. The stone was cold through her skirt. Above them the sky had gone dark blue and there were lights in all the windows of the palace, even the ones that were never lit.

“I thought you’d be pleased,” she said.

“I am pleased.”

“You look like a man counting.”

He picked up the cup and looked into it.

“I was thinking about the Lesser Ford,” he said. “Hodierne’s roof. It was mended with her son’s pay, which came on the day, because the Crown pays on the day now. Not Gaubert. Not the old duke. Not a treaty with a man in the south.” He turned the cup. “I was thinking that there are a great many roofs that depend on her now. Directly. On whether she pays.”

“She does pay.”

“She does.” He drank. “And if she were ever to decide not to, there’d be nobody standing between. That’s all. It’s a thing I thought, on the steps, at a wedding. It isn’t a complaint.”

Lyra looked out at the yard. A couple were dancing very slowly by the well, to the drum, which had lost its rhythm and found another. She did not know them. They did not seem to know anyone was watching.

“Hawise’s letter went out this morning,” she said. “With yours.”

“Will the lord agree?”

“I think so. His aunt has seen to it.” She paused. “Jaennis saw to it.”

“That was kind of her.”

“Yes,” said Lyra.

He glanced at her, and she thought for a moment he would ask what she meant by it. He did not. Mara came round the corner of the hall leading Berent by the sleeve, and Berent was saying that he had not been hiding, he had been looking for his other glove, and Mara was saying that his other glove was on his hand.

Lyra let herself stop thinking about the letter for the length of the next dance, which she danced with Berent, who trod on her foot, and apologised, and did it again, and apologised again with such despair that she kissed his cheek to stop him, and he went scarlet and danced the rest of it perfectly.

The lord’s answer came before the harvest. He agreed. His aunt, he wrote, had made a great point of it.

Then Hawise’s man wrote.

His name was Renaut. He was a carpenter, and he wrote the way carpenters measure, twice and with great care, and he said that he would marry Hawise gladly and work her land gladly but he would not put a new roof on a house that another man could walk in and claim on the strength of having been missing for years. He was very sorry. He had nothing against the brother. He had never met the brother. But he had been a soldier’s son himself and had seen what came home from wars, and he would not spend three summers’ wages on slates for a man who might come back to sit under them.

Elyas read the letter at Lyra’s table and put it down.

“He’s right,” he said.

“He’s partly right.”

“He wants a house.”

“He wants a house he can keep. So does she. So would Tibaut, if he came back.” Lyra drew a clean sheet towards her. “That’s three people who want the same house. It’s a sum.”

They worked it out between them over two evenings. It was Elyas who saw that Renaut would not mind the brother coming back if the roof came back with him, and Lyra who found the way to write it: that if Tibaut returned he should have a right to maintenance and a hearing, and a place in the house, and that any settlement of the holding between him and his sister should first repay to Renaut and Hawise, at a fair valuation, whatever they had spent on improvements in the meantime.

“And if he comes back with nothing?” said Elyas.

“Then he’ll have a sister and a roof and a place by the fire,” said Lyra, “and he’ll owe his brother-in-law for the slates, and they’ll quarrel about it every Christmas, and that is what families are for.”

Elyas laughed. He signed the letter to the lord first, and then, at Lyra’s insistence, a second letter to Renaut, explaining the clause in words a carpenter would use. Renaut wrote back within the month. He said it would do. He said he had measured it twice.

Hawise was married at Michaelmas. She sent Lyra a piece of the cake, very stale by the time it arrived, wrapped in a cloth, with a note in letters that grew larger towards the end to say that the agreement was in the chest under the bed and the key was round her neck.

Chapter Eleven — The Escort

The exchange was fixed for the day after the autumn pay, at the old stone crossing above the Lesser Ford, and Elyas rode down for it knowing only that the royal escort would be commanded by an officer from Valora.

He learned the officer’s name at the tower, from Wace, who had it on the order.

“Aeryl,” Wace said. “Cedric Aeryl. You know him?”

Elyas took the order and read it. The name was written in a clear, careful hand, beneath the seal of the Marshal’s office and above a smaller mark he recognised, after a moment, as Fayd’s.

“I’ve met him,” he said.

There were eleven Istovari men to come back. They had been taken in the raids of the last two years, most of them at the Lesser Ford and the villages above it, one of them from Cormin’s orchard while he was mending the wall. They had spent the time since in Acasia working somebody’s land. There were nine Acasians to go back the other way, taken in the incursion or since, and kept at the tower through the summer under Wace’s eye. The difference in the numbers had been the subject of four months’ correspondence. It had been settled by a sum of money which the Crown had paid, and which nobody at the ford was supposed to know about, and which everybody at the ford knew about to the last coin.

The escort came down the valley road in the late afternoon. Twenty riders, in coats cut to the same pattern, and in the middle of them a covered wagon for the Acasian prisoners who were not fit to ride. They came at a walk, in good order, with a man a little ahead of them on a grey horse, and when the man saw the tower he raised his hand and the column halted a hundred yards short of it, exactly, and waited.

Elyas walked out to meet him.

The man dismounted. He was of Elyas’s age or a little less, lean, with a good coat that pulled across the shoulders and a high collar. Above the collar, beneath the jaw on the left side, the edge of a scar showed white against his skin.

“Sir Elyas.”

“Cedric.”

Cedric paused at the familiar address, then took a folded paper from inside his coat and held it out.

“My orders. The Acasian party will be at the crossing at first light. I am to hand over nine and receive eleven, give my mark for them, and return to Valora with the eleven.”

“By night?”

Cedric glanced at him. “You’ve read them.”

“Wace has a copy.”

“Then yes. By night. The Marshal’s office would prefer the returned men not to be paraded through every village between here and the river.”

“Some of them are from these villages.”

“I know.” Cedric took the paper back. “That was going to be the difficulty.”

He did the thing well.

Elyas watched him do it through the evening and could find nothing to fault. He went through the tower with Wace and saw where the Acasian prisoners had been kept and asked each of them, through an interpreter, whether he had anything to complain of. Two of them had. He wrote down both complaints, and Wace’s answers, and asked Wace to sign beside his answers. He went to the wagon and looked at the man inside it, who had a fever and was lying on straw with his eyes half open, and sent for the surgeon, and asked the surgeon whether the man could be moved at dawn. The surgeon said he could be moved. Cedric asked whether he could be moved and then sent back over the mountains in a cart. The surgeon said that was a different question.

“Then answer it.”

“Probably. If it doesn’t rain.”

Cedric wrote that down too, word for word.

He ate with the officers at the tower, sparingly, and drank less. He was courteous to Wace, and to the sergeant, and to Berent, whom he recognised and greeted by name before Berent had recognised him. When Ermengarde came up from the village with a basket of bread for the escort and asked who was paying for it, he paid for it himself, on the spot, and asked her the price of grazing for twenty horses for one night, and paid that too.

“It’s half the rate,” Wace told him quietly. “The village rate.”

“I know,” said Cedric. “Sir Elyas’s terms. They were in the Marshal’s office. I read them before I came.”

Elyas heard that from the other end of the table. He had wanted to tell Cedric about the rate himself. Instead he sat listening to an officer who had already asked the right questions, ashamed of his disappointment.

Afterwards they stood on the tower roof, because there was nowhere else to be alone.

The river ran quietly below them. It was low that autumn, and clear. Across it, on the Acasian side, there was a light in the trees where the other party had camped.

“You fought at the Scar,” Cedric said.

“Yes.”

“So did I. The other end. I carried for the Marshal’s staff the last month.” He looked at the water. “I saw your returns come in. After. The ones with all the names.”

Elyas said nothing. He remembered the returns. He had sat up three nights writing them because he would not let a clerk do it, and Mara had come to the tent on the third and taken the pen out of his hand.

“You wrote not accounted for beside seven of them,” Cedric said. “The clerks wanted you to write presumed dead. It would have closed the returns. You sent them back three times.”

“How do you know that?”

“I was the one who carried them back.”

Elyas turned and looked at him. The scar showed white in the dark. He remembered it fresh: red and raised under two narrow strips of linen, in the lower yard at the Whiterun, on a morning when the physician had made him promise not to strike the boy’s face. He remembered the terms Berent had read aloud, and the opening he had offered on the fourth exchange, late enough to look earned. Cedric had not taken it. Cedric had gone for his throat. And Elyas, who had meant to give a little blood and preserve a face, had turned him and opened his own left arm to the next cut and let the blade find it, deeper than he intended, so that the steward could call it and the thing could end. He still had the mark. It ached before rain. He had never known whether Cedric understood what had been given him in that yard, or only that he had been made to stand in front of his father’s household and say he was satisfied.

Cedric was looking at the river. If he knew what Elyas was remembering, he gave no sign.

“I thought you were a fool,” he said. “Sending them back. There were men waiting on those returns for pay and pensions. Widows. Every week you held them up was a week they waited.”

“And now?”

Cedric considered.

“Now I think you were right,” he said, “and I still think you were a fool. The two aren’t difficult to hold at once.” He turned from the parapet. “We’re at the crossing at first light. I’ll wake your captain. You needn’t come.”

“I’ll come.”

“As you like, Sir Elyas.”

He went down the stair. Elyas stood alone on the roof for some time after, watching the light across the river, and could not have said whether he had been thanked, or judged, or only informed.

The exchange was made at dawn.

It was done with great care on both sides, because everyone on both sides knew what would happen if it was not. The Acasians came down to the far bank with the eleven, and Cedric took the nine down to the near bank, and the two parties looked at each other across forty feet of shallow water, and then an old man on the far side called something and Cedric’s interpreter called something back and the first of the Istovari men was sent across.

He came through the water very slowly. He was barefoot and thin and he kept looking back, as though he expected to be called back again. When he reached the near bank he stopped at the edge and stood with the water round his ankles and did not come out of it, and Elyas saw Cormin, who had come down from the orchard at first light without being asked, walk into the river in his boots and take the man by both arms and bring him up the bank.

“Aymon,” Cormin said. “It’s me. It’s the wall.”

The man looked at him. Then he began to cry.

Cedric waited until he had been brought up the bank, and then sent the first of the Acasians across. They went one for one, alternately, as had been agreed. The sick man went last on a litter, carried by four of the escort, who waded out to the middle and were met there by four Acasians, who took the litter from them without a word. One of the escort, a young man, stayed a moment in the middle of the river after the others had turned back, watching the litter go, and Cedric called his name once, quietly, and he came.

Ten Istovari men came across.

The Acasian party sent over, with the tenth, a belt of plaited leather and a knife with a horn handle, wrapped in a cloth. The interpreter spoke to the old man on the far bank for some time. Then he came to Cedric.

“The eleventh died in the spring. A fever. They say they buried him properly. These were his.”

Cedric took the belt and the knife. He unwrapped them and looked at them for a moment, and then he called Wace over and asked whether anybody at the ford knew whose they had been.

Ermengarde knew. The knife had been her husband’s brother’s. He had been taken from the lower field in the first raid, two summers before.

Cedric gave her the knife and the belt and asked her to say, in front of the interpreter, whose they were and where the man had been taken and when. She said it. The interpreter said it across the river. The old man on the far bank said something back.

“He says the place is marked,” said the interpreter. “With a stone. They’ll show it if anyone comes.”

Cedric wrote it down.

He did not go back by night.

Elyas learned this at noon, when he came down from the tower and found the escort still in its lines on the common and Cedric sitting on a bench outside Ermengarde’s house with a board on his knee, writing.

“The orders said by night.”

“They did.” Cedric did not look up. “Three of the ten can’t walk more than a mile. One of them can’t ride. I have one wagon and it has straw in it that a sick man has been lying on for four days. If I take them over the hills tonight in that, in the dark, I will arrive in Valora with eight.” He wrote a line. “I can explain two days’ delay. I would prefer not to have to explain two more graves.”

“And being paraded through the villages?”

Cedric did look up then.

“Half of them are from the villages. Their families are at the ford now. You saw the orchard man go into the water.” He went back to the board. “I sent to Valora for a second wagon at dawn. It will be here tomorrow. We’ll leave the day after, by daylight, on the valley road, and anyone who wants to look at them can look.”

Elyas stood beside the bench. Across the common, Cormin was sitting on the grass with the man called Aymon, sharing an apple, cutting it with his knife into pieces small enough for a man who had not eaten properly in two years.

“That was well done,” Elyas said.

Cedric finished his line before he answered.

“It was what the men needed,” he said. “It isn’t what I was ordered. I’ve written both.”

The report came to Lyra in the chancery a fortnight later, because the returned men had claims.

Every man taken in a raid on a Crown ford while in the Crown’s levy had a claim for the days of his captivity, at the levy rate, under a rule Pellam had made in the spring. Lyra had written the rule. She had not expected to see it used so soon, or by a man like Cedric Aeryl, whose name she knew as one of Fayd’s and whose history she knew in the way that everybody at court knew it, as a thing that had happened at a crossing on the Belworth before the war and been paid for.

She read the report twice.

The report was short, in a clear hand, each page divided into two columns. On the left, what had been ordered. On the right, what had been done. Ordered: to receive eleven. Received: ten, and the effects of the eleventh, identified by the reeve of the village as those of Garin son of Hue, taken from the lower field two summers past; burial place stated by the Acasian party to be marked; witnessed by the reeve and by Captain Wace. Ordered: to return by night. Returned by day, after two nights’ delay for a second wagon, on account of the condition of three of the men, as stated by the surgeon at the tower; surgeon’s words attached. And at the bottom, beneath his signature, a note: The grazing was paid at the rate agreed with the villages under the prince’s terms, which is half the rate stated in the Marshal’s schedule. I have not claimed the difference.

She took it down to Pellam.

“Have you seen this?”

He had. He took off his spectacles and rubbed his eyes.

“Fayd’s man,” he said.

“Yes.”

“He disobeyed his orders.”

“He wrote down that he disobeyed them. And why. And who can be asked.” Lyra put her finger on the left half of the page, and then the right. “I’ve never seen anybody do that. Most officers write what they did as though it were what they were told.”

Pellam put his spectacles back on and looked at the report again.

“He writes,” he said, “like a man who expects to be checked.”

“Is that good?”

“It’s useful.” He handed the report back to her. “Whether it’s good depends on why he expects it.”

Lyra filed the claims. The ten men were paid within the month, at the river house, on the day stated, and one of them, a man called Aymon, sent a message through the keeper to say that he had been paid and that he was helping Cormin with the wall. She kept Cedric’s report in the chancery with the claims, where it belonged. She made a copy of the first page for herself, the one with the two halves, and put it in her drawer.

Chapter Twelve — The Winter Grain

Perrine had put the grain into sacks because she meant to sell it.

Elyas had not been asked to come. He had been riding back from the Lesser Ford in the first week of the frost and had turned aside, as he sometimes did now, to a holding he had never seen but whose name he had carried in his head for two years: Matthis, son of Gilles, at the Sallows, below the river. He found it by asking. He found it, when he got there, with the barn doors open and the sacks stacked inside them in the grey light, and a man in the doorway with his hands beneath his arms.

Matthis had a cough. He had developed it in his last winter in the army and it had never left him, and it worsened in dust, and the barn was full of it. He tried to suppress it while Elyas dismounted. Perrine heard him from the house, crossed the yard, and pulled him clear of the doorway by his sleeve.

“Outside.”

“I’m outside.”

“Farther.”

Then she saw who had come in at the gate. She stood still for a moment with her hand on Matthis’s sleeve.

“You found us,” she said.

“You said south of the river.”

“I said it two years ago.”

“I remembered.”

She looked from him to his horse, then to the sacks behind her husband. He waited to be invited down.

“There’s a demand,” she said. “You may as well read it.”

The demand was lawful. Elyas read it twice at the kitchen table to be sure. Two years ago a bad harvest and a roof that had to be mended before the winter had used what the household had saved, and more. Matthis had borrowed the rest from a man in the village who lent to his neighbours. The lender had allowed one extension and now wanted payment by Martinmas. If the grain went to market to meet it, they would buy food later at the price of the hungry months. If it stayed in the barn, the lender could proceed against the holding.

Their daughter sat on the bench beside the hearth sewing a tear in one of the empty sacks. Elyas asked her name.

“Aline.”

“How old?”

“Old enough to be asked something else.”

Perrine gave her a look. The girl bent over her needle.

Elyas asked what quantity they needed until spring. Perrine knew. Matthis named a lower amount. They quarrelled over whether his mother would spend the winter with them.

“She eats,” Perrine said. “She may say she doesn’t. She eats.”

“She’ll be at my brother’s,” said Matthis.

“Your brother has four children in two rooms and a wife who wants her out. She’ll be here by Christmas and you know it.”

Matthis coughed. It went on for some time. When it stopped he did not go on arguing.

Elyas met the lender in the village the next morning.

The man was neither rich enough to forgive the loan nor poor enough to need it all that week. His name was Huon. He kept a mill and two cows, and his sister was to be married at Candlemas, and her marriage portion depended on several repayments falling due before it, of which Matthis’s was one. He feared that extending one would require extending the others. He said so frankly, while a child beneath the table tried to pull a buckle off his shoe.

“I don’t want the Sallows,” he said. “What would I do with it? I want my sister married to a man who won’t throw it in her face that she came with nothing.”

“If I found someone to take the debt from you—”

“At the full sum?”

“At the full sum.”

Huon thought about it. The child got the buckle off and held it up in triumph, and Huon took it from him without looking and put it on the table.

“I could sell the claim,” he said. “If your friends in Valora want it.”

“I have not offered that.”

“You asked what I could do.”

Elyas took the terms away. He had heard, in Valora and at the Lesser Ford, that Fayd’s people had begun arranging such purchases through a merchant house which wanted dependable returns from the south: the house bought a small man’s debt at its face, and the household that owed it paid the house instead, over a longer term and at a rate agreed in advance. It was being done up and down the river. Some said it was a mercy. Some said it was the merchant house buying the whole of the south one barn at a time. Elyas had heard both said by the same man on the same evening.

He wrote to Fayd’s office, and they answered within the week, which surprised him. The advance would clear Huon’s demand in full before Martinmas. Repayment would run across five harvests, at a rate that was not generous but was not cruel. Matthis would keep his grain.

Elyas read the terms three times. Then he wrote to Lyra.

He wrote to her as he would have written in Acasia, when they had both been cold and frightened and she had been the one who read the maps. He set down the terms and asked her to have someone read the right of early payment: if the harvest improved, could the household free itself, or must it pay for the whole term? What happened if a harvest failed? Could the merchant house sell the debt on to somebody else, and if it did, would the new man be bound by the same terms? He had learned to ask before returning to a barn with good news.

The answer took twelve days. Snow fell once and melted before noon.

It came in two parts. The first was Lyra’s letter, which said that the terms as offered gave the household a right to pay early but did not say at what sum, so that the merchant house could, if it chose, demand the whole of the five years’ return as the price of letting them go. It said that nothing in the terms prevented the debt being sold on. It said that the terms were no worse than a dozen others she had seen that autumn and a good deal better than some, and that she had spoken to Fayd’s clerk and then to Fayd, and that she enclosed what they had agreed.

The second part was the enclosure. It was a clause, fair-copied, with Fayd’s mark at the foot of it and the merchant house’s beneath. The household might pay early at any Martinmas, at the sum outstanding and no more. The debt might not be sold to another holder without the household’s consent, given before a reeve. And a copy of the whole instrument was to be lodged at the river house, with the keeper who had the shelf, and another at the chancery, so that if the house’s own copy were ever lost or amended there would be two places where the original could be found.

The last part was the difficult one, Lyra had written beneath. Fayd said duplicate custody makes it harder to know which correction governs. I said it makes it possible to see there was a correction. He gave way on it because it cost him nothing this time. It may not always. Keep your copy.

Elyas showed it to Mara.

“She got all of it,” Mara said.

“Yes.”

“How?”

He looked at the clause again. Fayd’s mark was very neat.

“I think she asked,” he said, “and I think he wanted the south to see that the terms could be made fair. It’s a good bargain for him if they trust it.”

“And for Perrine?”

“It’s a good bargain for Perrine.”

“Then it’s a good bargain,” said Mara. She handed the paper back. “I’ll come with you.”

Perrine had already sold two sacks.

“For his mother,” she said, when Matthis began to explain. “She needed bringing here.”

The old woman had come a week before Christmas, as Perrine had said she would, in a cart borrowed from Matthis’s brother, with her bed and her chest and a hen in a basket that she refused to be parted from. She slept now in a chair near the hearth with a quilt over her knees. She woke whenever someone said a number and supplied another which nobody could place.

The new terms were read at the kitchen table by the merchant’s representative, a narrow, patient man called Josse who warmed his fingers round a cup before each new passage. Mara sat beside Perrine. Aline listened with her hands under the table.

When Josse came to the clause about early payment, Perrine asked him to read it again. He read it again.

“At the sum outstanding,” she said. “And no more.”

“That is what it says.”

“Who made it say that?”

Josse looked at Elyas. Elyas said, “A woman in Valora.”

“The one who wrote down his pay?”

“Yes.”

Perrine nodded slowly.

Then Josse came to Lucan.

Lucan had written from Valora in the autumn. He was nearly done with his instruction at the academy. When it ended he would be entered in the Crown’s service as a junior officer, with regular pay, and he had heard that a service allowance could be set against sums due to the Crown for a household that sent a son. He wanted it set against this.

“This is not due to the Crown,” Josse said carefully. “It is owed to my house. The Crown’s paper by itself would not touch it.”

“Then why are you telling us about it?” said Perrine.

Josse turned a page. Fayd, he explained, had obtained an agreement under which his house would accept the Crown’s payment against part of the purchased debt if a son of the household entered the specified service for the specified term. The Crown would pay the reduction to the house directly. The remainder stayed with the family.

“What if he’s hurt?” Perrine asked.

“The earned reduction remains.”

“If he dies?”

The man looked down at the paper, found the place, and read it. Death in service would not restore the remitted sum. Desertion before the agreed term was a different matter; the suspended portion could be called in, after a hearing. Perrine asked him to read that again.

“Does he know?”

“He will receive his own terms.”

“Before we agree.”

“Yes.”

Mara leaned forward. “Can they stay on the longer repayment if he refuses? Without the remission. Just the five years.”

The man put a finger beside the earlier clause.
