“At this amount.”

Matthis bent to see it. He could manage it, he said, if the coming year was decent. He said it the way Elyas had heard men say they could manage a march on a bad ankle. Perrine looked at the sacks visible through the open inner door.

“Then we take that for now,” she said. “And he decides for himself. Not us. Not you.” She looked at Josse, and at Elyas, and at Mara. “Him.”

Lucan came home at the first thaw wearing boots good enough to annoy his sister.

Elyas was there by chance, or what he told himself was chance: he had come down to the Lesser Ford for the spring pay and turned aside on the way back. He sat at the end of the kitchen table and watched the family eat.

Lucan explained the army to Aline over dinner. Aline asked whether it had taught him to lift a dish without tipping grease onto the table. He accused her of being jealous. She said she envied only his ability to be paid for leaving work to other people.

“I’d go,” she said, “if they’d take me to do something worth doing.”

“They take women.”

“To cook.”

“To cook,” Lucan agreed. “And to ride dispatch, and to keep stores, and there’s a woman at the Lesser Ford who’s a sergeant. She can hit a mark at sixty paces.”

“I don’t want to hit a mark.” Aline pushed her bowl away. “I want to weave.”

It was the first time Elyas had heard her say it. He saw Perrine hear it too, and look at her daughter, and say nothing.

Perrine let them quarrel until Matthis began coughing. Then she took Lucan aside. Elyas could see them through the door: her face lifted to his, the young man’s hands opening as he explained something. She made him sit and read the offer aloud, all of it, from the beginning, including the part about desertion. He read it well. He had learned to read in the academy, properly, from a sergeant who had made them read orders aloud until they could do it in the rain.

He chose to serve.

He said so to his father first, and then to Elyas, because Elyas was there, and then, after a moment, to Aline.

“It isn’t for you,” he said.

“I didn’t say it was.”

“I know what you’re thinking.”

“You don’t,” said Aline. “You never have.” But she got up and went round the table and put her arms round him from behind, briefly, the way she might have done when they were both small, and then went out to see to the hens.

The first reduction reached the household before his company left. Elyas happened to be there when Josse brought the acknowledgement. Perrine put it beside Lucan’s letter, examined both, and asked whether the grain was now theirs to eat.

“It always was,” Josse said.

She gave him a long look. He coloured and tried again.

“You may keep it.”

After he left, she opened one of the remaining sacks. Aline brought a bowl. The old woman by the hearth woke at the sound and asked what they were making.

“Bread,” Perrine said.

She measured flour with both hands.

Elyas rode home that evening through a country that was just beginning to go green. At the Sallows the barn doors were shut and there was smoke from the chimney. At the next village a man was mending a fence with new posts, and at the one after a woman was driving three young pigs along the road with a switch, and all along the valley there were holdings where somebody had borrowed from somebody to get through a bad winter, and somebody in Valora had bought the debt, and the grain had stayed in the barn. He went on through the dusk trying to remember the last time he had been so tired from being glad.

Chapter Thirteen — The Child’s Name

Two years after the wedding, in the second week of the queen’s lying-in, Lyra was sent to find a woman who could mend a cradle.

She was at the palace that week on Tylan’s business, which was unusual, and on a list, which was not. Before the queen went into her rooms she had signed everything she could sign, and left instructions about everything she could not, and there remained eleven undertakings that she had promised to answer in her own hand and had not had time to. A widow’s pension. A ferry. A disputed wardship in the west. The pay of a company whose captain had written to her directly and been promised, directly, an answer. Nobody else could sign them. Nobody wished to let them wait. Tylan had sent for Lyra and given her the list and asked her to keep them from going wrong while the queen was occupied.

“What does that mean?” Lyra had asked.

“It means,” said Tylan, “that you remember which of them will be hurt by a fortnight’s wait and which by a month’s, and you come and tell me before the fortnight is up, and I go in and ask her.” He had looked at the list. “She trusts you to remember. She said so.”

Lyra had not known that the queen knew her well enough to trust her with anything. She did not ask how. She took the list and learned it, and carried it in her head for a fortnight like a jug filled too near the brim, and it was because she was already in the passage outside the queen’s rooms, waiting to speak to Tylan about the ferry, that the nurse came out and saw her and sent her for a cradle-mender.

There were craftsmen enough in the palace to build a ship. None could undertake the cradle before the afternoon, and the woman who watched the queen’s daughter had discovered that one joint worked loose when she rocked it. She did not intend to wait for an appointment while holding a sleeping child.

Lyra knew a repairer near her lodgings. She found her eating breakfast and paid for the meal to be wrapped. They carried the tools back between them. At the palace gate the guard asked whether the woman had been summoned.

“By the person with the baby,” Lyra said.

He let them through.

The room smelled of warm linen. Tylan stood beside a window with Matylde against his shoulder. He had a stain down the front of his coat and the patient, baffled face of a man who had achieved the thing asked of him and been given it again immediately.

“She settled,” he said.

“She will wake if you say so,” the nurse answered.

The repairer knelt by the cradle. Lyra moved a basket out of her way. On a chair lay a small green garment with one sleeve inside out. A comb, a military report, and half a piece of bread occupied the table.

Margery came in for the garment. She turned the sleeve, felt inside the cuff and found a loose thread, which she bit through. Tylan bent a little so she could wipe the child’s cheek with the clean end of the cloth on his shoulder. Matylde slept on.

Veralyn came from the inner room fastening her cuff. She looked at the cradle and asked how long.

“Before you need it,” the repairer said, then realised whom she had answered. “Your grace.”

“Good.”

She took the bread. Tylan began telling her about the report. She listened while picking a hard piece of crust away, then asked him to leave it until she had eaten.

The child woke. The nurse moved towards Tylan, and the queen said, “No,” without looking round, and held out her arms, and Tylan gave the child to her. Veralyn sat down in the chair by the window and loosened her gown and put the child to her breast with the concentration of a woman doing a thing she had been told by three physicians and her own mother not to do, and intended to go on doing. The nurse’s face said a great deal. The queen did not look at it.

“The wet-nurse is very good,” Tylan said mildly.

“Then she can nurse somebody else’s.”

Lyra looked at the window. The repairer, on her knees by the cradle, looked very hard at a joint.

Lyra stood near the door, unsure whether to stay. The queen noticed.

“You found her?”

“Yes.”

“Thank you. Does somebody owe you for it?”

“Her breakfast.”

The repairer raised her head. “I have that.”

“Then somebody owes Lyra.”

It was settled with a word to the attendant. Lyra felt foolishly pleased. She had spent three days that week pursuing a much smaller sum on another person’s behalf.

“The ferry,” said the queen, without looking up from the child. “Tylan says you’ve been waiting to tell him about the ferry.”

Lyra told her. It was the ferry at Aubrac, on the Istovar road, where the ferryman’s lease ran out at midsummer and the lord had promised to renew it if the queen confirmed the toll, and the queen had promised to confirm the toll, and had not, and the lord was a man who took an unanswered letter as permission to do what he liked. The ferryman had eight children. He had been told nothing.

“A fortnight?” said Veralyn.

“Ten days. After that the lord will have let it to his cousin.”

The queen held out her free hand. Tylan put a pen in it, and a board with the confirmation on it, and she signed it on her knee with the child still at her breast, and gave it back.

“What else?”

Lyra told her. There were four more that would not bear a month. The queen signed three of them, one after another, in the chair, and asked about the fourth, and listened, and said it could wait until she had read the other side’s letter, and Lyra saw that she was right. When it was done Veralyn looked at her properly for the first time.

“You kept them all.”

“Tylan asked me to.”

“Tylan asks a great many people to do things.” The queen shifted the child to her other side. “Most of them write them down.”

“I did write them down, your grace. I just didn’t need to look.”

Veralyn laughed. It was the laugh Lyra had heard through doors, in rooms where something was being settled, and it was different heard close to: warmer, and more tired, and very pleased. The child startled at it and began to cry. The queen hushed it, still laughing, and Lyra went out into the passage with the three signed papers against her chest and the fourth in her head, and found Jaennis there.

Jaennis had a parcel under her arm, wrapped in silk. She was talking to the guard at the door. The guard was explaining, with great courtesy, that the queen’s mother was within and that the queen had asked for no more visitors before noon.

“Her mother,” said Jaennis.

“Yes, my lady.”

“Of course.”

She saw Lyra. Her face did not change. She smiled and asked whether the child was awake, and Lyra said she was, and hungry, and Jaennis said, “Good,” and gave the parcel to the guard and asked him to take it in when he could, and went away down the passage without hurrying. Lyra had seen the queen’s mother go in an hour before, a tall grey woman walking with the ease of somebody who had never been asked to wait at a door. The queen’s mother had stayed ten minutes, looked at the child, held her for the length of a prayer, and given her back to the nurse with the careful competence of a woman who had borne four children and, it was said, nursed none of them. Then she had left. Jaennis would have stayed all morning. The guard knew it, and the nurse knew it, and Lyra suspected, watching Jaennis’s straight back go round the corner of the passage, that Jaennis knew they knew.

When the guard took the parcel in, Lyra heard the queen exclaim at whatever was inside it, and call for Jaennis, and be told she had gone.

The naming brought Elyas north with Mara and a newly knighted Berent. Elyas had given him his spurs before the men who had followed him through the orchard; afterwards the new knight had returned to the household as usual and found that Mara still expected him to carry things.

Berent kept touching his spur as though it might have ceased to be there since he last checked. Lyra asked whether it fitted. He said yes too quickly, and Mara told him to stop twisting his foot before he unseated himself.

They waited with a crowd of relations and people whose place required explanation. Jaennis arrived beside Vaelrie. The younger woman had been laughing, but stopped when she saw a particular cousin. Jaennis took her hand without looking at him.

Varitin read. His voice filled the room without haste. When the child cried he waited until Tylan had shifted her, then began the sentence again. Lyra remembered him asking for the names of people who had failed to return from the war. Today he spoke a name everyone would know before the week was out.

Afterwards Elyas leaned over Matylde and let her grip his finger.

“A formidable hold,” he said.

“She’s had instruction,” Veralyn answered.

“From whom?”

“Everybody who means to remove something from her.”

Tylan laughed. His face changed when he did. Lyra watched him take the child back and wished, for no reason she could name, that Riarhin were there to see it.

They ate at Lyra’s lodgings that evening. The seamstress downstairs had lent her two chairs and declined an invitation on the grounds that she knew what Elyas ate and had already eaten it once that day. Mara went down to persuade her and returned carrying a pie.

Elyas produced a letter he had begun to Riarhin. He wanted Lyra’s northern carrier to take it. Mara told him to add that the child had spat on a southern envoy. He protested that it had been milk and quite accidental.

“That distinction will comfort him,” Mara said.

Berent asked if Riarhin would approve his spurs. Elyas said she would probably ask what he meant to do with them. Berent considered this, then went on eating.

“What do you mean to do with them?” Lyra asked.

Berent swallowed. “There’s land,” he said. “A little. My uncle’s. He died at midsummer and there’s nobody else. Forty acres and a house with a bad roof and six families who’ve been paying their rent to a steward who drinks.” He looked at his plate. “A knight can hold it. A squire can’t.”

“So you’ll be a landed man,” said Mara.

“A very small one.”

“They’re the ones who get into the most trouble,” said Elyas. “Big enough to be asked for things. Too small to say no.”

Berent grinned. “Then I’ll send them all to you, sir.”

“Please don’t.”

Mara told Lyra about the Sallows. Perrine had paid the first of the five years at Martinmas, in full, and on the day, and had made Josse write on the back of the instrument that she had paid it, in front of the reeve, and then had asked the reeve to write that Josse had written it. Lucan had been posted to a company in the north. Aline was weaving at home, on a loom that had been Matthis’s mother’s mother’s, and selling what she made at the market in the village, and talking about a weaver in the town who took apprentices.

“And the clause?” said Lyra.

“Perrine calls it the woman in Valora,” said Mara. “As in, it’s in the woman in Valora, Josse, read it again.”

Lyra laughed.

Elyas said, without looking up from his pie, “Cedric Aeryl was made a captain at midsummer. Did you hear?”

“I saw it in the lists.”

“Fayd’s recommendation. And the Marshal’s.” He turned his cup. “He’ll be good at it.”

“Yes,” said Lyra, thinking of a report in two halves.

Mara looked from one of them to the other and said that if they were going to talk about captains she was going to talk about the pie, which was the seamstress’s, and which had a crust that Mara intended to learn if she had to sit in the woman’s kitchen for a week to do it.

Later, after they had left, Lyra set the borrowed chairs outside her door. The room was warm from their bodies and the little fire. On the table lay a scrap Elyas had rejected because he had begun with a phrase too formal for Riarhin. Lyra turned it over and used the back for her own note.

She described the cradle, the repairer’s breakfast, the way Tylan smiled when nobody seemed to be looking. She asked about the garden at Glenruan and whether Riarhin still meant her to come.

She did not ask when she would return. The first version had contained the question. She put that sheet under the others to light the morning fire.

Chapter Fourteen — The Good Year

Lyra told Pellam that autumn, on a morning when the chancery smelled of wet wool because everyone had come in through the rain.

She had meant to tell him for a month. She had written it down three times and burned two of the attempts. In the end she went up to his room with a return he had asked for, and put it on his desk, and said it while he was reading the first line, so that he would have to finish the line before he could answer.

He finished the line. Then he took off his spectacles.

“Commissions,” he said.

“For the chancery, when you want them. For others when they ask. I would keep the rule about the receiving houses. I would still come in three days a week, if you want me.”

“And the other days?”

“The other days I would be working for whoever pays me.”

“Fayd.”

“Sometimes. And the treasury. And the Marshal’s office, about candidates. And a woman who keeps a wharf and has been paying her fee twice. And a carter who carried for the army in the last winter and still can’t find who hired him.” She stopped. “They come anyway, Master Pellam. I do their work in the evenings and on the chancery’s paper and I’m not paid for either. I’d rather be paid for the evenings and give you the days honestly.”

He was quiet for a while. Rain ran down the window behind him. On the desk the return lay with his thumb on its corner.

“You understand what you would lose,” he said.

“A desk.”

“A name. When a man in Istovar gets a letter from the chancery he answers it, because the chancery can make him. When he gets a letter from a woman who used to be at the chancery he answers it if he likes her.” He put the spectacles down. “And when somebody decides that what you know is inconvenient, the chancery is a door they must go through to reach you. A lodging above a seamstress is not.”

“I know.”

“Do you?”

She thought about it honestly, because he had asked honestly.

“I know that the chancery can make me write what it likes,” she said. “And I know that you’ve never asked me to. But you won’t always be the one asking. And there are people who come to me because I’m not the chancery, and if I stay they’ll stop coming, or they’ll come and I’ll have to tell them that what they tell me belongs to the office.”

“It does belong to the office.”

“Then I’d rather they didn’t tell the office.”

Pellam looked at her. Then he picked up the spectacles again and put them on and looked at the return.

“Three days,” he said. “And the receiving houses. And when a man in Istovar doesn’t answer your letter because he doesn’t like you, you’ll bring it to me and I’ll write to him on the chancery’s paper and make him.” He turned a page. “That isn’t a favour. It’s the rule. Go away. I’m reading.”

Fayd offered her a room.

He did it the same week. He had heard, as he heard most things, before she had told anyone but Pellam and Isaut. He found her in the passage outside the treasury, where she was waiting to see Ferrand about a wharf, and walked with her to the window, and said that there was a room in his office with a good light and a stove that drew, and that he would pay her a retainer, and she could take what other commissions she liked on the understanding that his came first.

“No,” said Lyra.

He did not seem surprised.

“May I ask why?”

“Because yours would come first.”

“Most people would consider that an advantage.”

“Most people aren’t trying to be somewhere you can’t find them.”

That was more than she had meant to say. He looked at her with interest.

“You think I would look for you?”

“I think you would never need to. That’s the difficulty.” She looked out of the window. In the court below, a line of carts was waiting to unload. “I’ll take your commissions, Master Fayd. I’ll take them on terms, one at a time, and tell you each time what I won’t do. You’ll pay me for what I do. If you want the other thing, there are people in your office already who do it very well.”

He was silent. A cart below lost its tailboard and a load of turnips went across the cobbles, and a dozen people ran to pick them up, and a dozen more stood and gave advice.

“Very well,” said Fayd. “On terms.” He smiled, with something in it she had not seen before and could not name. “I shall enjoy finding out what they are.”

The bridge at Varenne was opened on the first day of harvest, and it did not rain.

It had rained every day for a week. The masons had worked through it under sacking. The road on either side had turned to mud, and the royal company that had kept the road through the winter had spent the last three days laying brushwood so that the carts could reach the approaches without sinking to the axles. The night before, Harald had stood at the window of his house on the hill and looked at the sky and said that it would rain on the opening as it had rained on everything else in his life. In the morning the sky was clear from one edge to the other, and the river below the new arches was brown and quick and full, and the stone of the bridge was pale in the sun and looked as though it had always been there.

Elyas stood at the Istovar end with his brother and watched the first cart cross.

It was Gaubert’s. He had asked for the honour and paid for it, in the form of a loan to the masons in the last month when the advance had run short, and he drove it himself, badly, with his wife beside him and a barrel of wine in the back for the workmen. The horses did not like the new stone and would not walk on it until Wace came forward and took the leader by the bridle and walked it across, talking to it the whole way. The crowd on both banks cheered, and went on cheering, and when Gaubert reached the far side he stood up in the cart and took off his hat and nearly fell out.

Tylan was on the Valora bank. He had come down for the day, with a small escort and a large sheaf of papers he was pretending not to read. He crossed on foot after Gaubert, alone, and met Harald in the middle, and the two men shook hands above the river while a herald read the terms.

Elyas knew the terms. He had helped write some of them. The bridge had been built against its future tolls, with an advance from the treasury arranged through Fayd’s office and a merchant house on the river, and the tolls were assigned for twenty years to pay back the advance and keep the approaches. The royal company would keep the road until the duke could keep it himself. The men who had built the bridge had been paid on the day, every week, through the receiving house at the river, under a rule Lyra had written. None of that was in the herald’s reading. The herald read the queen’s name, and the duke’s, and the Marshal’s, and the date.

People cheered the queen’s name loudest.

He found Lyra afterwards at the Valora end, sitting on the parapet with her feet dangling over the water and a cup of Gaubert’s wine in her hand.

“You came,” he said.

“Pellam sent me. The receiving house wants its books looked at before they pay the last week. And I wanted to see it.” She looked along the bridge. “It’s good.”

“It is.”

“It went up in two summers. Ferrand said four when they proposed it.”

“Ferrand always says four.” Elyas sat down beside her. The stone was warm. “Tylan says the queen wants the next one in one.”

“The next one?”

“Downstream. At the Aubrac ferry.” He saw her face change. “You know it?”

“I know the ferryman. He has eight children.”

“The bridge would put him out of work.”

“The bridge would put him out of work,” Lyra agreed, “and his lord would take the tolls, and the ferryman would be offered a place collecting them, if someone thought to ask for it.”

“Will someone?”

“I will.”

He laughed. After a moment she did too.

On the far bank a boy had climbed onto the parapet and was walking along it with his arms out, and his mother was shouting at him from the road, and a soldier from the royal company was walking along the bridge below him, not hurrying, keeping exactly level with him, ready. The boy reached the end and jumped down into the soldier’s arms, and the soldier put him on the ground and said something to him, and the boy went back to his mother looking pleased and chastened in equal parts.

“It’s all going so fast,” Elyas said.

“Is that bad?”

“No.” He watched the soldier walk back to his post. “No. When I came home from the war I thought it would take twenty years to get the roads right. Pay on the day. Men who don’t steal pigs. A bridge here. It’s been three.” He turned the cup in his hands. “Everyone’s better at it than they were. Me. You. Wace. Fayd’s people. Even Harald. We know what to ask for now, and who to ask, and how long it takes. It takes less time every year.”

“And she asks for more every year.”

“She asks for more every year,” Elyas said, “because every year she gets it.”

“The ferryman,” she said. “At Aubrac. When the bridge comes, if nobody asks, what happens to him?”

“He’ll go somewhere else.”

“Where?”

Elyas looked at the river for a while.

“I’ll ask,” he said.

That night there was a feast in Harald’s hall, and Harald made a speech, and it was a good speech, better than Elyas had heard him make before. He thanked the masons by name. He thanked the royal company and Wace. He thanked Gaubert for the loan, which made Gaubert weep. He thanked the queen, and the Marshal, and the treasury, and when he came to the end he thanked his brother, briefly, for the watch at the Lesser Ford, and sat down.

It was the first time Elyas could remember Harald thanking him for anything in public. He found he did not know what to do with his face.

Mara, beside him, put her hand on his knee under the table.

“Drink your wine,” she said. “He meant it.”

“I know he meant it.”

“Then stop looking as though you’ve been handed a bill.”

Across the hall, at the lower tables, Wace was telling everyone about the horses on the new stone, and the men of the royal company were laughing, and Gaubert’s wife was laughing, and Harald at the high table was looking down at them all with an expression of cautious, almost disbelieving satisfaction, as though he had been told for years that his house would fall down and had just noticed that it was still standing.

Tylan leaned across and said something to Harald. Harald laughed.

Elyas put his hand over Mara’s on his knee. He had not expected to hear men cheering a bridge in his brother’s hall.

He drank his wine. It was Gaubert’s, and very good. Outside, it began to rain again.

Part Three — Protection

Chapter Fifteen — The Protection

Harald had dressed for the meeting in a coat Elyas remembered from a more hopeful dinner.

It had been altered across the waist. The alteration was skilful, but a strip of unfaded cloth showed when his brother lifted an arm, and Elyas found himself looking at it while a man from the treasury explained what the next advance would require. The room was cold. A servant had lit a fire too late to do more than warm the stone immediately behind it. Every man who entered found a reason to stand near the hearth before taking his place.

The bridge at Varenne had been rebuilt against its future tolls. Part of that income had then been assigned to maintain the approaches. The remainder would not support the guard Harald had promised to keep upon the road. It might have, in an ordinary year. It had not been an ordinary year. The rain that had held off for the opening had come back in the autumn and stayed, and the river had taken away forty yards of the Istovar approach in a single night in November, and the carts that should have been paying tolls through the winter had been going round by the old ford at the Lesser Crossing, where there was no toll at all. A royal company had kept the road through the winter anyway. Its service needed renewing.

Elyas had spoken to Wace the week before. Wace’s men had been at the Lesser Ford for three years now. Tassin had married Aude’s elder sister. Two of the others had taken land on the common, with Ermengarde’s leave and the village’s, and were clearing it on their free days. None of them wanted to be moved.

“Tell the duke that,” Wace had said.

“He knows.”

“Then tell the treasury.”

“How much?” Harald asked.

The officer told him.

“And if we refuse?”

“The company will be withdrawn when its current term is complete.”

“Will you leave the road undefended?”

The officer looked at him, then at Elyas. Neither answered. Harald pushed the paper away.

“They will not,” he said.

It was meant to end the question. Elyas could see the treasury man understanding that it had answered one instead.

They were given bread and a dish of stewed fruit while the papers were copied.

Elyas stood beside his brother at the window. In the yard below, a farrier was bent over a horse’s foot. Another horse waited, its lead looped over a post, occasionally moving its nose to investigate the farrier’s tools. The boy holding the first animal kept drawing the second one’s head away without any lasting success.

“You could raise what is due,” Elyas said.

“From whom?”

“The estates which have paid nothing while the smaller holdings have been examined twice.”

“They provide service.”

“When you call it.”

“And they will.”

“Call it, then.”

Harald turned from the window. He had the look Elyas remembered from childhood, when some reasonable proposal had required him to acknowledge a difficulty in front of somebody younger.

“You do not know what each man can give.”

“Neither do you unless you ask.”

“I have asked.”

“And?”

“They have had a war. They have had repairs. They have sons to settle and widows to maintain. They cannot be made into coin because you have discovered an arithmetical necessity.”

Elyas looked back at the yard. The horse had finally picked up a brush. The boy took it from him and set it farther away.

“Perrine has had those things,” he said. “Nobody has found them a sufficient answer.”

“Perrine.” Harald repeated the name carefully. “Is this another person living at your house?”

“No.”

“Then don’t bring her into every room you enter.”

Fayd arrived after noon with the proposed protection.

Elyas had known it was coming. Everybody in Vhasia had known since the spring. Sornay had accepted one for his barony in the west, and Lormey for his in the north, and the old duke of the coast, whom Elyas remembered from his childhood as a man who had once thrown a royal messenger into a fishpond, had accepted one in the summer and been seen afterwards at court walking beside the Marshal with every sign of contentment. Each had been different. Each had been, when Elyas read it, a sensible arrangement for a particular difficulty: a debt, a road, a garrison, a harbour that needed dredging and a duke who could not pay for it. Each had ended with the same short clause, in the same words, acknowledging the queen’s protection over the persons and rights named in the schedule.

When there were enough of them, somebody in Valora had begun calling her the Protector of Vhasia. Elyas did not know who had said it first. By the autumn it was on the coins.

Harald’s preserved the duke’s title, his ordinary courts, the customary service owed by his houses. It assigned defined revenues to the maintenance of the royal forces and roads. The queen’s officers would oversee collection where the revenues secured her advances. The authority was limited in wording to the undertakings named. There were enough undertakings to require a separate schedule.

Elyas read that first.

A ferry appeared. So did three mills and the bridge they had been discussing all morning. Another crossing, whose toll had been granted to a house related to Harald by marriage, remained under its existing agreement. The treasury man explained that the Crown’s right there began only after a default.

“Which may never occur,” Fayd said.

“And if it does?”

“The payment is made. The right passes.”

“Until repaid?”

“Under the terms of that agreement.”

Elyas looked for the release clause. It required repayment of the principal and costs from revenues already assigned to other needs. It was possible. He could imagine a rich harvest, an untroubled season, an unexpected sum. He could not imagine who would be allowed to wait while Harald assembled it.

“The companies are needed,” Harald said.

“Yes.”

“The roads are repaired.”

“Yes.”

“The alternative is to undo both.”

“There are other sources of money.”

“Name one available before the men leave.”

Elyas named an estate whose obligation had been repeatedly deferred. Harald shook his head. There was a marriage negotiation involving its heir; calling the payment now would endanger something he would not explain before the treasury man. Fayd listened with his hands resting beside the papers. He supplied neither the objection nor its answer.

“Let the queen protect what we cannot presently afford,” Harald said. “We do not cease to be Vhasian because she keeps a road open.”

Fayd said nothing. He was looking at the schedule, at the line which named the Lesser Ford.

“The company at the ford,” Elyas said. “Wace’s company. Under this, whose are they?”

“The queen’s,” said Fayd. “As they are now.”

“And the tower?”

“Built with the duke’s stone and the queen’s labour. It would be held under the protection, for the maintenance of the road.”

“And the men who’ve taken land on the common?”

Fayd looked up. For the first time that day Elyas thought he saw something in the man’s face that was not patience.

“I did not know that any had,” he said.

“Two. With the village’s leave.”

Fayd made a note. “Then their leave will stand. The protection names the tower and the road. It does not name the common.” He finished writing. “Thank you. That would have been a difficulty later.”

Elyas believed him. On the way home he tried to remember what Fayd had actually promised.

The public acknowledgement was held two days later.

There was room for more people than had come. The empty places showed between households which ordinarily made a point of appearing together. Harald spoke clearly. Elyas was glad of that. His brother had prepared, understood the importance of being seen to choose, and bowed without looking as though he had been bent.

Veralyn received him with warmth. She called the arrangement a protection of rights and persons already bound to her by promises she intended to keep. The herald read her title at the end of it, the whole of it, and for the first time Elyas heard the new words said aloud in a room full of Vhasians: Protector of Vhasia. Nobody objected. Somebody near the back began to clap, and others followed, and Harald, who had been looking straight ahead since he rose from his bow, turned his head at the sound as if he had been struck. Tylan stood beside her. The green stone lay motionless against her breast while she listened to the terms read aloud.

Elyas had refused to sign an attestation that the arrangements enjoyed the unqualified consent of the district’s houses. He had signed a narrower account of the roads he had inspected and the companies he had seen in service. Harald had been angry. Fayd had accepted the correction and had a fresh sheet made.

Now Elyas watched his name read where it belonged. The people hearing it could still believe more than he meant.

Afterwards Veralyn came to him.

“You look as though you’ve buried someone.”

“I haven’t.”

“Then tell your face.”

He smiled despite himself. She took his arm and drew him towards the side of the room, where an older man was waiting with a grandson determined to touch everything within reach.

“They’ve asked to meet you.”

The older man had travelled the southern road with a cargo that would have been robbed two years earlier. He wanted Elyas to know the new watch had escorted them past a damaged crossing. No fee had been demanded. One of the soldiers had carried the boy when the cart tilted.

“That’s good,” Elyas said.

“It is,” Veralyn answered, looking at him.

He crouched to ask the boy whether he had been frightened. The boy said no. His grandfather said he had screamed until the soldier gave him a button.

The boy opened his fist. The button lay damp in his palm.

Mara had not attended the acknowledgement. She had been asked too late to leave a woman she was helping, and had declined an offer to find someone else for her.

At dinner Elyas told her about the child.

“And the rest?” she asked.

He described the agreement. She listened while cutting a bruise from a pear. It was one of theirs, small and hard near the core, better cooked than eaten as it came.

“Did you promise for anyone who hadn’t agreed?”

“No.”

“Then keep your copy.”

He brought it downstairs. She made room in the chest beside the papers for the house. He hesitated before putting it in.

“What?”

“Nothing.”

She waited.

“I helped them make it work.”

“Sometimes it worked.”

He put the agreement away. Mara gave him the better half of the pear, which he disliked enough that she laughed at his expression. They cooked the rest with a little of the sugar Lucan had brought.

Two days later a letter came from Perrine.

It was in her own hand this time, the large, uncertain hand he had seen on the scrap about the shelf, and it had taken her some time to write. The Sallows had paid its second Martinmas on the five years. The merchant house had written to say that under the new arrangements in the duchy its instrument would henceforth be registered with the queen’s officer at the river, rather than with the duke’s court, and that this changed nothing in the terms. Perrine wished to know whether this was true.

Elyas read the letter to Mara in the kitchen.

“Is it true?” she asked.

He thought about the schedule, and the release clause, and the short sentence at the end that everybody signed.

“The terms are the same,” he said. “The clause is the same. Lyra’s clause. The copies are where they were.”

“Then what’s changed?”

“Who she’d go to,” said Elyas, “if the terms were broken.”

Mara was quiet for a moment.

“Write and tell her that,” she said. “Exactly that. Don’t tell her nothing’s changed.”

He wrote that the terms and the clause were unchanged. If anyone tried to sell the debt without her consent, she should go first to the keeper with the shelf, then to Lyra, then to him. Only after sealing it did he notice that he had left out the duke. He sat with the letter in his hand, but did not open it again.

Chapter Sixteen — Whose Seal Makes It Good

The miller’s widow at Aubrac had sold a quarter of her mill to her son-in-law in the spring before Matylde’s birth, and in the autumn she was told she had not.

She came to Lyra because the ferryman sent her. The ferryman had a place now, collecting tolls at the site where the new bridge was to go, at a wage that was less than the ferry had brought in a good year and more than it had brought in a bad one, and he had it because Lyra had written four letters and gone twice to the treasury and once, on a wet afternoon, to Ferrand himself. He had sent her a cheese. Now he had sent her Guillemette, which Lyra suspected he regarded as a second cheese.

Guillemette was a small, upright woman of sixty with a basket on her arm and a paper in the basket, and she laid the paper on Lyra’s table and put her hand flat on it as though it might try to leave.

“That is my son-in-law’s share,” she said. “Written in the lord’s roll at the Lady Day court, before the steward and two men who can swear to it. He paid me for it. I have the money. I have spent some of the money. And now a man from the river comes and says it isn’t a sale.”

Lyra read the paper. It was a fair copy of an entry in a manor roll, of the kind she had seen a thousand times: a sale of a fourth part of the mill at Aubrac, with its water and its suit, from Guillemette widow of Andri to Colin her daughter’s husband, for a sum stated, in open court, witnessed. There was nothing wrong with it.

“Who is the man from the river?”

“The queen’s officer. At the new house by the bridge that isn’t built.”

“What did he say?”

“That a sale of a mill in the schedule isn’t good unless his seal is on it.” Guillemette’s hand pressed harder on the paper. “What schedule? I’ve never been in a schedule in my life.”

The schedule was the one attached to the protection of the lordship of Aubrac, accepted by its lord in the summer.

Lyra found it in the chancery on her first morning of the week. It was very like the one she had seen for Harald’s duchy, and for Sornay’s barony, and for the others: a list of revenues set aside for the maintenance of the queen’s roads and forces, and of the places and rights that produced them. The ferry was on it. So was the mill at Aubrac, entire, with its water and its suit, because the lord had long taken a share of the multure, and the multure was one of the revenues. The protection said nothing about the sale of shares in things on the schedule.

A circular from the treasury did.

It had gone out in the late summer to every queen’s officer in the protected lordships, and it said, in four careful paragraphs, that instruments touching revenues assigned under a protection should be registered with the queen’s officer and endorsed with his seal, so that the Crown might know at any time who held what part of a revenue pledged to its service; and that instruments not so endorsed should not be recognised by the Crown’s officers for the purposes of the protection. It was signed by a clerk of the treasury Lyra did not know, over Ferrand’s mark.

She read it three times. It was sensible. It was the kind of thing she would have written herself, if she had been asked to make sure that the Crown knew who owned the things it was relying on. It did not say that a sale entered in the lord’s roll was void. It said the queen’s officer should not recognise it. Guillemette had already spent some of the money.

She went to Aubrac.

It was three days’ ride. The ferryman met her at the landing, which was still a landing, with a new hut beside it where he sat with a strongbox and a notched stick for the carts, and he waved her across the water for nothing and walked her up the lane to the mill himself.

Colin the son-in-law was there, grinding. He was a big, anxious young man with flour to the elbows who wanted very much to be told that he had not been cheated, and could not believe it when he was. The steward who had entered the sale was there too, summoned by Guillemette, and swore to it again in front of Lyra with the resentful air of a man whose court roll had been called a liar. And the queen’s officer from the river came up in the afternoon, because he had heard that a woman from Valora was asking questions, and wished to answer them himself before she made up her mind.

He was a decent man. Lyra saw that at once and was sorry, because it would have been easier otherwise. His name was Thibaut Marchal and he had been a notary in a market town before the protection, and he kept his seal on a cord round his wrist, and he explained with real distress that he had received the circular in the summer, and that the sale had come to him for endorsement in the autumn, and that by then the merchant house at the river had bought the lord’s share of the mill and asked him to register the whole of it in their name.

“The whole,” said Lyra.

“They had bought the lord’s interest. The lord’s interest, under his roll, was the whole. Less any sale he had allowed in his court.” Marchal turned the seal on its cord. “The circular said I should not recognise instruments not endorsed. Colin’s wasn’t endorsed. The house’s was. So I registered the house’s.”

“Did you know about Colin’s?”

“Guillemette told me. Afterwards.”

“And?”

“And I wrote to the treasury and asked what I should do.” He looked at her with the unhappiness of a conscientious man who has done what he was told and seen what came of it. “They have not answered.”

That evening, at the inn by the landing, a clerk came to find her.

He was Marchal’s clerk, a thin young man called Thierry with ink-stained cuffs and a habit of looking over his shoulder, and he sat down across from her at the table and did not order anything, and for some time said nothing either.

“You should know something,” he said at last.

“Tell me.”

“Not if you’ll take it to Master Fayd.”

Lyra put down her cup.

“Why Master Fayd?”

Thierry looked at the door. “Because it’ll go back to him, and from him to my master, and from my master to me, and I’ll be the one who told.” He swallowed. “The house bought the lord’s share in the first week of summer. The circular came in the last week. The schedule wasn’t published till after the protection was accepted, at midsummer. But the house’s man knew the mill was on it in the spring. He said so, in the office, in front of me. He said Fayd’s people had told him which mills in the lordship would be in the schedule, so that the house could buy before the price moved.”

“He said Fayd’s people.”

“He said the office. I knew what he meant. So did my master.”

Lyra sat very still. Across the room somebody laughed, and a dog under the next table sighed and rolled over.

“If that’s true,” she said, “Colin’s share was bought out from under him by somebody who knew before anybody else that his roll was about to stop counting.”

“Yes.”

“And you want me not to say so.”

“I want you not to say so to Fayd.” Thierry’s hands were shaking. “Say it to whoever you like in Valora. The chancellor. The Marshal. Only don’t let it be traced to this office, and don’t take it to his, because his is where it came from.”

Sitting there with the cup going cold in her hand, she saw that there was nobody in Valora to whom she could say it who would not ask where it had come from, and that the first thing any of them would do, if they believed it, was ask Fayd.

“I won’t take it to Fayd,” she said.

Thierry let out his breath.

“And I won’t use it,” said Lyra. “Not without your leave. Not in a way that points here.”

“Then what good is it to you?”

“None, probably.” She drank the cold wine. “But you told me, and now I know, and I’d rather know.”

It cost her.

It cost her first the argument she had meant to make. Without Thierry’s fact she could say that Colin’s sale was good in the lord’s roll and that the circular had come after it, and she said both, in a letter to the treasury, very clearly. What she could not say was that the house had bought knowing that the roll was about to stop counting, and without that the letter was a complaint about the timing of a sensible rule, and the treasury had a great many of those.

It cost her next an introduction. She asked a clerk in Ferrand’s office, whom she knew slightly, to arrange for her to see the man who had drafted the circular. The clerk asked why. She said she wished to understand what the circular was meant to do about sales made before it. The clerk said that was a question for the treasury’s answer, which would come in due course. She said she would like to ask it in person. He asked again why. She could not tell him, and he heard that she could not, and the introduction did not happen.

And it cost Colin a season. The treasury’s answer came at Candlemas, and it said that the matter of sales made before the circular was under consideration and would be resolved in a general direction, and that in the meantime the queen’s officer at Aubrac should continue to recognise the registration he had made. The merchant house took the whole of the multure that spring. Colin ground the grain and was paid a wage for it by the house, as though he were a servant in his own mill, and Guillemette wrote to Lyra a letter which said nothing at all about the money and a great deal about her son-in-law’s face.

Lyra kept the letter. She did not take it to Fayd.

He came to her in the spring.

He came himself, which he had never done, climbing the stair above the seamstress’s shop with his hat in his hand and his head bent under the low turn of it, and knocking on her door. Oudine let him up. She stood at the foot of the stair afterwards with her pins in her mouth for some time, looking at the place where he had gone.

Fayd sat in Lyra’s second chair. He looked round the room once, briefly, taking it in: the window, the rocking table with its folded paper under the short leg, the whetstone, the hanging in front of the bed. Then he looked at her.

“Aubrac,” he said.

She said nothing.

“You wrote to the treasury in the autumn about a sale of a quarter-share. The treasury wrote back that the matter was under consideration. You asked to see the man who drafted the circular, and did not say why, and were refused.” He put his hat on his knee. “I have come to ask whether you would still like to see him.”

“Why?”

“Because the general direction about sales made before the circular is being written now, and it will be better if somebody who has been to Aubrac reads it before it goes out.” He paused. “And because I would like the south to see that these things can be made fair.”

Elyas had written almost those words about him once, in a letter about the Sallows, as a reason to trust the clause. She wondered whether Fayd knew that, and thought he probably did.

“On what terms?”

“Yours,” said Fayd. “As always. You read the draft. You tell the clerk what you think of it. You tell me what you think of it, or not, as you please.”

“And if I think the house bought knowing the mill was on the schedule?”

He did not move. His face did nothing at all.

“Then you would say so,” he said, “and I would ask how you knew, and you would not tell me, and I would have to decide what to make of it.” He stood and picked up his hat. “I expect I would decide that you had a reason. You usually do.”

He went to the door. At the door he turned.

“The direction will say that sales entered in a lord’s roll before the circular reached the queen’s officer are to be recognised. With his seal added, on application, without fee. I have seen the draft. That part is already in it.” He put his hat on. “You may want to read it anyway. Good afternoon.”

She read it anyway. It said what he had said. She sat with the clerk who had drafted it, a fussy, pleasant man who kept a sparrow in a cage in his office and fed it crumbs from his breakfast, and she went through it line by line, and found two places where a widow in Aubrac could still be cheated if an officer was careless, and he changed both.

The direction went out at midsummer. Colin’s quarter-share was endorsed at the river with Marchal’s seal, without fee, on application, and the merchant house wrote to say it was happy to acknowledge the error in registration and would repay the multure it had taken from his share since the spring. It repaid it. Guillemette sent Lyra a cheese.

Lyra did not know, afterwards, whether Fayd had learned about the house’s early knowledge from somebody else, or had always known, or had guessed from her silence. She knew that she had kept Thierry’s fact, and that Colin had lost a season’s multure while she kept it.

She knew also that she would do it again. She did not much like knowing that. She ate the cheese.

Chapter Seventeen — A Person to Spare

Aline’s trunk stood on the bed, open and half packed.

Mara saw it before Elyas did. He was listening to Matthis explain the new assessment and believed, at first, that the girl was visiting somebody. The examiner waited at the table with a cap laid across his knee. Perrine stood behind him. Nobody had offered him a drink.

Before harvest, Perrine had sent for Elyas with a note carried by the reeve’s boy that said only Come if you can. There is a man here counting us. He had come the next morning, with Mara, because Mara had read the note over his shoulder and put on her boots.

The Sallows lay beside a road now marked for widening. Surveyors had come through in the spring with chains and stakes and gone away again without saying what they were for, and in the summer a notice had been read at the church door about the queen’s road from the river to the southern approaches, and the stakes had turned out to run along the bottom of Matthis’s lower field. The purchased debt had been reduced by Lucan’s service but not extinguished. New charges for protection and road work had fallen due under the schedule, along with an older levy which Matthis believed his carting for the bridge at Varenne should have satisfied. The examiner had allowed part of the carting. The remainder was beyond what the household could pay that season.

Another service remission was available. Aline was of age. She was healthy and had been judged capable of instruction.

“She has instruction,” Mara said.

The examiner glanced at the trunk.

“In what?”

“Weaving. She has a place to go.”

“There are looms here.”

“There are horses here,” Aline said. “You let my brother leave.”

Elyas watched the man look from her to his return. His name was Rainaud. He had a list of fifty holdings on this road and the next and had been to thirty-one of them. He did not seem cruel. He seemed tired of households explaining why the person he had been sent to identify could not be spared.

“Your brother is serving.”

“I want to work.”

“And be paid,” Perrine added.

“She would be paid.”

“Doing this,” Mara said, putting a hand on the trunk’s lid.

Matthis said, into the silence, “We could pay the house off.”

Everyone looked at him.

“The clause.” He turned to Elyas. “The woman in Valora’s clause. Any Martinmas, the sum outstanding, and no more. It’s the third year. There’s less outstanding than there was. If we paid the house off, the debt’s gone, and there’s only the Crown’s charges, and we could meet those if we weren’t paying the house as well.”

Rainaud looked at his papers. He did it slowly, as though he were hoping to find something different there from what he knew he would find.

“With what?” he said.

Matthis did not answer. Perrine did.

“The lower strip.”

“The lower strip is on the survey,” said Rainaud. “The road authority would buy it. At the survey price. Which is fair.” He turned a page. “It would be enough to pay the house.”

“And the water?” Elyas asked.

“The road authority would preserve access.”

“Whose access?”

The examiner turned another page. The draft reservation allowed the household to water its stock at stated times. Elyas read it. The words were clear. The old freedom to go down with a thirsty animal would become a permission shared with whatever the road required.

He looked at the clause in his mind, the one Lyra had fought for and Fayd had initialled with his neat mark. At any Martinmas, at the sum outstanding and no more. It was there. It had been kept. Josse had never once tried to charge them more than was owed. The Sallows could free itself of the house tomorrow, exactly as the clause promised.

It would only cost them the water.

They went into the yard to see the stock.

The examiner had counted two adults able to do the regular work besides Aline. Matthis’s mother, now largely confined to the chair, was entered as a dependant. Perrine pointed out that someone had to lift and wash her. The man said allowance had been made for care.

“How much?” Mara asked.

“It isn’t reckoned by the hour.”

“Then what have you allowed?”

He showed her. Elyas saw her face change while she read.

“You’ve allowed that she’s fed.”

“And housed.”

“Who changes what she lies in?”

“Her family.”

“That is what we’re asking you to count.”

He took the sheet back. In the barn Matthis bent to lift a sack and began coughing. The examiner waited until he had finished, then amended something.

“If his capacity is reduced, the service remedy may not be permitted.”

Perrine stared at him.

“You’ll say we can’t spare her and take the land?”

“A sale of the lower strip would satisfy the present demand.”

“You said that would pay the house.”

“It would pay the house, or it would pay the Crown’s charges.” Rainaud looked at the sheet. “Not both.”

Elyas asked to see how the capacity had been reckoned.

Rainaud showed him. Elyas read it looking for the error. Two adults able to do the regular work, less something for Matthis’s cough, which Rainaud had now entered. One dependant, fed and housed. Stock: a mare, two cows, a dozen sheep, hens. Expected yield from the upper field, the lower field, the orchard strip. Against that, the charges. And beneath it, set apart at the foot of the sheet, one young woman of eighteen, in health, unmarried, not needed for the regular work of the holding as reckoned above, and therefore capable of being spared.

“Who does the harvest?” Elyas asked.

“The family.”

“Which family? You have two adults, and one of them coughs.”

“There is help at harvest. I have entered it.” Rainaud pointed. “A boy. Perrine’s cousin’s son.”

“He isn’t counted as a worker.”

“He isn’t resident.”

“And Aline is?”

“She is resident.”

“She is leaving,” said Elyas. “Her trunk is on the bed. She has a place with a weaver in the town at Michaelmas. You’ve counted her as resident so that you can count her as spare, and you’ve not counted the boy because he isn’t resident, though he’ll be here every harvest for as long as his mother lives. If you counted them the same way, they’d come out the other way.”

Rainaud looked at the foot of the sheet. He did not answer at once.

“And the loom,” said Mara.

He looked up.

“There’s a loom in the house. You walked past it. It was Matthis’s grandmother’s. Aline has been weaving on it for two years and selling what she makes at the market in the village, and last Martinmas the money from the loom paid a third of what they owed the house.” Mara held out her hand for the sheet. “Where is it?”

“I don’t assess looms.”

“Then you haven’t assessed what this household can bear. You’ve assessed what it would bear if it were a farm and nothing else.” She did not raise her voice. “It isn’t. It’s a farm and a loom and an old woman who has to be lifted four times a day by somebody who knows how, and that somebody is Perrine, and when Perrine is lifting her she isn’t in the field. You haven’t counted that either.”

Rainaud looked at her. Then he took the sheet back and sat down on the edge of the water-trough and began, very slowly, to write in the margins.

Aline stood in the barn doorway holding a brass buckle. She had taken it from her trunk, perhaps to do something with her hands. She bent the tongue back and forth until Perrine told her she would break it.

Lucan arrived the next day on leave.

He came to Elyas’s house first, because Perrine had written that the prince was helping. He wore a soldier’s coat with an ensign’s mark on the sleeve and brought a little parcel of sugar for Mara. She thanked him and put it away before he could make it part of his request.

“Aline thinks she’ll be put among strangers.”

“She will,” Mara said.

“So was I.”

“Did you want to go?”

“Yes.”

“Does she?”

He looked towards Elyas.

“They’re good terms, sir.”

“I’ve read them.”

“She could learn. There are women in the companies. Good ones. There’s a woman in my company who keeps the stores and she’s better at it than any man we’ve had, and she’s paid the same, and nobody lays a hand on her because the captain would have them flogged and then the rest of us would have them again.” He leaned forward. “It’s a good life, sir. Madam. I didn’t know it would be. I thought I was going for the pay and to get Father out of the house debt. But I eat every day. I sleep dry. I’ve learned to read properly, and to reckon, and to swim, and I’ve seen the sea. When I tell a man to do a thing he does it because the rule says so, not because my father is somebody’s cousin.” He stopped. “I’m not saying it to persuade you. I’m saying it because it’s true.”

“I believe you,” said Elyas.

“She thinks I’m asking her because of the money.”

“Are you?”

Lucan moved his cap from one hand to the other. The gesture was his father’s, and Elyas suddenly remembered the receiving yard, the new boots, the man trying not to seem lame.

“I can’t pay it all.”

“No one has asked you to.”

“She has. By refusing.”

Mara sat down opposite him.

“Then tell her that. Don’t tell her she wants your life.”

He told her. Elyas was not there. Mara was, having gone down to the Sallows with Lucan in the cart on the pretext of taking Perrine a jar of the sugar. She told Elyas about it that night.

“He told her it was a good life. All of it. The dry beds and the reading and the sea.”

“And?”

“And she said she believed him.” Mara was unpinning her hair in front of the little glass, and she stopped with a pin in her fingers. “She said, I believe you, and I’m glad, and it isn’t mine. And he said it could be. And she said, That’s what they all say about the thing they chose.”

Elyas was quiet.

“Then she said she’d go if she had to,” Mara went on, “if it was that or the strip, because she wouldn’t have them lose the water on her account. She said it very calmly. And Perrine went out of the room.”

“What did Lucan say?”

“Nothing. He sat there.” Mara took out the pin. “Then he said, I didn’t come to make you, and she said, I know, that’s why I can say it to you, and he put his head in his hands.”

They obtained a postponement until harvest.

Elyas rode to the river and saw the road officer and the queen’s officer both. He took Rainaud’s sheet, with Rainaud’s own notes in the margins about the loom and the boy and the lifting, and laid it on the table in front of them. The queen’s officer read it and said that the examiner had found reason to doubt his first assessment and that the service remedy would be held over until the household could be reassessed after the harvest was in. The road officer said that the survey could be looked at again for a route to the water that did not run through the lower strip. The merchant house accepted nothing yet. Josse wrote, very courteously, that a Crown postponement could not change the private portion without the house’s consent, and that the house had not been asked.

Elyas had to promise that the current crop would be gathered and a proposal brought before the next term. He promised. He wrote the promise down himself, and signed it, and felt, as he signed, the same thing he had felt in the receiving yard with Roul’s return in front of him and Lyra saying Read it: that he was putting his name to a sum he had not seen the bottom of.

Aline missed the first date at which the weaver could take her. Mara wrote to ask whether another would be possible. Perrine let the girl keep packing, although the trunk stayed open on the bed.

When Elyas left the house, Matthis came after him into the yard.

“I voted for the protection.”

“I know.”

“We all did. The carts got through last winter. Nobody took the mare.”

“Those were good reasons.”

Matthis looked towards the lower strip. Two men had put stakes in it while Elyas was inside.

“What do I say to her?”

Elyas had no answer ready. He went with him to pull one stake far enough aside for the mare to pass.

Chapter Eighteen — The Road Drawn In

Elyas’s letter came a week after harvest, with Rainaud’s sheet copied out in Mara’s hand and a question at the end of it that was not really a question: He is not the only one. Is he?

Lyra did not know. She found out.

It took most of the autumn. Rainaud gave her his list, when she wrote and asked for it, with a covering note that said he had no objection to the chancery seeing what he had done and would be glad if somebody would tell him whether he had done it right. There were fifty holdings on it. Other examiners on other roads were less forthcoming, but the queen’s officers had to lodge their assessments at the river houses, and the river houses had shelves, and the keepers of the shelves had learned since the peace that the woman from Valora read things and gave them back. By Martinmas she had a hundred and sixty assessments from four roads, copied or noted, and a copy of the survey for the queen’s road from the river to the southern approaches, which was not yet proclaimed and which she had got from a clerk in the road office by asking him for it on a day when he was too busy to ask why.

Lyra spread the survey across her floor because her table was too small.

The seamstress came upstairs for the chair she had lent, found her kneeling among papers, and asked whether she was moving. Lyra said no. The woman stepped over a proposed bridge to reach the window and held the chair there while deciding how to get it out again.

“You could do this where you work.”

“I am working here.”

“So I see.”

She set the chair down. Lyra had copied the names of disputed holdings on separate scraps. They lay along the road like bits of cloth awaiting pins. The survey was still provisional; the assessments were being enforced already. Two estates farther away had received extensions. A third, on the projected approach to a crossing, had been examined twice.

The seamstress put a foot on a corner before the draught could lift it.

“Is somebody losing their house?”

“Several people may.”

“Mine?”

“No.”

“Then tell me when you need the room.”

She took the chair downstairs. Lyra sat back on her heels. The floor was cold through her skirt, and she had begun with the confidence that fitting the pieces together would tell her what to do next.

It told her what had been done.

She laid the assessments along the road one by one, where their holdings lay. The survey ran from the river crossing south through the Sallows and the villages beyond it to the Lesser Ford, and then on, past the tower, towards the southern approaches and the passes into Acasia. Along it, like beads on a thread, lay the holdings that had been examined in the last two years. Some had been examined twice. Some had been found capable of sparing a son or a daughter; some had been found incapable, and had sold a strip, or a right, or a share in a mill; some had been refinanced through the merchant houses on the river and were paying still. Away from the road, on the other side of the valley, on the tracks that went nowhere the queen needed to go, there were holdings in the same condition, with the same debts and the same bad harvests, and nobody had examined them at all.

The assessments followed the line on the survey the way water follows a ditch.

And it was careful. That was the part that kept her on the floor after her knees had begun to ache. She found a holding by the Lesser Ford where the examiner had deferred a seizure for a year because the widow’s only son was needed to get the harvest in. She found three where the date on which a recruit was to present himself had been moved from midsummer to Michaelmas, after the harvest, by a note in a different hand which she recognised, after a moment, as the Marshal’s office. She found a mill where the sale of a share had been refused, not granted, because the man who would have bought it could not run a mill, and the examiner had written that the district would lose its flour. The harvest dates were entered beside the departures. She checked three more.

She did not know who. She knew what.

The official responsible for the assessments met her in a storehouse.

He was overseeing sacks reserved for seed. A farmer had accused his men of taking grain which the family required, and the official had come to have the seals checked. He showed Lyra the broken cord, made the porter bring the second sack, and waited while the contents were compared.

“The family had put eating grain in the reserved lot,” he said. “If we returned the first one without looking, they would sow badly and blame the collection.”

“Do they have food?”

“After this is settled, they will.”

She waited until it was. The porter resealed the sack. A woman standing by the cart crossed herself, took hold of its rail, and asked whether she could go. The official nodded.

Lyra laid the list of holdings on the lid of a chest.

“These follow the survey.”

“Yes.”

“You knew.”

“We cannot collect everywhere at once.”

“Why here?”

“Because the road must pass somewhere. Because the companies can protect this route. Because grain brought down it reaches market before it rots. Which of those troubles you?”

“The survey hasn’t been proclaimed.”

“The landholders will be paid for what is taken.”

“After the assessment has made them sell.”

He took the list. He knew the holdings by name and pointed to one where collection had been postponed until the crop was in. Another household had lost its only sound plough animal; the authority had provided a replacement under hire. At a third they had delayed a son’s enlistment because no one else could repair the mill.

“These people are still there,” he said. “They wouldn’t be if I followed every warrant the day it arrived.”

“And if they don’t enlist?”

“Then we consider the other means.”

He gave back the paper.

“Who told you which holdings?” she asked.

He looked at her with something like pity.

“The survey,” he said. “Which you have. And the road office, which drew the survey. And the treasury, which has to find the money for the road. And the Marshal’s office, which has to find the men to guard it and wants its recruits after harvest and not before. And the merchant houses, which hold the debts and want them paid. And the queen, who wants the road.” He picked up the broken cord from the seed sack and wound it round his finger. “Nobody told me which holdings, madam. Everybody told me which road.”

She went to the academy the next week, because the Marshal’s hand was on three of the assessments and she had a door in the academy wall that opened when she knocked.

Tylan saw her in the room above the practice yard. He had grown heavier in the last year, and greyer at the temples, and he looked, she thought, more tired than a man should look who slept in a palace; but he was cheerful, and quick, and he had her assessments in front of him before she had finished explaining.

“Yes,” he said. “Those are mine. The dates.”

“You moved them.”

“I moved every intake in the south to after the harvest.” He tapped the sheet. “The examiners were taking recruits in the spring. A boy goes in the spring and there’s nobody to get the harvest in, and by Michaelmas the household can’t meet its charges, and the examiner comes back and finds them a second son to spare, or a strip to sell. I saw it in the returns. I stopped it.” He looked up. “Recruits after harvest. Seizures deferred until the crop’s in. It’s in the direction I sent to every examiner on the road in the spring. It’s saved more holdings than anything else anybody’s done.”

“I know,” said Lyra. “I’ve seen them. Rainaud’s list. The widow at the Lesser Ford whose son got the harvest in.”

“Then what’s the difficulty?”

She looked at the sheet, with the Marshal’s note on it in a clerk’s hand.

“The difficulty is that it works,” she said. “Before, the examiners took a boy in the spring and the holding failed and everyone could see it was wrong. Now they take him in the autumn and the holding doesn’t fail, and the harvest is in, and the charges are met, and the boy’s in the army. It looks like a household that could spare him. You’ve made it better. You’ve made it work better.”

Tylan was silent for some time. Below, in the yard, a line of recruits was forming across a road and opening to let a cart through, and closing again, exactly.

“Would you rather I hadn’t?” he said at last.

“No.”

“Then what would you have me do?”

Tylan did not look pleased to have won.

“I don’t know,” she said.

“Neither do I,” said Tylan. “If you find out, come and tell me.” He handed the sheets back. “I mean that. The door’s still open.”

Fayd met her outside Pellam’s room, where both had been kept waiting.

She asked whether the Crown had purchased the rights necessary to connect the road. He asked which portion. She named it. He knew the answer without sending for a man.

“Some. Others remain in negotiation.”

“Are the households being told which?”

“They are being told what they owe.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

“No.” He looked at the door. “You know what happens when a purchase is announced before it can be completed. Every price rises. Every cousin discovers an interest.”

“And the people who live there discover last.”

“Bring me a particular abuse.”

She gave him Matthis’s terms, stripped of Lucan’s private letter and Aline’s angry comments. Fayd read them standing. In the passage beyond him two men spoke quietly in Ecktorian. One carried a case she had seen delivered to Fayd’s office before, though she did not know whose mark it bore.

“The house will agree to a longer term,” he said.

“On what security?”

“The holding. The lower strip remains available for purchase.”

“They need the water.”

“Then preserve access.”

“The draft does. At hours the road authority chooses.”

He read that passage again.

“I will ask for a standing right appropriate to their stock.”

“Can you obtain it?”

“Yes.”

The certainty relieved her. She hated that she had begun hoping to hear it before he spoke.

“I need the original demand retained,” she said. “And the earlier agreement. Nobody should replace them with a clean account of what has finally been granted.”

“They belong with the settlement.”

“They belong in the chancery too.”

“Duplicate custody makes it harder to know which correction governs.”

“It makes it possible to see there was a correction.”

Pellam’s door opened. Fayd gathered the sheets but left her the earlier demand.

“You can keep that.”

“I intended to.”

He smiled without pleasure and went in.

That night she wrote it down.

She wrote it three times. The first version said that the Crown was using its assessments to clear the line of the queen’s road of freeholders, and she read it and tore it across, because she could not prove using and she could not prove clear, and a paper that said what she could not prove would be answered by somebody pointing out that she could not prove it. The second version listed the holdings and the assessments and nothing else, and she read it and tore that across too, because anyone who read it would see a list and not a line.

The third version she kept. It set out the survey and the assessments together, holding by holding, with the dates. It said that the assessments had been concentrated along the line of the proposed road, and gave the numbers. It said that in many cases the timing of assessments and of service remissions had been adjusted to protect the harvest, and gave the cases, and said that this had preserved the cultivation of the holdings concerned. It said that the pattern could not have arisen by chance. It did not say who had chosen it. It did not use any word that meant intended. At the end it said: The households on the line of the road are being asked to bear the cost of the road through the ordinary operation of the assessment, in addition to any compensation for land taken. Whether this is the Crown’s intention the writer cannot say. It is the Crown’s effect.

She sent it to Pellam, and a copy to Elyas, and kept a third.

Pellam read it and sent for her.

“This is very careful,” he said.

“Yes.”

“It will be read as an accusation anyway.”

“I know.”

“By whom do you want it read?”

She thought about that.

“By whoever decided,” she said. “So that they know somebody saw.”

Pellam looked at her over his spectacles, and then down at the paper, and then he signed the covering note that would send it to the treasury and the road office and the Marshal, on the chancery’s paper, as a report on the operation of the assessments along the queen’s road, and put it in the tray.

The next introduction she requested was refused.

The merchant’s officer would discuss the family terms with Pellam’s appointee. He would not discuss them privately with Lyra. No one said the refusal was Fayd’s. She went by the proper route and lost eleven days.

At the end of them the concession was granted. Matthis kept access to water. Aline could take the weaver’s later opening if the household met the adjusted payments. Elyas was asked to attest that he had explained the terms and believed the holding capable of bearing them. He sent the paper back unsigned with three questions about the crop.

Lyra answered two. For the third she had to write to Perrine.

That evening Master Ridow read aloud a sentence from her translation and asked whom she believed the verb governed. She looked at it until the marks ceased to form words.

“I don’t know.”

“You did last week.”

“Yes.”

He closed the book.

“Come back when you’ve slept.”

She wanted to argue that she had paid for the hour. Instead she put the book in her bag. At the door he called her back and gave her a pear wrapped in paper.

“From the disputed tree,” he said. “You may draw your own conclusion.”

Chapter Nineteen — Assent

The order closing the old ford at the Lesser Crossing to carts was read at the church doors in the week after Candlemas, and on the Sunday following Ermengarde walked eleven miles to Elyas’s gate to tell him what she thought of it.

She did not come in. She stood at the gate with her scalded hands folded over the top bar and waited while Nelle went to fetch him, and when he came she told him, without greeting, that her village had taken its cheese and its wool to the western market by the old ford for as long as there had been a market, and that the bridge at Varenne was nine miles downstream and charged a toll going and a toll coming back, and that by the time a cart of cheese had paid both there was not much left of the cheese.

“Who closed it?”

“The queen’s officer. On account of the tolls.” She looked at him. “Because the carts go round by the ford instead of paying at the bridge, and the tolls pay for the bridge, and the bridge is in the schedule. So now the carts can’t go round.”

“Come in,” Elyas said.

“I won’t. I have to walk back.” She unfolded her hands. “I didn’t come to ask you to open it. I know you can’t. I came so that you’d know. Because Gaubert’s been telling everyone you made the bridge, and you did, and I wanted you to know what it costs at the other end.”

Mara brought her bread and a cup of ale at the gate, since she would not come in, and Ermengarde drank the ale standing and put the bread in her apron for the road. Then she walked back the eleven miles.

Gaubert came three days later, on a horse, and did come in.

He had heard about Ermengarde. Everybody at the ford had heard about Ermengarde. He sat at Elyas’s table and drank his wine and explained, with great good humour, why the ford ought to stay closed.

“The bridge cost money, Elyas. You know how much. You helped borrow it. The tolls pay it back. If every cart in the valley goes round by the old ford the tolls don’t come in, and if the tolls don’t come in the advance isn’t paid, and if the advance isn’t paid the house on the river takes the tolls for itself for as long as it likes, which is in the schedule, you read it.” He spread his hands. “I’m not saying it’s kind. I’m saying it’s arithmetic.”

“You lent the masons money.”

“I did. And I’ve had it back, with interest, from the tolls, and I’m carting for the royal company twice a week, and my wife has a new gown, and I’ve paid off my father’s debts, which I never thought to see. The road’s been good to me. It’s been good to half the valley.” He drank. “Ermengarde’s village is the other half. I know that. I’m sorry for it. But you can’t have a bridge and a free ford next to it. It doesn’t work.”

Elyas looked at him across the table. Gaubert was not lying, and he was not cruel, and five years ago he had been a man who lost horses and blamed three other men for it. The road had made him somebody who paid his debts.

“What if it worked for some of them?” Elyas said.

He proposed it at Varenne, at the bridge-house, on a wet morning at the end of February: the villages of the Lesser Ford, and only those, to take their own produce to the western market by the old ford on market days, in their own carts, without toll. Everybody else to use the bridge. The royal company at the tower to see that nobody else used the ford, since the ford was under its eye in any case.

It took a day. Gaubert argued and then agreed, because he could see that the villages of the Lesser Ford had never paid much in tolls and never would, and that the carts that mattered were the merchants’ carts, which would still have to cross at Varenne.
