Wace agreed, because he said his men were at the ford anyway and could as well watch carts as water. Ermengarde agreed, and so did the reeves of the three other villages, standing in a row at the back of the bridge-house in their wet cloaks, each of them looking as though he expected at any moment to be told that it had been a mistake. Harald agreed by letter. The queen’s officer at the river agreed, provided the merchant house that held the advance agreed, since the tolls were pledged to it.

The merchant house did not agree.

Its agent at the river was polite and very firm. He was not Josse; Josse had been moved to the south. He was an older man with a Viterran way of speaking, who had come up from one of the house’s branches across the Belworth, and he said that he had no authority to diminish the security on which his house had advanced the money for the bridge, and that any exemption, however small, was a diminution, and that he would have to write to his principals.

“How long?”

“A month. Two.”

“The market is on Thursday.”

The agent smiled with real regret. “Then I am afraid,” he said, “that on Thursday they will have to use the bridge.”

Elyas wrote to Lyra that night. He wrote the whole of it, and at the end he wrote: I have their assent, all of them, and it does not reach the one man who can say no.

She came herself.

She came in four days, in a hired cart, with a bag and a board and her hair coming down, and walked into the bridge-house at Varenne on the morning of the second market day while Elyas was standing at the window watching Ermengarde’s village pay toll to cross.

“Who is the principal?” she asked, without greeting.

He told her the name of the house.

“Not the house. The man. Who in Valora can say yes without writing across the Belworth?”

He did not know. She did. She had found out on the road, from a clerk in the treasury who owed her something and a woman at the river house who kept the correspondence, and she had a name, and the name was a man in Valora who managed the house’s affairs on this side of the river and reported to its branches on the other, and who had, she said, a desk in a room two doors from Fayd’s.

“Fayd’s,” Elyas said.

“Two doors.” She put her board down on the table. “I don’t know what that means. It may mean nothing. It may mean he likes the light on that side of the building.”

“Will he see you?”

“He’ll see you,” said Lyra. “You’re the prince who built the bridge.”

They went to Valora together. It took three days more. On the fourth the man saw them.

He was pleasant and quick and interested, and he had already read Elyas’s proposal, which surprised Elyas. Lyra did not look surprised. He agreed at once that the exemption was small. He agreed that the villages of the Lesser Ford had never been a large part of the tolls. He agreed that the house had no wish to be thought hard in a district where it had a great many debts outstanding and hoped to have more.

“But I cannot simply give up a security,” he said. “You understand. If I give up a little here without any undertaking in return, every district in the south will want a little, and I will have given up a great deal.”

“What undertaking?” said Lyra.

He turned to Elyas.

“Yours, my lord.”

Elyas waited.

“A list,” the man said. “Of the carts entitled to the exemption. Named. Certified by you each quarter, as the person who proposed the arrangement and whom the villages trust. And your undertaking that if a cart not on the list uses the ford, or a cart on the list is used to carry another man’s goods, you will answer for the loss to the tolls. Not the company. Not the queen’s officer. You.” He smiled. “It seems only fair. It was your idea.”

Elyas looked at Lyra. She was looking at the man.

“He would be answerable for every cheese-cart in four villages,” she said.

“He would be answerable for the arrangement he proposed.”

“And if one of them cheats?”

“Then he would know which one,” said the man pleasantly, “and could deal with it. Better than any officer of mine. They’re his people, aren’t they?”

They walked out into the passage. Two doors down, the door to Fayd’s office stood open. A clerk was writing inside. Neither of them looked at it.

“Well?” said Lyra.

“It’s what they asked for. The villages. It gets their carts to market.”

“It makes you the man who counts the carts.”

“Somebody has to.”

“Somebody,” said Lyra, “is the queen’s officer. Or the company. Or the house’s own man at the bridge. It isn’t you. It was never going to be you, until you asked for this, and now it will be you, every quarter, with your name on the list. When Ermengarde’s cousin puts his neighbour’s wool on his cart because the neighbour’s horse is lame, it will be you who has to decide whether to tell the house. And when you don’t, it will be you who pays.”

Elyas stood in the passage with the rain running down the window behind her.

“Is there another way?”

“There is two months of waiting while he writes across the Belworth. And then probably no.” She pushed her hair back. “I found the man. That was the part I could do. This is the part he wants in exchange.”

He thought about Ermengarde at the gate with her hands folded on the bar. He thought about the cheese.

“I’ll sign it,” he said.

“I know.”

“You think I shouldn’t.”

“I think it’s the only thing on the table.” She looked away from him, down the passage, at the open door. “I think we’ve made it possible for them to have their market day, and I think we’ve made you their warden to do it, and I think I found the man who made you their warden. I don’t like any of it.”

They went back in. Elyas signed. The man thanked him warmly and said that the house would be glad to hear of any other arrangements the prince thought might be of benefit to the district. The exemption began on the next market day.

Guichard was at the Lesser Ford when Elyas rode down with the first list.

He had eight men with him, in plain dark coats without a badge, and good horses, and they were camped not at the tower but in the orchard below Cormin’s wall, where Cormin had allowed it because, he told Elyas, they had asked very politely and paid in advance. Elyas had not seen Guichard since the end of the war, in a tent at the Scar, where Guichard was a squire of nineteen who had left his father’s contingent without leave to carry a wounded man to the surgeons, and his father Rousset had wanted him hanged for it, and Elyas had sat up half a night making Rousset give the boy a hearing, and then the other half making sure the hearing was fair.

He was twenty-three now. He had filled out. He came across the orchard to meet Elyas with his hand out and a smile on his face, and he said, “My lord. I’d hoped to see you. I owe you a great deal and I’ve never had the chance to say so.”

“You don’t owe me anything.”

“I owe you my neck.” He said it lightly. “I’m with the queen’s household now. The guard. The Mound, some people call it. You’ll have heard.”

Elyas had heard. He had heard it from Berent, who had heard it from friends, and from Wace, who had heard it from nobody and did not like it. Four men in the queen’s guard at the peace, then a dozen, then more, younger sons and heirs of minor houses, sworn to the queen’s person and to something else that nobody quite explained, going about on the queen’s business with the queen’s warrant and answering to nobody else. They had recovered stolen cattle from a lord in the west whose own servants would never have testified against him, and returned them to the tenants he had stolen them from. Elyas had heard that too. He had been glad of it.

“What brings you to the ford?”

“A man.” Guichard glanced towards the tower. “Taken up last week for threatening a queen’s examiner on the road. He said men could disappear on that road. We’ve had him brought to the tower for questioning before he goes on to Valora. I’m here to make sure it’s done properly.”

“By whom?”

“By us.”

Wace came out of the tower while they were talking. He did not look at Guichard. He looked at Elyas.

“My lord. A word.”

They walked down to the water. Wace’s arm was stiff in the cold and he held it against his chest.

“They’ve got a man in my tower,” he said, “and I don’t know what for, or who he is, or where he’s going. They came with the queen’s warrant and I read it and it’s good. It says I’m to give them room and not interfere. I asked the lieutenant there what the man was charged with and he said it was the queen’s business and he’d report directly to Valora.” Wace looked at the water. “Three years I’ve held this ford. Every man who’s been taken up at it, I’ve known what for, because I took him up, or the reeve did, or the queen’s officer did, and they told me. Now there’s a man in my own tower and I can’t get an answer.”

“Have you asked Valora?”

“I wrote to the Marshal’s office. They said it was the household’s business and not the army’s.” Wace spat into the river. “I used to be entitled to know who was locked in my own building.”

Elyas went back up to the orchard. Guichard was sitting on Cormin’s wall, eating an apple from Cormin’s tree, and he looked up and smiled.

“Captain Wace would like to know what the man is charged with,” Elyas said.

“I know. He asked me.” Guichard turned the apple in his hand. “I told him the truth, my lord. It’s the queen’s business, and I report to Valora. It isn’t that I don’t trust the captain. It’s that the warrant says what it says, and I’m bound by it, and if I start telling every captain on every road what the household is doing, the household can’t do it.” He took a bite. “You’d have said the same, in the war.”

Elyas thought that he probably would have.

“And if the man’s family want to know?”

“Then they can apply to Valora.”

“Through whom?”

Guichard looked at him with the open, pleasant face of a young man who had been saved once and had grown up to be useful, and was proud of both.

“Through you, my lord, if they like,” he said. “Everyone knows you’ll carry a letter.” He finished the apple and threw the core into the grass. “And if anyone ever troubles your house, you know where to send. We look after our friends.”

Elyas thanked him and rode back to the tower with the list of carts in his saddlebag. He gave it to Wace to hold. Wace read it and asked whether Elyas knew that Ermengarde’s cousin had already been seen at the ford with a cart of wool that was not his own.

Elyas said he would speak to him.

Chapter Twenty — Men at the Gate

Mara made the rules in the spring, at the kitchen table, on the back of a letter from Harald.

She had not meant to make rules. She had meant to make supper. But there had been three men in the hall when she came in from the garden, and one of them was asleep on the bench, and another had a sword across his knees and was oiling it, and the third was eating bread from the crock that she kept for the household, and none of them was anyone she had asked into the house. Nelle had let them in because they said they had come to see the prince. The prince was at the Lesser Ford with a list of cheese-carts. They had been waiting since noon.

When Elyas came home she gave him supper and then she gave him the letter from Harald, turned over.

Callers in the hall. Not the kitchen, not upstairs, not the garden after dark.

Weapons left at the gate with whoever keeps it. Collected when they go.

Food for anyone who has walked. Bread and what is in the pot. Not the crock.

Beds: I decide. Not Nelle. Not Berent. Not you.

Mornings are ours. Nobody before the bell at the chapel, unless somebody is dying.

He read it twice.

“Is this about the crock?”

“It is about the man with the sword on my bench,” said Mara. “And the crock.”

“They came a long way.”

“I know they did. I fed them. I’ll feed the next ones. But this is a house, Elyas, not a hall of justice. I live in it. So does Nelle, and she’s frightened of men with swords, though she’d never tell you. So does Berent, when he’s here.” She put her finger on the fourth line. “And if I don’t decide who sleeps under this roof, then every man who’s ever served with you and every man who’s ever been refused by an officer will decide it for me, and one night I’ll come downstairs and find somebody at my table whom I wouldn’t have let into the yard.”

He looked at the list again. Then he took a pen and signed it at the bottom, under her last line, and gave it back to her.

“What’s that for?” she said.

“So that when they argue with Nelle, she can show them I agreed.”

Mara looked at his name, and at him, and for a moment he thought she would laugh. She pinned it to the kitchen door instead.

After that they came in the hall.

They came in the mornings after the chapel bell and in the afternoons and, sometimes, at dusk, and they sat on the bench under the window and waited. A carter from the Lesser Crossing whose cousin had been caught with another man’s wool at the ford and wanted Elyas to say it had been a mistake. A notary from Aubrac with a question about a mill that Elyas could not answer and sent on to Lyra. A widow from the Sallows road whose son had been found capable of being spared and who wanted, she said, only to know whether the prince thought the army was a good life, and who listened to his answer, and thanked him, and went away without saying what she would do. Two brothers from the west who had quarrelled over a boundary for eleven years and had heard that the prince would hear anybody, and who wanted him to hear them both, at once, in the same room, which he did, for most of an afternoon, while Mara brought them bread and did not bring them the crock.

Most of them did not know what they wanted when they came. They knew that something had been done to them, or was about to be, by somebody they could not reach. They had tried the officer and the officer had sent them to the river house and the river house had sent them to Valora. They came to Elyas because he was nearer than Valora and because he would listen, and because other people had told them he would.

Some of them he could help. Most he could not. He could write a letter, and he did, until his hand ached. He could tell them which door to knock on next, and he did that too. He could not open any of the doors himself.

“Why do they keep coming?” Berent asked him once, when the hall had emptied at dusk.

Elyas was sitting on the bench where they had sat, with his elbows on his knees.

“Because I let them.”

Berent let the second pair through because he knew the first man.

Elyas was in the stable examining a sore-backed mare when Mara came to fetch him. She had flour on one sleeve and no expression he could safely mistake for patience.

“You have gentlemen in the garden.”

“Who?”

“Ask the gentleman at the gate.”

Berent stood beside four horses. He had allowed the visitors to keep their swords because one had served at the ford and another claimed a message from a neighbour. The last two had arrived as companions of the first. Now all four were under the pear tree, and one was telling a fifth man outside the gate to bring his horse in too.

Elyas stopped him.

“Leave it there.”

The man began to explain. Elyas turned to Berent.

“What did we agree?”

“The hall for callers. Weapons left.”

“Then do it.”

The old soldier under the tree stood up and said there was no need to make the boy apologise for recognising a friend.

“He is a knight,” Elyas said. “He can hear a correction.”

He could see that this made it worse. He let it stand.

Berent did not argue. He went red to the ears and turned and walked to the gate and stood in front of the fifth man’s horse, and said in a loud, level voice that anybody who wished to see the prince would leave his weapon at the gate as the lady of the house required, and that this applied to gentlemen who had served at the ford as much as to anybody. Then he went back to the pear tree and said the same thing to the four men sitting under it, and held out his hand.

The old soldier looked at him. Then he unbuckled his sword and gave it to him, hilt first.

“Well said, sir knight,” he said. It might have been mockery. Berent took the sword as if it had not been, and the others followed, one by one, and Berent carried the five swords to the stable and laid them on the bench inside the door, in a row, with their belts coiled beside them. Elyas watched him do it. Later, when the visitors were in the hall, he went to the stable and found Berent there, sitting on an upturned bucket beside the row of swords with his head in his hands.

“I knew him,” Berent said without looking up. “From the ford. He carried the wounded boy’s saddle. I thought—”

“I know what you thought.”

“It was your rule. The lady’s rule. I read it every day on the kitchen door.”

“Yes.”

“I’m sorry, sir.”

“You’ve mended it,” said Elyas. “Come in and eat.”

The fifth man had brought news of a detained cousin. The cousin had threatened an examiner, regretted it, and been taken to a house outside the local captain’s authority. There was no answer about where he would be heard.

One of the others began praising the Mound. Its men had recovered stolen cattle from a lord whose own servants would never testify against him. Elyas knew the case. The young man who had led that recovery had once needed his help to get a hearing himself.

“Then ask them,” Elyas said.

“We did. They say this is protection of the queen’s business.”

“Was she there?”

“No.”

“What did he threaten?”

The story changed. The examiner had been told that men could disappear on that road. The cousin had meant bandits. Everyone in the room insisted upon the meaning except the old soldier, who studied his boots.

Elyas wrote a request for the man’s place of detention and the charge. Berent carried it to the local captain. He returned before dinner with an answer: the captain had no power to produce the prisoner. The Mound’s men held him under a separate authority and would report directly to Valora.

Mara read the reply over Elyas’s shoulder.

“Will these men eat here?”

“I haven’t asked.”

“They’ve asked Nelle.”

He went to tell them that two could remain and the others must find lodging in the village. One laughed as if this were an invitation to protest. Elyas waited until he stopped.

The old soldier stayed.

His name was Vautrin. He had lost two fingers on the Scar and retained an ability to make a horse stand while other men argued with it. After dinner he helped Berent settle the animals. Elyas found them talking beside the stall, the younger man’s earlier embarrassment eased by useful work.

Vautrin waited until Berent had gone for water.

“You shouldn’t have to ask like this.”

“Like what?”

“For a man to be brought before somebody.”

“What should I do?”

“There are people who’d stand with you.”

Elyas set down the brush.

“Stand where?”

“If it came to it.”

“Say what you mean.”

Vautrin looked towards the door. Outside, water struck the bottom of a bucket.

“Twelve from my parish. More if the others heard you meant it.”

“No.”

“I haven’t called them.”

“Don’t.”

“They’d come willingly.”

“They would come to a house where my wife receives people who need help. You would make every one of those people answer for an army.”

“It wouldn’t need to fight.”

“Then keep it at home.”

Berent came in with the bucket. He heard the last words and stopped. Elyas took the bucket from him and poured the water into the trough.

“You can stay the night,” he told Vautrin. “In the morning you’ll go home. If your neighbour has an answer about his cousin, he can send it.”

Vautrin watched the horse drink.

“You’ll still hear him?”

“Yes.”

Mara was waiting upstairs.

“He offered men,” she said.

“Berent told you.”

“Berent asked where to put him.”

“In the room already made up.”

She sat on the edge of the bed. There was mending beside her, a stocking stretched over a smooth wooden shape. She had not begun it.

“Do you know what the others will say when they learn he slept here?”

“That I listened.”

“Did you?”

“I refused him.”

“I know. Will they?”

He took off his coat. The stable smell clung to it. Mara watched him fold it, put it on the chair, and move it because he had covered her work.

“I won’t send him into the road after dark.”

“There is an inn.”

“He has no money for it.”

“We could pay.”

“Yes.”

Neither moved. He knew she had given him a way out and that he was declining it because of something as difficult to explain as the man’s damaged hand resting on a horse’s neck.

“I want him to know he can ask without bringing twelve men to make the question matter.”

Mara picked up the stocking.

“Then tell him that before breakfast. And next time let me hear the offer before you promise the bed.”

Berent went to Vautrin’s parish three days later.

He carried the refusal in writing, at his own request. Elyas let him read it aloud before sealing it. The words were plain: no men were to assemble in Elyas’s name, no summons had been issued, no service would be accepted on that footing.

“Will that make it worse?” Berent asked.

“What part?”

“Giving it a paper.”

“It has already been spoken.”

“And if they laugh?”

“Let them.”

Berent returned the following evening wet and annoyed. Vautrin had received him; two neighbours had been present; one had laughed. They had promised not to raise men. Berent had made them repeat it before the village priest.

“Did you threaten them?”

“No.”

“Good.”

“I wanted to.”

Elyas took his horse. Berent looked relieved to surrender it and ashamed of the relief. In the kitchen Mara had left food near the fire. He sat down without removing his wet coat, and she made him get up again.

A month later Vautrin came back.

He came in the morning after the chapel bell, alone, on foot, with his sword already in his hand to give to Nelle at the gate. He had news of the detained cousin. The cousin had been moved from the tower to somewhere else, and nobody could say where, and his mother had written to Valora through the priest and had no answer.

Mara let him in. She gave him bread. She did not look at Elyas when she did it, and Elyas did not look at her, and Vautrin sat in the hall on the bench under the window with his maimed hand round the cup and told the prince what he knew.

He did not mention the twelve men from his parish. Neither did Elyas.

When he had gone, Mara came into the hall and sat down on the bench where he had sat.

“He’ll come again,” she said.

“Yes.”

“And the others. The ones who laughed at Berent.”

“Probably.”

“You know what some of them want.”

“I know what some of them want.” Elyas looked at the door. “I refused it. In writing. Before a priest. They know I refused it.”

“And they keep coming.”

“And I keep letting them.” He turned and looked at her. “Would you have me shut the gate?”

Mara was quiet for a long time. Outside, Nelle was sweeping the path where Vautrin had walked, and the sound of the broom came in through the open door, steady, like somebody breathing.

“No,” she said at last. “I’d have you know why you don’t.”

“I know why.”

“Then tell me. Not now. Some time when you’ve thought about it.” She stood up. “And tell me before you tell anybody else.”

Part Four — An Answer in the North

Chapter Twenty-One — Elsewhere

Harald sent for him in the spring, and did not send the steward. He sent a groom with a horse already saddled, which meant now.

Elyas found him in the gallery with the old glass, standing where they had stood five years before, the night Harald had asked him to go south and write that he was acting for the duke. Harald was thinner. The coat he wore had been altered twice. He did not turn when Elyas came in.

“They’re saying in Valora that you keep a court,” he said.

“I keep a hall. People sit in it.”

“They sit in it and complain about the queen’s officers, and the queen’s officers are there under a protection that I signed, that you helped me sign, that you stood beside me for in front of the whole of the duchy.” Harald turned. “Every man who walks out of your gate and says the prince listened to him is a man telling the queen that the Duke of Istovar can’t govern his own people without his brother holding their hands.”

“That isn’t what I tell them.”

“It doesn’t matter what you tell them. It matters what they say.” Harald put his hand on the cold glass. “I have Fayd’s people at the river and the Marshal’s at the ford and the queen’s officer in every court in the duchy, and the tolls pledged, and the mills in the schedule, and I have kept it together, Elyas. I have kept it together by agreeing. By being the duke who agrees. And you’re making it look as though there’s another duke down the road who doesn’t.”

Elyas stood beside him. Below, the court swam under the old glass.

“What would you have me do?”

“Stop.” Harald did not look at him. “Stop receiving them. Send them to me. Or to the officers. Or to Valora. Anywhere. Stop being the place they go.”

“And when they come to you, what will you do for them?”

Harald was silent.

“The Sallows,” Elyas said. “The widow on the Aubrac road. Ermengarde’s village and the ford. The man at the tower whose mother can’t find out where he’s been taken. If I send them to you, what will you do?”

“I’ll send them to the officers.”

“They’ve been to the officers.”

“Then I’ll send them to Valora.”

“They’ve been to Valora.”

“Then there’s nothing to be done!” Harald’s voice cracked across the gallery. A servant at the far end stopped and then went on, very quickly, out of the door. Harald lowered his voice. “There’s nothing to be done, Elyas. That’s what I’m trying to tell you. They’ve signed. We’ve all signed. The roads are good and the pay comes on the day and the bridge is standing. And for that the queen decides, and her officers decide, and we don’t. You can’t have the one without the other. You helped make the one. You can’t now pretend you don’t see the other.”

“I see it.”

“Then stop embarrassing the settlement.”

It was, Elyas thought afterwards, the only time in his life he had heard his brother speak with real force. In the whole of it Harald had not once asked what was happening to the people on the bench in the hall, or whether anything could be done for any of them, or whether they were right.

“I’ll think about it,” he said.

Harald looked at him with an expression Elyas could not read, and then turned back to the glass.

“You always say that,” he said, “and then you go and look.”

By the fifth spring after the gift, Elyas knew the palace passage by the patch where rain darkened the ceiling.

The roof had been repaired. The mark remained. He waited beneath it while a clerk went to discover whether the queen meant the ferry petition to be heard before or after the military business. Across the passage Tylan spoke to an officer about horses taken twice from the same village. He made the officer name the person who had authorised the second taking.

“They were sound animals, my lord.”

“That wasn’t in dispute.”

“The schedule—”

“Return them. Charge the feed to the officer who retained them after the correction.”

The man bowed. Elyas watched him leave with the unmistakable relief of having a disagreeable instruction which was at least an instruction.

Tylan came over.

“You could have sent that petition.”

“I did.”

“Yes. I saw the answer.”

“Then you know why I’m here.”

He was admitted while she was finishing something else.

Two men stood before the table with the samples of cloth, a stout lord from the western Scar country and a younger man in a coat too good for him, and between them on the table lay a wardship that had been in dispute for three years. Elyas knew the case. Everyone at court knew the case; it was the one people mentioned when they wanted an example of a thing that would never be settled. A girl of eleven, heiress to a mill and two farms, claimed by her dead father’s brother and by her dead mother’s cousin, each with a document, each document sound. The chancery had heard it twice. The queen’s court had heard it once and sent it back to the chancery.

Veralyn had the two documents in her hands. She read the last lines of each while Elyas stood by the door. Then she put them down.

“The child is to go to her mother’s cousin,” she said. “He has a wife and children of his own and lives within a day of the mill. The uncle lives at the other end of the kingdom and has never seen her. The mill is to be kept by the cousin for her until she is sixteen, and its accounts shown each year to the chancery. The uncle will be paid, from the mill, the sum he has spent in this suit, so that nobody can say he was put to cost for asking.” She looked at the stout lord. “Does that satisfy you?”

The man opened his mouth. He had been three years in the suit. He had, Elyas knew, borrowed against his expectations of the mill to pay for it.

“Your grace—”

“Good,” said Veralyn. “Then it’s done.” She turned to her clerk. “Write it. I’ll sign it tonight.”

The younger man bowed very low and went out almost running. The stout lord stood for a moment longer, looking at the documents on the table, and then bowed, and went out slowly, and Elyas saw his face as he passed the door and knew that the sum he had spent in the suit was not the sum he had borrowed, and that he was going home to the man who had lent it with a queen’s judgment and nothing else.

It was a good judgment. The child would be with people who knew her. The mill would be kept. The suit that could never be settled had been settled in the time it took to read two last lines. Elyas had seen her do it a dozen times now, and each time he had admired it, and each time he had watched somebody go out of the door behind it carrying the part she had not looked at.

“Elyas,” said the queen, turning. “The ferry.”

She listened to the ferry account standing among the samples of cloth.

A woman held a length of green against black. The queen looked at it, touched the edge, and asked Elyas whether the ferryman had refused the new terms or had been unable to understand them.

“He understood. He can’t keep the crossing under them.”

“Who can?”

“The company to which its future income has been promised.”

“Will they keep it?”

“Yes.”

“Then the crossing remains.”

“The family loses its livelihood.”

“What did Harald offer?”

“He referred them to the company.”

The queen put down the cloth. The green stone at her throat lay against the dark gown as though it belonged to it.

“What do you propose to put in Harald’s place?”

“I came about a ferry.”

She laughed. He knew the laugh and had liked it in other rooms.

“Yes. And next week you will come about a mill. What do you propose to put in his place?”

“A duke who hears them would help.”

“You know one?”

He felt Tylan move behind him. The woman holding the cloth was studying its edge with great attention.

“My brother is duke.”

“And I am Protector. Shall we go on reciting what we know?”

Elyas drew breath. He could have named an arrangement: a tenancy, compensation, a longer term. He had brought each before. She would ask who could be made to perform it and he would have to come back to the same company.

“Let the family remain under a lease they can afford.”

“Write the proposed terms. Tylan will have them examined.”

She turned to the woman with the cloth. The audience was over.

Tylan took him to a room overlooking the practice yard.

Two young men below were attempting the same movement. One improved when corrected; the other grew quicker at doing it wrongly. Tylan watched until the instructor separated them.

“There is a command on the southern approach,” he said. “Properly provisioned. Men who need somebody who knows the ground and can deal with the local houses.”

“How far south?”

He explained. Elyas knew the places. There was work there he could do, unfinished since the last incursion, and officers whose quarrels needed settling before another raid made them fatal.

“You’ve thought about this.”

“Yes.”

“Mara?”

“She could join you when the appointment is settled.”

“When?”

“After you’ve taken up the post and seen what you require.”

Elyas looked down at the yard. The less successful pupil had sat on a bench and was having his wrist examined.

“What happens to the people coming to my house?”

“They bring their requests through the appointed officers.”

“They have.”

“Then they appeal.”

“To the people whose settlement they dispute.”

“To the authority capable of changing it.”

“And if that authority won’t?”

Tylan turned from the window.

“You cannot maintain a second court because the first disappoints people.”

“I have no court.”

“You receive men. They leave saying what Elyas thinks. Some say what they hoped you’d think. Others say what suits them. You cannot correct all of them.”

“I can refuse the use they ask of me.”

“You can refuse it and still be used.”

Elyas knew. Hearing it from Tylan gave him the bitter satisfaction of being warned about the thing he had already tried to warn others of.

“The village with the horses,” he said. “You heard them.”

“They were right.”

“And if they refuse the road?”

“After we’ve returned what was wrongfully taken and paid what is due? On what ground?”

“It is their ground.”

“Every road is somebody’s ground.”

Below them the injured boy stood and tried the movement with his other hand. Tylan watched him, then looked back.

“Take the command. You can do good work there.”

“I have work at home.”

“Think before you answer.”

“I have.”

Tylan put the appointment aside. For a moment he looked older than he had when the conversation began.

“Then I’ll leave it open a little longer.”

He told Mara that night.

He told her everything, in the kitchen, while she scrubbed a pot: Harald in the gallery, the ferry, the queen’s laugh, the room above the practice yard. The command on the southern approach. The good men who needed somebody. The work unfinished since the last incursion. When he came to the part about her joining him when the appointment was settled, she stopped scrubbing.

“And you said?”

“I said I had work at home.”

She put the pot down in the water.

“You said no.”

“He’s leaving it open.”

“You said no, Elyas. Before you’d come home. Before you’d asked me.”

He had. He had not thought of it as deciding for her. He had thought of it as refusing something he did not want.

“Do you want to go?”

“I don’t know.” She dried her hands. “I might. I might want a house on the southern approach where nobody knows your name and nobody sits on my bench. I might want you to have men who need you and a thing to do that you can finish. I might want to sleep through the chapel bell for a year.” She looked at him. “I might also want to stay. I would like to have been asked.”

“I’m asking now.”

“Now you’ve already said no.”

He sat down at the table. She stood at the basin with the cloth in her hands. Upstairs, Nelle was moving about, putting the house to bed.

“What happens to them?” he said. “The bench. The Sallows. Ermengarde’s carts. The man in the tower.”

“Somebody else.”

“Who?”

Mara was quiet for a long moment.

“You asked Tylan that,” she said. “What did he say?”

“He said they’d go to the appointed officers.”

“And you thought that was no answer.”

“It isn’t.”

“No,” said Mara. “It isn’t.” She hung the cloth over the edge of the basin. “And yours isn’t either, love. I’ll stay and they can keep coming isn’t an answer. It’s a place to stand.” She came and sat opposite him. “I don’t want to go south. I think. I don’t know. But I want you to think about it as if it were a real thing, and not as if it were a trap Tylan set you. Because I think he meant it. I think he’s trying to save you.”

“From what?”

She looked at him steadily.

“From the thing Harald’s afraid of,” she said. “The thing you keep telling me you’ve refused.”

Chapter Twenty-Two — What the Matter Requires

The detained man’s mother wrote to Lyra because the priest told her to, and the priest told her to because he had written to Valora three times himself and had begun to think that his letters were being put on a shelf.

Her name was Sibille. She could not write; the priest had written for her, in a round, careful hand, and had added at the end in a smaller one that he would vouch for every word. Her son was called Anseau. He had been taken on the southern road in the winter for saying to a queen’s examiner that men could disappear on that road, which he had meant, she said, about robbers, because a man had disappeared on that road the year before and been found in a ditch in the spring. He had been held at the tower at the Lesser Ford for a week. Then he had been taken somewhere else. Since then nobody would tell her where he was, or what he was charged with, or whether he was alive.

He is a fool, the letter said, and he has a temper, and he says things. He does not do things. I would like to know where he is, so that I can send him a shirt.

Lyra read it at her table with the whetstone on the corner of the page. Below, Oudine’s treadle was going, steady as rain.

She knew the name. Elyas had written to her about it in the spring. Berent had carried a request to the local captain, and the captain had said that the man was held by the queen’s household under a separate authority and that he could not produce him. Wace had written to the Marshal’s office and been told it was the household’s business. That was as far as anybody had got.

She wrote to the chancery first, because that was the rule. Pellam’s clerk wrote back within the week that the chancery had no record of Anseau son of Hugon, of the Sallows road, in any court or prison under its authority, and that matters touching the queen’s household were not within its competence.

Then she went to Fayd.

He saw her in his room, with the underdone-pastry wall, which had faded in five years to something nearer the colour of old bread. He had her letter from Sibille on his desk before she had finished explaining. He read it through without comment.

“You want to know where he is.”

“His mother wants to know where he is. She wants to send him a shirt.”

“I understand.” Fayd put the letter down. “The household does not report to me.”

“It reports to the queen. You have the queen’s ear.”

“I have a great many people’s ears. It doesn’t mean they tell me things.” He leaned back. “But I could ask. I could put it to the household that a man has been held for five months on a charge nobody will name, and that his mother has written to the chancery, and that this is the kind of thing that makes people in the south uneasy at a time when we would rather they were not. I could very probably get an answer.”

“Then ask.”

“I would need to know more.”

She waited.

“The household took this man because it believed he was not alone,” said Fayd. “That is what the household does. It does not take men for saying foolish things to examiners; the examiner would have dealt with that himself. It takes men when it thinks the foolish thing is the edge of something else.” He looked at her. “If I go to the household and say release this man, they will ask me whether I know what the something else is. And if I say no, they will say, then how do you know he should be released? And they will be right.”

“And if you say yes?”

“Then I will have to tell them what I know.” He folded his hands. “You know the prince’s house. You know who goes there. You’ve had letters from the prince about half the men on that bench, and from his lady, and from Berent. If you could give me an account of who has been coming to that house, and what they’ve been saying, and to whom, I could take it to the household and say: here is the whole of it, there is nothing in it but a lot of angry farmers and a prince who is too kind to send them away. Let the man go. And I think they would.”

It was reasonable. She heard how reasonable it was. She could see him doing exactly what he said, walking into some room in the palace with her account under his arm and getting a foolish young man with a temper sent home to his mother. She could see the account itself, in her own hand, as she would write it: careful, fair, every name spelled correctly.

“No,” she said.

He did not seem surprised. He never did.

“May I ask why?”

“Because the matter is Anseau. Not the bench.” She put her hand flat on Sibille’s letter. “I can give you him. His name, his mother’s name, his father’s, his village. What he said and to whom and on which day, as she tells it. What the priest says of him. Where he was held and when he was moved. That’s what the matter requires. That’s what I’ll give.”

“It may not be enough.”

“Then it won’t be enough.”

“And he’ll stay where he is.”

She did not answer. Fayd looked at her.

“You understand,” he said, “that I am not the only person who can find out who goes to the prince’s house.”

“I understand that.”

“And that when somebody else finds out, they won’t be as careful as you.”

“I understand that too.”

He nodded slowly. Then he took a clean sheet and wrote on it, in his neat hand, what she dictated: Anseau son of Hugon; his mother Sibille, of the family of the Saulniers; his village; the date and place of his arrest; the words he had used; the priest’s name. When she had finished he read it back to her, and she corrected one date, and he corrected it.

“I will send this,” he said. “As it is. I will say that I have it from a person who knows the family and whom I trust. I will not say more, because you have not given me more.” He sanded it. “I don’t promise anything will come of it.”

“I know.”

“Good afternoon, Mistress Lyra.”

She went to Isaut’s wedding that week, because it had been fixed for months and because she had promised, and because she wanted, for one day, to be somewhere nobody would ask her for an account of anything.

Isaut was marrying a cooper from the river quarter, a large, shy man with enormous hands who had come into the shop for a pair of gloves two winters before and had needed, it turned out, eleven more pairs before he found the courage to say what he had actually come in for. Isaut’s mother wept through the whole of the service and then, at the feast, told everybody within reach that she had seen it coming from the third pair. Isaut’s brother came up from his company on leave in a coat with a lieutenant’s mark on it and was made to dance with every aunt. Lucan came with him, because they had been in the same intake, and danced with Lyra, and told her, very seriously, that Aline had gone to the weaver in the town at Michaelmas and had sent him a scarf she had made, and that it was the best scarf in the company, and that three officers had tried to buy it from him.

“Did you sell it?”

“No,” said Lucan, scandalised. “She made it.”

Lyra laughed, and danced, and drank the cooper’s cider, which was very strong, and at the end of the evening sat on the step of the glover’s shop with Isaut, who had taken off her shoes, and watched the cooper carry Isaut’s mother home on his back because she had declared that she could not walk another step and would sleep in the road.

“He’s kind,” said Lyra.

“He is.” Isaut rubbed her feet. “He’s kind and he’s large and he doesn’t talk. I’ve never been so happy in my life.” She leaned her head on Lyra’s shoulder. “You should marry somebody.”

“Who?”

“Somebody who doesn’t talk.”

“I’d talk for both of us.”

“That’s what I’m going to do,” said Isaut contentedly, and fell asleep there on the step, and Lyra sat with her until the cooper came back and carried her in too.

Nothing came of it that she could see.

The summer went on. Anseau was not released. Fayd did not send for her, and when she met him once in the passage outside the treasury he said only that he had sent the page and had had no answer, and that the household was slow about such things, and asked whether she had seen the new wall in the Marshal’s office, which was blue. She said she had not. He said it was very blue.

She heard from the priest at harvest.

He wrote to thank her for her trouble, which he said Sibille had heard of and was grateful for, though there was still no word of her son. And he wished Lyra to know, the priest wrote, because he thought she would want to, that in the last week two gentlemen from the queen’s household had come to the village and spent a day there, very courteously, speaking to Sibille and to himself and to several of the neighbours. They had asked who in the village had been to the prince’s house in the last year, and when, and with whom, and what had been said there. Sibille had told them she did not know. Several of the neighbours had told them a great deal. One of them had been Vautrin, who had lost two fingers on the Scar and did not like being asked questions by young men in dark coats, and had said so, and had been asked a good many more.

They were very civil, the priest wrote. They paid for their dinners. I thought you should know that the questions were asked. I do not know whether anything will come of it.

Lyra sat at her table with the letter under the whetstone. Oudine’s treadle had stopped. The street outside was quiet.

She had refused. She had given the matter what it required and nothing more, and she had been right to, and she would do it again. Anseau was still wherever he was. And the questions had been asked anyway, by somebody else, in the village, of the people she had been trying not to name, and Vautrin had answered some of them, and she had no idea what he had said.

She wrote to Elyas that night. She told him about the priest’s letter, and the two gentlemen from the household, and the questions. She did not tell him what she had refused Fayd, because it seemed, now, like something she had done for herself. She told him that the household knew who went to his house. She told him to be careful.

She read the warning again before sealing it. He would already know to be careful.

Chapter Twenty-Three — The Name to Use

Lyra’s letter about the priest came on a Tuesday. On the Thursday Elyas asked Berent to stay after supper.

Berent had been at his own holding most of the summer. The forty acres had turned out to have a spring on them that nobody had cleared in ten years, and the six families had turned out to be seven, because one of the sons had married and built a hut against the barn wall without asking anybody, and the steward who drank had turned out, when Berent finally sat him down and went through the rents with him, to drink less than he stole. Berent had dismissed him and was collecting the rents himself, and had discovered that this involved sitting in seven kitchens every quarter being told why the rent was late. He had come back to the pear-tree house for the harvest because Elyas had asked him, and because, Mara said, he wanted to sit in a kitchen where nobody owed him anything.

Mara cleared the table. She did not leave. Elyas had asked her not to.

“I want to send for Riarhin,” Elyas said.

Berent looked at him, and then at Mara, and then back.

“Where is she?”

“Ubral. Glenruan, or near it. Her mother’s people.” Elyas turned his cup on the table. “I want someone to go north and find her and ask her to come. Quietly.”

“Why quietly?”

It was Mara who answered.

“Because there are men from the queen’s household asking the neighbours who comes to this house. Because Lyra has written to say so. Because if a knight in the prince’s service rides north to fetch the woman who brought the queen her green stone, and nobody knows why, somebody will make up a reason.”

“Then why send at all?”

Elyas did not answer at once. He had been trying for a week to find the words, and had found several, and none of them had survived being said aloud in his own head.

“Because I’ve been right for too long,” he said at last. “About everything. Every man on that bench tells me I’m right. Harald tells me I’m wrong, but he tells me I’m wrong because he’s afraid, not because he’s thought about it. Tylan tells me I’m wrong and offers me a command in the south, and I can’t tell any more whether he’s right or whether he’s trying to get me out of the way. Mara tells me—”

“I tell you to think,” said Mara.

“Mara tells me to think. And I’ve thought, and I can’t find the place where I’m wrong, and that frightens me more than anything. Because I must be. Somewhere. Nobody’s right about everything for five years.” He looked up. “Riarhin will tell me where. She always has. She doesn’t care what I am. She doesn’t want anything from me. She’ll sit at that table and tell me I’m a fool, and why, and I’ll believe her.”

Berent was quiet.

“You’re asking me to go,” he said.

“I’m asking whether you will.”

“It’s harvest.”

“I know.”

“The families. The rents. There’s a man on my land who’s built a hut against the barn and I haven’t decided whether to make him pull it down.” Berent looked at his hands. “I’m not— I’m a very small landed man, sir. You said so yourself. If anybody asks me why I rode north, I can’t say I went on my own business, because I haven’t got any business in Ubral. And I can’t say I went on yours, because then it’s yours. And I can’t afford to lose what I’ve got.”

“I know.”

“So what do I say?”

Mara put her hand on the table between them.

“You say you went to fetch a friend,” she said. “Because that’s true. You say the prince asked you to ask her to visit. Because that’s true too. You don’t say anything else, because there isn’t anything else. And if anybody tells you that a knight fetching a friend is a conspiracy, you tell them to come and say it in my kitchen.”

Berent looked at her. Then he laughed, a short, startled sound, and rubbed his face with both hands.

“You’re both mad,” he said.

“Probably,” said Elyas.

“I’ll go.” Berent dropped his hands. “After the barley. I’ll go after the barley. And the man with the hut can keep it until I come back, and then I’ll decide.” He stood up. “I’ll need a horse that isn’t mine. Mine’s lame. And someone who knows the road north.”

“Lyra will know,” said Mara.

By harvest the carts coming into Valora had begun to queue before the south gate opened. Lyra met Berent in a saddler’s shop inside the walls because he needed a strap and she did not want to make his visit a matter of arriving at her office.

The saddler was arguing with him about the old fastening. It had failed because Berent had punched another hole too close to the edge. Berent maintained that it had held for a year. The man answered that a year was how long the leather had spent objecting.

Lyra waited beside a display of reins. A little boy under the counter was tying knots in offcuts. He offered her one. She accepted it, and the saddler told him to charge next time.

“Elyas wants Riarhin found,” Berent said when they were outside. “Quietly.”

“Does Mara know?”

“Yes.”

“Why me?”

“I may need a horse. Or a letter carried. He said you’d know somebody who didn’t need to know everything.”

The road ahead of the shop was crowded with a timber wagon. They stood against the wall while men shouted to one another, every instruction contradicted by somebody with a different view of the wheel.

“Is there a letter?” Lyra asked.

“A private request.”

“Don’t show it to me.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

She looked at him. He had grown used to being answered, she realised. The boy who had waited behind Elyas now expected to conduct his own business. He caught her expression and smiled faintly.

“I can keep a thing.”

“I know. It’s easier if fewer people give you theirs.”

She brought him a name that afternoon.

The carrier kept a northern stopping house and forwarded packets for traders she knew. He would not promise secrecy against every inquiry. He would provide a remount if one was available, send a message onward, and remember that Lyra had answered for the introduction.

Berent read the slip, repeated the name, and asked what he should say if the man had gone.

“His wife manages the house. Deal with her. Don’t ask a stranger at the door where he keeps his letters.”

“I had not planned to.”

“Good.”

He put the slip away. She gave him the little leather knot the saddler’s boy had made.

“For the strap,” she said.

“It won’t hold anything.”

“Then keep it off the saddle.”

He laughed. The laugh made her miss him before he had left.

Elyas wrote the last part of his letter after Mara went upstairs.

He had told her he was asking Riarhin to come. They had discussed the danger to Berent, the likelihood that Riarhin would refuse, and whether a woman who had finally gone to find her own people ought to be called back because Elyas could not make his brother listen.

“Ask her,” Mara had said. “Don’t make it sound like permission to stay away.”

Mara had written her own letter. She had written it at the kitchen table in the afternoon, quickly, and had not shown it to him, and he had not asked. He knew it began with the pear tree, because she had said so while she was sanding it: that it had borne well for two years and badly for one, and she had been advised to leave it alone, and had, with considerable difficulty. She had written Riarhin’s name on the outside with a firmness that made it look like an instruction. Then she had added a line at the bottom, in smaller writing, and folded it before he could see.

Now he sat with the lamp turned low and wrote what he had not said to his wife.

He began with Berent, so that she would hear him first. Then the agreements, each of which finished a different quarrel: the bridge against its tolls, the debt against the right to appoint the collector, the company kept in pay until the duke could afford his own. He wrote about Tylan and the horses taken twice, and how Tylan had returned the second taking and charged the officer for the feed, and then asked why the village should refuse the queen a road after she had heard it fairly. He meant both things. Remember that about him. He wrote about Veralyn laughing when he said he had come about a ferry, and asking again. He wrote that the men who came to him were some of them his friends and some of them delighted to make him do what they could not persuade their neighbours to do themselves, and that he was trying to tell them apart, and did not always manage it.

Then he came to the part he had been avoiding.

If I am killed, you will be told that I had time to stop it.

He read the words. They looked theatrical in his own hand. He nearly crossed them out. The offers had been made; he had refused them; he could still take Tylan’s command. If he did, the house would remain, Mara could follow, the petitioners could seek another door. He lifted the pen and went on.

Upstairs a board creaked. He waited, but Mara did not call. He wrote that the letter was no instruction to her. He asked Riarhin to hear her anger without deciding in advance how much of it was suitable.

Then he sat with the pen drying between his fingers.

He could tell Mara. He could go upstairs with the sheet and let her read it, endure her refusal to be managed even by a sentence promising not to manage her. Instead he wrote that it was not a request to make a war for him. He asked her to come if she could, and to bring whatever she had learned that might make him less certain he was right. I would rather be embarrassed before you than proved right before a grave. He signed it. Then, below his name, in the space that was left, he added a line about the pear tree. He wanted Riarhin at their table, disagreeing with him while Mara poured something and Berent tried not to grin. The wanting steadied his hand.

He folded the letter too soon. Ink transferred faintly to the facing sheet.

Berent left at first light.

Mara had wrapped food. Elyas handed over the sealed letter in the stable and said no address should be written outside it. Berent looked at it for a moment. Then he took off his riding coat and turned back the lining at the breast, and with the point of his knife unpicked two stitches of the seam, and slid the letter inside, and stitched it shut again with a needle and thread from his bag, badly, while Elyas held the lantern.

“Where did you learn that?”

“From the lady.” Berent bit the thread. “She says every man should be able to mend his own coat. She didn’t say what for.”

Mara’s letter he put in the pocket of the coat, over the mended place, where anybody could find it.

“If you can’t find her, send word.”

“I’ll find her.”

“Don’t turn a hope into an order you gave yourself.”

Berent nodded. He checked the new strap. The little useless knot Lyra had given him hung from his bag.

At the gate Mara asked whether his aunt knew how long he would be away. He said she knew enough to complain about it. Mara kissed his cheek, which embarrassed him more than anything Elyas had said.

They watched until he turned out of sight.

“He’ll be all right,” Elyas said.

Mara shut the gate.

“Come and eat.”

Chapter Twenty-Four — The Man Returned

Cedric brought the detained man back in a covered cart.

Elyas had been expecting an answer to his latest inquiry. He saw the royal escort at the gate and went out carrying the letter he had received that morning, intending to ask why one office had told him to apply to another which denied holding the man. Then Cedric got down and asked whether the household could provide a chair.

The prisoner’s ankle had swollen. He had turned it on a stair, Cedric said. The local surgeon could inspect it. There was a written account of the fall and a direction that the examination should be independent of the escort.

“Can he go home?” Elyas asked.

“Yes. After his undertaking is witnessed.”

“What undertaking?”

Cedric gave him the paper.

The man admitted threatening the examiner and promised to present himself if summoned. No admission of a wider conspiracy was required. His cousin had come with the escort and was standing beside the cart, too relieved to speak coherently.

Elyas read the terms aloud. The prisoner listened, asked whether the examiner must hear the apology in person, and looked down when Cedric said yes.

“I’d sooner pay him.”

“You may have to do that as well,” Cedric answered. “This is what lets you go home.”

They carried him into the house. His name was Anseau. He was younger than Elyas had imagined from the letters, perhaps twenty, with a sullen, frightened face and a bruise going yellow on one cheekbone that Cedric’s account did not mention. Mara brought a stool for the injured foot and sent Nelle for water. Cedric took his gloves off before helping adjust the cushion beneath the man’s calf. The gesture was careful and unshowy. Elyas found himself remembering a younger man with a newly marked face, furious at being made to hear what he had done.

Mara remembered him too. Elyas saw it when she straightened from the stool and found Cedric kneeling on the other side of it. She had held the basin in the physician’s room at the Whiterun while Elyas’s arm was washed. She looked at Cedric now the way she looked at everybody who came into her house hurt or bringing somebody hurt: quickly, from head to foot, to see what needed doing.

“You’ve been riding since before dawn,” she said.

Cedric looked up. “Since midnight, madam.”

“Then you’ll eat before you go. And your men.”

“There’s no need—”

“It isn’t a need. It’s bread.” She turned to Nelle. “The crock. For the escort.”

Cedric stood. For a moment he seemed not to know what to do with his hands. Then he bowed, as he might have bowed to the Marshal’s wife, and thanked her, and went out to tell his men.

The bruise on Anseau’s cheek was from the first week, he said, when he had been taken off the road. One of the men had hit him when he tried to run. After that nobody had hit him. They had fed him and given him a blanket and asked him questions.

“What questions?”

Anseau looked at Cedric’s back through the window, and then at Elyas.

“Who goes to your house, my lord.”

Elyas was very still.

“I said I’d never been. They said my cousin had. I said yes, once. They asked who else. I said I didn’t know. They asked again the next day. And the next.” He looked at his foot on the stool. “They weren’t unkind. They just kept asking. After a while I told them the names I knew. Everybody knows who goes to your house, my lord. It isn’t a secret. My cousin. Vautrin, from our parish. The carter from the Lesser Crossing. The widow on the Sallows road. I told them.” His voice cracked. “I’m sorry.”

“You have nothing to be sorry for.”

“I didn’t tell them anything that isn’t true.”

“I know.” Elyas put his hand on the young man’s shoulder. “Nobody who comes here does anything that isn’t known. That’s the point of a hall.”

He said it and believed it. He also went to the window, afterwards, and stood looking out at the escort eating Mara’s bread in his yard, and thought about a list of names written down somewhere in the queen’s household in Anseau’s words, beginning with my cousin and ending with the widow, and wondered what it looked like to somebody who had not sat on the bench.

After the witness had signed, the cousin went to arrange the journey home. Cedric remained at the table, checking that his copy contained the same wording as Elyas’s.

“You got him out,” Elyas said.

“The order released him.”

“Someone got the order.”

Cedric looked up.

“Yes.”

Elyas was uncertain whether thanks would be accepted as condescension. He offered them anyway. Cedric inclined his head and resumed reading.

“Your visitors will say it was your persistence,” he said.

“Was it?”

“It was useful.”

“What else was useful?”

“A man willing to admit what he said. An examiner willing to accept an apology. Someone finding where the first inquiry had gone.”

“And whose fault was that?”

Cedric folded the copy.

“An office had entered the man under his mother’s family name. Another searched under his father’s. Each answered the question it had received.”

“And who found it?”

“A page came to me from Master Fayd’s office at the beginning of the month, with both names on it. The man’s and his mother’s family. From a person who knew the family, it said.” Cedric folded his hands on the table. “I took it to the household’s clerk and we searched under the second name and there he was.”

It was an explanation Elyas could believe. That made the weeks harder to account for, rather than easier.

“Would he still be there if nobody had asked?”

“Someone did.”

They went outside. The escort had watered its horses and drawn them into the shade. One rider was letting a small boy examine the leather guard on his wrist. Cedric told him not to unbuckle it; the man obeyed without the least resentment.

They walked together to the gate. Cedric’s men were mounting. Beyond them, down the lane, a man was driving two cows towards the village and had stopped to watch the queen’s riders, and his cows had stopped with him.

“The men who held him,” Elyas said. “At the tower, and after. They weren’t yours.”

“No. The household’s.”

“Do you work with them?”

Cedric considered the question as though it had been asked in an examination.

“I work beside them. When Master Fayd asks it.” He looked at the man with the cows. “They’re good at some things. Better than we are. They can go into a lord’s hall where the lord’s own servants won’t speak, and come out with the cattle and the man who stole them. I’ve seen them do it. The west is quieter for them.”

“And at others?”

Cedric was silent for a moment.

“They answer to the queen,” he said. “Only to her. It makes them quick. It makes it difficult to find out where a man has been put.” He pulled on one glove. “I found this one because a page came with two names on it. If it had come with one I’d still be looking. So would his mother.”

“Does that trouble you?”

Cedric turned his head and looked at Elyas directly. The scar showed white under his jaw.

“Things trouble me when I can do something about them, Sir Elyas,” he said. “I found him. I brought him back. I wrote down how.” He pulled on the other glove. “What else would you have me do?”

Elyas did not have an answer.

“You’ve learned to command,” he said.

Cedric looked at the rider, then back at him.

“I’ve had work.”

“I didn’t mean—”

“I know what you meant.”

For a moment the old encounter stood between them so plainly that Elyas expected Cedric to name Whiterun. He did not. He asked instead whether Berent was still in Elyas’s service.

“Yes.”

“I haven’t seen him.”

“He’s away.”

Cedric gathered his reins. “Please remember me to him.”

Elyas promised to do so. The words felt ordinary until the escort had gone.

Inside, Mara was kneeling beside the returned man’s chair. She had found a bruise below the bandage and wanted the surgeon to see it before the ankle was covered again. The man’s cousin offered to fetch the surgeon faster. She gave him the name of the house and told him to come back with the man, not a promise.

Elyas folded Cedric’s copy into his own papers.

That night Mara asked him what Cedric had said about Berent.

“He asked whether he was still in my service.”

“And you said yes.”

“And he said he hadn’t seen him, and I said he was away.” Elyas was sitting on the edge of the bed pulling off his boots. “And he asked me to remember him to Berent.”

Mara was brushing her hair. She stopped.

“He didn’t ask after Berent,” she said. “He asked where he was.”

Elyas held the boot in his hands.

“He asked whether he was in my service. It’s an ordinary thing to ask.”

“It is,” said Mara. “And you told him Berent was away, and he didn’t ask where, and he didn’t ask when he’d be back, and he didn’t ask why. Would you not have asked? If you’d known a man at the ford, and his lord said he was away?”

He thought about it. He would have asked out of courtesy, even if he had not cared about the answer.

“Perhaps he didn’t want to seem curious.”

“Perhaps,” said Mara. She began brushing again. “Or perhaps he was being very careful not to seem to want to know. I don’t know which. Neither do you.”

Elyas put the boot down. Across the room the shutter moved in the wind and the pear tree scraped once against the wall, and was still.

In the morning he wrote to Lyra. He told her that Anseau was home, and how, and that the error had been in the names, and that a page had come from Fayd’s office with both of them on it and that Cedric had found the man that way. He told her Anseau had been asked who came to the house, and had told. He did not tell her what Mara had said about Berent. He thought about it, with the pen lifted, and decided that it was a thing Mara had noticed and he had not, and that he did not yet know what it meant, and that Lyra had enough to carry.

He sealed the letter and gave it to the carrier. Then he went out and looked at the pear tree for some time, and decided that Mara was right and it wanted cutting back, and that it could wait until Berent came home to hold the ladder.

Chapter Twenty-Five — After the Other Guests

Elyas’s letter about Anseau came on the same morning as Jaennis’s invitation, and Lyra read them in the wrong order.

She read the invitation first because it was on top. It was written in a secretary’s hand, with a line at the foot in Jaennis’s own: a supper, a few friends, a lady visiting the court from the south who wished to hear what arrangements could be made for travellers between Viterra and the southern cities, and who had been told that nobody in Valora knew more about carriers than Lyra. Do come. It has been too long. Wear the blue.

Lyra had not known that Jaennis knew she had a blue dress. Then she remembered that Oudine had made it, and that Oudine talked to Vaelrie, and she smiled, and put the invitation aside, and opened Elyas’s letter.

A page came from Fayd’s office at the beginning of the month, with both names on it.

She read that line several times.

She had given Fayd that page in the early summer. She had watched him write it in his neat hand and sand it and say that he would send it as it was. He had sent it. He had sent it at the beginning of the month, after harvest, after two gentlemen from the household had spent a day in Anseau’s village asking who went to the prince’s house, after Anseau himself had been asked the same question every day for a week until he answered it. Then the page had gone to Cedric, and Cedric had searched under the second name, and there the young man was.

She did not know that Fayd had held it. He might have sent it at once and the household might have sat on it all summer. The household was slow about such things; Fayd had said so. She did not know. She sat at her table with the letter in one hand and the whetstone in the other and knew only that the thing she had given, the small, exact, sufficient thing, had been used at a time and in a way she had not chosen, to close a matter after somebody else had got what they wanted from it.

She went to the chancery and did a day’s work badly. In the evening she came home and looked at the blue dress hanging behind the curtain, and thought about sitting in her room with Elyas’s letter, and thought about a lighted room and a table and people laughing, and a woman from the south who needed to know about carriers, which was a thing Lyra could tell her, exactly, and be thanked for.

She put on the blue.

The woman visiting from the south wanted to travel with three trunks and no men.

She explained this before the first dish was served. One trunk held clothes, another gifts, the third things she declined to identify because every man she had consulted had suggested leaving them behind. Lyra asked whether the difficulty was finding passage or being allowed to choose it.

“Both, apparently.”

Jaennis laughed and told the servant to put the third trunk on the table so they could at least inspect the cause of the trouble. The visitor said she had not brought it to supper. Maelis expressed disappointment with a gravity that lasted until the wine was poured.

There were seven of them at table. The windows stood open to the garden. A breeze moved the candles enough to send little shadows across the cloth, and someone had brought a branch of late flowers whose scent strengthened whenever an elbow brushed it. She was glad she had come.

The visitor asked about carriers who would keep their undertakings. Lyra named routes, explained where to change companies, and declined to recommend one man without first knowing whether he still travelled that way. Jaennis listened as though the details interested her. She asked which roads Lyra would choose herself.

“On whose purse?” Lyra said.

“Mine, if you make it sufficiently tempting.”

“Then I shall need another sheet.”

Vaelrie told a story about a journey with Ouen, whose principal grievance had been the state of a bird’s housing. Lyra laughed. There was affection in the account, especially where Vaelrie attempted to imitate his voice and failed because she could not keep herself from smiling.

“He took care of you?” Jaennis asked.

“He made everybody take care of me. I was exhausted by it.”

“Write and tell him.”

“I intend to.”

Jaennis reached across to move the flowers out of her way. Their hands touched. Neither made anything of it, and Lyra looked down at her plate before she seemed to be watching.

After the fish Maelis did the silks.

She did not call it anything. She said only that the visitor from the south had been promised an entertainment and that she was sorry it would be a small one, and took from her sleeve a knot of handkerchiefs, five or six of them, tied end to end, plain white linen. She drew them through her hand slowly, in the candlelight, and as they passed over her palm they went pale gold, and then rose, and then the deep warm yellow of the little pears on the sideboard. The knots stayed where they were. The colour moved round them like water round stones.

The visitor from the south put her hand to her mouth. Vaelrie clapped. Lyra watched Maelis’s face, which did not change at all, and then Maelis’s hands, which were steady and ordinary and had a little ink on the forefinger, like a clerk’s.

“How is it done?” the visitor asked.

“Patiently,” said Maelis.

She drew the silks through once more and they were white again. She put them away in her sleeve. Jaennis, across the table, touched one of the knots as it went past, lightly, the way a woman touches a seam to learn how it was sewn, and Maelis moved the silk out of her reach without appearing to. It was so small and so quick that Lyra would not have seen it if she had not been watching Maelis’s hands. She did not know what it meant. She thought, with the part of her that could not stop reading returns, that it had been a refusal, very courteously made. Then the wine came round again and Vaelrie began on another of Ouen’s grievances, and she forgot it.

She was enjoying herself. That was the truth of the evening, and she held to it afterwards. She had been in the chancery since dawn and worrying since breakfast and she was in a lighted room with good wine and flowers and women who laughed easily, and the visitor from the south was grateful to her, and nobody had asked her for anything she did not want to give. She laughed at Ouen’s grievances. She told the visitor about the carrier at the Aubrac landing who kept a notched stick and a strongbox, and the visitor laughed. Maelis said very little and watched everybody, and once, when Lyra looked up, was looking at her, with an expression Lyra could not read, and looked away.

The others left in stages. Maelis had an appointment elsewhere and complained that nobody had told the appointment what a pleasant evening it was interrupting. The southern visitor wanted to see a shawl Jaennis had described. Vaelrie took her to find it. A servant brought fresh water and removed the dishes.

Lyra rose with them, but Jaennis asked her to stay.

“Only a little. You have told everyone else how to get away.”

She sat again. The room was quieter with the table partly cleared. Jaennis loosened the fastening at her wrist and rubbed where it had pressed.

“How is Elyas?”

“Well.”

“And Mara? Does she find the house convenient now?”

“She has made it so.”

“That sounds like Mara.”

Lyra smiled. She could answer this. She could talk about a house and a garden without making the people in it available for use.

She told Jaennis about the kitchen door, and the rules pinned to it on the back of a letter from Harald, and Elyas’s signature at the bottom so that Nelle could show it to anybody who argued. Jaennis laughed until she had to put her cup down. She told her about the pear tree, which bore badly, and which Elyas kept threatening to cut and never did, and Mara’s opinion of this. She told her about Berent’s forty acres and the man who had built a hut against the barn without asking. Jaennis listened to all of it. She had a way of listening, Lyra had noticed long ago, that made you want to go on: she remembered the small things and asked about them later, as if they had mattered to her, and perhaps they had. Lyra had seen her do it with Vaelrie, and with the queen, and with a boy who held horses at the palace gate. She had never been on the other side of it for so long.

It was pleasant. The room was warm, and a woman who had known Elyas since he was a boy at Tarlington wanted to hear about his kitchen door.

Jaennis asked whether Elyas was happy there. Lyra said he had been away and had much on his mind. About the queen? Among other things, she answered, and knew she had said enough.

Jaennis’s expression softened. She leaned back, leaving room for Lyra to decline the next question.

“Has he sent for anyone who might reassure him?”

For a moment Lyra thought of Mara standing in the garden with wet hands, deciding whether to welcome an unexpected horse. Then she thought how tired they all were of guarding themselves against people they loved. Jaennis had given them this room once. The sister of the missing man had her house now; there had been a wedding, a child, so many things which required somebody to trust somebody else before every condition could be made safe.

“He’s sent Berent north,” she said.

Earlier in the evening, while Vaelrie was talking about Ouen’s bird, she had decided that if the talk came near this she would stop at Berent’s name and let it stand as a man visiting his land. She heard the sentence reach its end instead, and was glad of it.

Jaennis listened.

“To find Riarhin. Quietly. I gave him an introduction in case he needed a carrier.”

The words were a relief. Lyra heard the whole sentence with pleasure at having said it plainly. Jaennis thanked her for trusting her, and asked nothing more about it.

They spoke for a little longer. Lyra could not later remember all of it. She remembered laughing at something about the third trunk. She remembered asking whether the southern visitor would want a written account of the route.

At the door Jaennis touched her hand.

“I’m glad you stayed.”

“So am I.”

Outside, the air had cooled.

Lyra walked home past a bakery which was washing its floor. Water ran beneath the door and darkened the stones. A boy lifted a tray to let her pass. She thanked him and went on, turning the evening over with the contentment of having been good company.

At the next corner she stopped.

She had told Jaennis where Berent had gone.

The fact did not yet frighten her. She examined it as she examined a sentence which had become unclear at its end. There had been reasons for telling. Jaennis cared for Elyas. Riarhin was her friend. The northern journey was a request, not a rising. There was nothing in it which could not be explained.

She had intended not to tell.

That remained when the other reasons had been considered. She stood beside a shuttered shop until a man passing asked whether she needed help.

“No,” she said.

At home she took off the blue dress and hung it carefully. She lay awake until the room began to lighten, rehearsing the sentence before and the sentence after, unable to find the place at which she had changed her mind.

In the morning she wrote to Elyas.

She wrote it three times. The first version explained. The second apologised. She tore them both across. The third said only what she had said, to whom, and when, and that she had meant not to, and that she did not know why she had. She read it twice. It looked very small on the page, and very clear, like a sum that had come out wrong and could be checked by anybody.

She gave it to the carrier herself, at the south gate, and stood watching until his horse was out of sight.

Chapter Twenty-Six — The Correction

Elyas answered in four days.

Berent is carrying a private request to an old friend. I will answer for it if I am asked. Have you been questioned? Tell me if you have. Do not blame yourself for a journey I chose to send.

And beneath, in a different ink, as though he had come back to it:

I believe you.

Lyra read it at her table, lingering over the last line. He had answered her confession as a friend. She had wanted that. She read the line again, waiting for it to help.

She put it under the whetstone and went to the chancery.

The summons came on the fifth day, from neither the chancery nor Fayd: a small folded paper with the queen’s household seal on it, very neat, asking Mistress Lyra to attend upon an examiner of the household at the third hour, in a room in the east range, to assist with a matter touching the northern correspondence.

She had never been in the east range. It was the oldest part of the palace, low and thick-walled, and the rooms opened off a long passage lit by windows too high to see out of. The room she was shown into had a table and two chairs and a fire, and a clerk at a small desk in the corner, and a man behind the table who rose when she came in.
