He was about fifty, grey, with a pleasant, tired face and ink on his second finger. His name was Aimeric. He thanked her for coming. He asked whether she would like water. She said no, and then yes, and he poured it himself.

“This is not an examination of you,” he said. “I want to make that clear. The household has an entry concerning a journey to the north by a knight in the service of the prince of Istovar. I understand you may be able to help me make it accurate.”

“What does it say?”

He did not show it to her. He read from it.

“That on or about the first of the month Sir Berent, knight, of the prince’s household, departed northward into Ubral, bearing correspondence from the prince to persons there, for the purpose of seeking the countenance and support of a power outside the Protector’s peace in matters touching the prince’s following in Istovar.”

Lyra sat very still.

It was all true. Every word of it could be defended. Berent had gone north. He had carried a letter. Riarhin was in Ubral, and she was a power of a kind, and outside the Protector’s peace in the sense that she was outside anybody’s. And Elyas wanted her countenance. He had said so. He wanted her to come and sit at his table and tell him where he was wrong about the people on his bench.

It was all true, and it was a lie.

“That isn’t what it was,” she said.

Aimeric picked up his pen.

“Then tell me what it was.”

She told him.

She told him that Berent had gone to find a friend of the prince’s, an old friend, a woman who had served the queen and brought her a gift, and to ask her to visit. That it was a private request. That nothing in it touched the prince’s following, or the Protector’s peace, or anybody’s support for anything. That Elyas wanted an old friend to come and disagree with him, which was a thing he had always wanted, and which nobody who knew him would think strange.

Aimeric wrote it down. The clerk in the corner wrote it down too. When she stopped, Aimeric read back what he had written, carefully, and she corrected one word, and he corrected it.

Then he looked up.

“How do you know?”

She had known the question was coming. She had not known until it came how much it would weigh.

“That it was private,” he said. “That it was a request to visit. That it touched nothing else. The household has your name on a list of persons known to have been aware that Sir Berent was travelling. That is all it has. It does not have you as a person who knew what he carried.” He laid the pen down. “If you are to correct the entry, the household will want to know how you know what you are correcting it with. Did you read the letter?”

“No.”

“Did the prince tell you its contents?”

“No.”

“Then how do you know it was a private request to visit?”

The fire ticked. In the corner the clerk’s pen had stopped.

“Berent told me,” Lyra said. “When he came to Valora before he left. He needed a horse, perhaps, or a letter carried, on the road north. He asked me for the name of somebody who could help. I gave him a carrier at a northern stopping house. I asked him why. He said the prince wanted Riarhin found. Quietly.”

Aimeric wrote. She watched the word go down on the page. Quietly.

“And you asked him nothing else?”

“I asked him whether the prince’s wife knew.”

“And?”

“He said she did.”

Aimeric wrote that down too. Then he read it back to her, all of it, the carrier and the stopping house and the name of the carrier’s wife, which he asked for and which she gave because he asked and because it was on the page already in every way that mattered, and the word quietly, and the prince’s wife knowing.

She heard it read back in his tired, pleasant voice.

She looked again at the route she had given him.

“One more thing,” said Aimeric.

She was already standing. He stood too, out of courtesy.

“The household has a note that the matter of Sir Berent’s journey was first spoken of, outside the prince’s house, at a supper given by the dowager nine days ago. By you. After the other guests had gone.” He looked at her with what seemed to be real curiosity. “You had not told the chancellor. You had not told Master Fayd, with whom I understand you do a good deal of business. You had not, so far as the household knows, told anybody at all. And then you told the dowager, at her table, in the evening.” He paused. “I am not asking in order to reproach you. I only wondered why.”

Lyra opened her mouth.

She had an answer. She had had it for nine days. Jaennis cared for Elyas; Jaennis had known him since Tarlington; Jaennis had asked, kindly, whether he had sent for anyone who might reassure him, and it had seemed absurd to keep a friend’s kindness at arm’s length because other people had made them all so careful. She had said it to herself on the walk home and in the night and every morning since. It was a good answer.

She heard herself about to give it, and stopped, because it was not an answer to the question he had asked. He had not asked what reasons there had been for telling. He had asked why she had told. And she did not know. She knew that she had decided at the table, while Vaelrie talked about Ouen’s bird, not to say it. She knew that she had said it. She did not know what had happened in between, except that she had been glad.

“I wanted to,” she said.

Aimeric waited.

“That’s all I can tell you. I had decided not to, and then I wanted to, very much, and I did.” She looked at him. “I’ve never done that before. Not about anything that mattered.”

He looked at her. Then he nodded, as if she had told him something ordinary, and wrote a short line, and did not read it back.

She went to Pellam first.

He was eating his dinner at his desk, a piece of cold fowl on a board, and he listened to her with the knife in his hand and did not eat any more of it. When she had finished he put the knife down.

“You corrected it.”

“Yes.”

“And the correction put you on the road with the carrier and gave them quietly and the prince’s wife.”

“Yes.”

Pellam took off his spectacles and rubbed the bridge of his nose. He looked, she thought, older than she had ever seen him.

“Would you have left it standing? A power outside the Protector’s peace?”

“No.”

“Then you did the only thing you could.” He put the spectacles back on. “It was right. It will be used against him. I’m sorry.” He drew a sheet towards him. “Write it down for me. What you told the examiner. Every word, as near as you can. It’ll go in the chancery’s books with today’s date, so that if the household’s entry is ever tidied, there will be another place where somebody can see what was said.”

“Duplicate custody,” said Lyra.

“You taught me the phrase.” He pushed the ink towards her. “Write.”

She wrote. It took an hour. When she had finished he read it through, and signed it, and had it entered while she watched, and then told her to go home and eat something, in a voice that did not allow for argument.

She walked home the long way, past the glover’s shop, without going in.

At her table she took out Elyas’s letter and read I believe you again, and then she took a clean sheet and wrote to him. She told him about the east range, and Aimeric, and the entry, and the words in it. She told him that she had corrected it, and how, and what the correction had cost: that the household now had from her the carrier and the stopping house and the word quietly and the fact that Mara had known. She said she was sorry. She crossed that out, because it was not useful. She wrote instead that he should expect to be asked, soon, and that he should think now about what he would say about Mara, because they would ask about Mara.

Then she sat with the pen in her hand.

I wanted to. She had said it aloud to a stranger in a room with a fire, and it had sounded, said aloud, like the truest thing she had said all morning and also like something that did not belong to her. She turned it over. It did not change. She tried to find, in her memory of the evening, the moment when not wanting to had become wanting to, the way she would look for the moment in a return where one figure became another. There was no such moment. There was the table and the flowers and Vaelrie’s bird and Jaennis’s face, kind, attentive, leaving room for her to decline, and then there was the sentence, whole, and the gladness.

At the bottom of the letter to Elyas she wrote, very small, I don’t know why I told her. She sanded it, sealed it, and took it to the carrier. That night she did not sleep.

Chapter Twenty-Seven — The Seal on the Warrant

The warning reached him while he was sawing a dead branch from the tree.

Mara held the ladder. He had meant to wait for Berent. Then the tree had dropped a limb across the path in a wind on the Sunday and Nelle had tripped over it carrying the milk, and he had gone for the saw. He had insisted he could manage without her and then stepped onto a rung which moved. She came back without comment. Now he stood with the saw against a branch too thick for the easy cut he had promised.

Nelle brought the letter into the garden. He read it on the ladder because the sap had made his hands sticky and he did not want to climb down with the saw loose.

“Lyra?” Mara asked.

“Yes.”

He read it again. Mara reached up for the saw.

“Come down.”

He answered before dinner. Berent carried a private request to an old friend. He would answer for it if asked. He asked Lyra whether anyone had already questioned her and added that she should not blame herself for a journey he had chosen to send.

Mara read the reply.

“You haven’t answered what troubles her.”

“She said something she meant not to say.”

“She knows that.”

“What should I write?”

“That you believe her.”

He added it.

Her second letter came five days after.

He read it at the kitchen table with Mara beside him, and when he had finished he passed it to her without a word and went and stood at the door and looked at the garden. The branch he had cut lay on the path where it had come down, split along its length. He had meant to cut it smaller.

Behind him Mara read the letter through twice.

“Quietly,” she said.

“Yes.”

“And that I knew.”

“Yes.”

She put the letter down on the table and smoothed it flat with both hands, from the middle to the edges, the way she smoothed a sheet on a bed.

“She says to think now about what you’ll say about me.”

“I’ll say you knew I’d asked an old friend to visit. Because you did.”

“And that I didn’t know what you’d written.”

He turned round. She was looking at him steadily.

“I didn’t read it,” she said. “You didn’t show me. Berent sewed it into his coat in the stable with you holding the lantern and I was in the kitchen. I know there was a part of it you didn’t tell me. I’ve known since the night you wrote it. I heard you sitting up.” She folded her hands on the letter. “I’m not angry. Or I am, but not about that. I’m telling you so that when they ask, you don’t try to protect me by saying I knew everything. I didn’t. Say so.”

“Mara—”

“Say so, Elyas.”

He came back to the table and sat down opposite her.

“What else,” he said.

She was already thinking. He could see it: the same look she had worn at the station, going down a row of beds, deciding who could wait.

“The refusal,” she said. “The paper Berent took to Vautrin’s parish. The one the priest witnessed. Where is it?”

“In the chest. My copy.”

“And the priest’s note?”

“With it.”

“Then tonight I’m going to copy them both. Fair. And the list of everyone who’s sat on that bench since the spring, and what each of them asked for, and what you told them. Everything you’ve told me. Everything I’ve seen.” She stood up. “And I’m going to give the copies to Nelle, and Nelle is going to take them to her sister’s in the village tomorrow and put them under the floor where her sister keeps the money she doesn’t tell her husband about. So that when they come and take the papers out of this house, and they will, there’ll be another set they didn’t take.”

“That’s what Lyra always says.”

“I know,” said Mara. “I’ve been listening.”

A note came from Tylan on the seventh day.

It was very short, in his own hand, unsealed, carried by a young officer who would not stay to eat. The southern command is still open. It will not be open after the end of the month. T.

Elyas read it at the gate while the officer turned his horse. He read it again in the hall, sitting on the bench under the window where the petitioners sat, with the house quiet around him and Mara upstairs copying by the last of the light.

He could go. He could ride to Valora tomorrow and tell Tylan yes, and be on the southern approach by the new moon with good men and a thing to do, and send for Mara when the appointment was settled. The bench would empty. The men who came to it would go to the appointed officers, and be sent to the river house, and be sent to Valora, and some of them would get an answer and most would not, and none of them would be his to answer for. Mara had said it: Tylan was trying to save him.

He thought about Ermengarde’s cousin with his neighbour’s wool. About Anseau with his foot on the stool, saying I told them the names I knew. About the widow on the Sallows road who had asked only whether the army was a good life.

He thought about the letter to Riarhin, and the sentence in it that Mara had not read. If I am arrested, I intend to answer in my own name and insist that those accused of following me be heard separately.

He took the note upstairs and gave it to Mara. She read it by the candle and gave it back to him, and did not say anything, and went on copying.

He did not answer it.

That night, in the dark, he told her.

She had asked him in the spring, in the hall, on the bench where Vautrin had sat: tell me why you don’t shut the gate. Not now. Some time when you’ve thought about it. And tell me before you tell anybody else. He had thought about it all summer. He had not found the words until the night Tylan’s note came, lying beside her in the dark with the shutter open a crack and the pear tree moving outside it.

“When I was at the Scar,” he said, “writing the returns. The ones Cedric carried back. There were seven men I still couldn’t account for. I sat up three nights. You came and took the pen away.”

“I remember.”

“The clerks wanted presumed dead. It would have closed the returns. The widows would have had their pensions a month sooner. I wouldn’t write it.” He looked at the ceiling. “I wouldn’t write it because I didn’t know, and I wasn’t going to put my name to a thing I didn’t know about seven men just because it would be tidier. And some of the widows hated me for it. I know they did. They’d have had the money.”

Mara said nothing. He felt her turn her head on the pillow.

“That’s what the gate is,” he said. “A man comes to it and he says, this has been done to me. And I don’t know whether he’s right. Mostly I can’t help. Sometimes he’s a fool, or worse, and he wants me to be the thing he can’t be himself. I know that. But if I shut the gate, I’m writing presumed beside his name. I’m saying: he was dealt with somewhere, by somebody, properly, and it’s closed. And I don’t know that. Nobody knows that. The officer doesn’t know it and the river house doesn’t know it and Valora doesn’t know it, because they’ve all sent him to each other.” He was quiet for a moment. “I’m the last place he can come where somebody will write down not accounted for. That’s all. That’s the whole of it. It isn’t brave and it isn’t wise. I just won’t write the other thing.”

Mara did not answer. The pear tree scraped once against the wall.

“Thank you,” she said at last.

“For what?”

“For telling me first.” She found his hand under the blanket. “It’s a terrible reason, love. It’s going to get you killed.”

“I know.”

“I know you know.” Her hand tightened. “It’s still the reason I married you. I just didn’t know that was what it was called.”

Cedric came nine days after the first letter.

Elyas saw him from the window before the knock. There were more men this time, and no cart. Two remained at the gate. Another went towards the stable and was stopped by Nelle, who told him the mare kicked. He waited for Cedric’s gesture before entering.

Mara was in the little room beside the hall. She came out wiping her hands on a cloth. Elyas put the letter he had been reading on the table and opened the door himself.

“Cedric.”

“Sir Elyas.”

The form was correct and sufficient. Cedric took out a folded warrant.

“You are required to accompany me.”

“Under whose authority?” Mara asked.

He named it and showed her the seal. She leaned forward to inspect it. Elyas watched the movement with an absurd wish that she would finish wiping her hands before touching the paper.

“What is alleged?” he asked.

Cedric read. Unauthorised correspondence, withholding of information concerning the assembly of men owing service elsewhere, acts against the Protector’s peace. He did not claim an army had marched. Elyas heard the events of the past weeks fitted together without the refusals that lay between them.

“I declined the men.”

“You can give that answer at examination.”

“I am giving it now.”

“I will record that you did.”

Mara asked where he was being taken. Cedric named the first lodging and said onward conveyance would be under separate instruction. She asked which papers would be taken from the house.

“Those relating to the matters in the warrant.”

“Who decides?”

“I do.”

“Then you will list them.”

“Yes.”

“Every one. Before they leave the house. And give me a copy with your name on it.”

Cedric looked at her. It was the look Elyas had seen on him in the yard, when she had given him bread: as though he had been handed something he did not know where to put.

“You will have it, madam,” he said.

The search took two hours.

The men were careful. One apologised when he displaced a jar. Another asked Nelle to identify household accounts before setting them aside. Their courtesy made the house strange. Elyas stood beside his own table while strangers decided which of its contents concerned him privately and which concerned the Crown.

Cedric found the copy of the refusal sent to Vautrin’s parish. He read it and placed it with the papers to be taken.

“That goes with the accusation,” Elyas said.

“It goes with the examination.”

“Keep the witnesses with it.”

“They are named.”

Mara looked at him sharply. Elyas knew why. The paper which proved he had refused men also told the Crown where some of the offer had begun.

He had known that when he signed it. Knowing did not prevent the small sick movement in his stomach when Cedric put it into the case.

Mara followed the searchers from room to room with Nelle beside her. She did not speak to them except to name things. That is the household account. That is a letter from my cousin about a marriage. That is the prince’s copy of the protection of Istovar, you may take it, there are others. Nelle carried a slate and wrote down each thing as it went into the case, in her slow, square letters, and when Cedric’s clerk made his list Mara stood beside him and read it against Nelle’s slate, item by item. They agreed. She took the copy of the list when it was given her, and read it through once more, and folded it, and put it inside her bodice, and did not say thank you.

He was allowed to change his coat.

Mara came upstairs with him. For a little while neither spoke. He put one arm into the wrong sleeve and swore. She took the coat from him and held it while he began again.

“Don’t let them make you answer for everybody,” she said.

“I won’t.”

“You say that when you mean you will answer for them yourself.”

He turned towards her. Downstairs a chair moved. Somebody asked Nelle for string.

“I have refused what they say I meant to do.”

“I know.”

“There are witnesses.”

“I know.”

She straightened his collar, then stopped with her hands still on it.

“Come home.”

He put his arms round her, and she held on to him. When they drew apart she was angry, and he was grateful for that because he could recognise her in it.

“I’ll send where I’m taken.”

“They had better.”

At the gate Cedric accepted Elyas’s sword.

A neighbour had come out into the road. He removed his cap but said nothing. Elyas thought of asking him to look in on Mara, then saw Nelle standing beside her and understood that she did not require a new guardian in front of her own house.

He mounted. His hands were free. Cedric rode beside him, the sword secured with the other things being conveyed.

They passed the cut branch still lying on the garden path. It had caught in the upper growth and split when he finally drew it down. He had meant to saw it smaller.

Part Five — What Can Be Said

Chapter Twenty-Eight — The Earlier Sheet

Lyra learned of the arrest from a man who wanted her to change an address.

He had carried a petition to Elyas’s house, found soldiers, and been told to take his business elsewhere. He now wanted the name of the officer to whom it should go. He had come to Lyra because the original direction was in her hand.

She made him sit. He had been walking since morning and would have continued standing if she had not moved the chair beneath him.

“What did they take?”

“I don’t know.”

“Was Mara there?”

“His lady? Yes.”

“Did she speak to you?”

“No.”

She poured water. Her hand shook when she put the jug down, which annoyed her so sharply that for a moment she could think of nothing else.

The man still held his petition. She took it from him, read the first line, and discovered she had read it before.

“The mill wheel,” she said.

“Yes.”

She wrote a new direction. She did not send him to Fayd. There was an officer responsible for the disputed watercourse, and the man’s claim could be heard without making his visit to Elyas the reason for hearing it. She explained where to go and what answer to ask for. He thanked her with relief which made her want to tell him to leave quickly.

She waited until he had gone before shutting the door.

Then she sat down and wrote to Mara.

She did not know what to write. She wrote that she had heard. She wrote that she would find out where he had been taken and under what authority and what was alleged, and that she would find out who had the refusal and make sure it was kept with whatever else they had, and that anything Mara needed from Valora she should ask for. She wrote that she was sorry. She did not cross it out this time. She wrote that she was trying to find out what had been used.

She read it over and it seemed to her the letter of a clerk. She could not make it anything else. She sent it.

Pellam received her at the end of the day.

He had the first notice. He also had instructions to preserve documents relating to Elyas’s visitors. Lyra asked whether the refusal of service had been received with the other papers.

“Which refusal?”

She described it. Berent had delivered it. A priest had heard the men promise not to assemble.

“How do you know?” Pellam asked.

“Elyas wrote to me.”

“Bring the letter.”

“It contains other business.”

“Then identify the passage. The original must remain available.”

It was exactly what she had asked of others. She went home to fetch it.

On the stair the seamstress asked whether she had eaten. Lyra said yes, then came back down and admitted she had not. They ate bread and the remains of a stew in the workroom. A boy’s new coat lay across the table with its sleeves empty and upturned. Lyra kept her letter away from the grease.

“Will they let him go?” the woman asked.

“I don’t know.”

“People are saying he had men.”

“People offered him men.”

“Then he had the offer.”

“Yes.”

The seamstress nodded as though this were a distinction she could use, and went on eating.

Mara’s answer came on the fourth day.

It was short. It was in Mara’s hand, which Lyra had seen on a hundred notes about bread and linen and a sick child in the next lane, and it was steadier than any of them.

He is at the first lodging. Then Valora. They took the refusal and the priest’s note and a great many other things and I have a list of them. I have copies of what matters. I don’t need anything from Valora except him.

You say you are trying to find out what was used. What use are you, Lyra, if you couldn’t find out before?

M.

Lyra read it standing at the window. Below, in the street, Oudine’s boy was sweeping the step, badly, raising more dust than he moved. She watched him until he had finished, and then she read the letter again.

It was fair. She tried to find the place where it was unfair and could not. She had spent five years finding out things before they happened to people. She had found the road in the assessments and the clause for the Sallows and the man at the Aubrac mill, and she had sat at Jaennis’s table in a blue dress and said the one sentence she had meant not to say, and she had not found out, before or since, why.

She did not answer. She could not think of anything to write that would not be a request to be forgiven.

She put the letter in the drawer where she kept things that had taught her something, on top of Cedric’s report in two halves, and went to see Pellam.

Fayd found her the following morning.

He did not summon her. He came to the room where she had been allowed to compare the dates of Elyas’s letters with the receiving entries, took the empty chair, and asked whether she needed another copy made.

“I need the first kept with the later one.”

“It will be.”

“Where he refuses.”

“I have read it.”

“Then you know.”

“I know he refused that offer. I know he continued to receive the men who made it.”

She turned the sheet towards him.

“He told them not to assemble.”

“Yes.”

There was no pleasure in his agreement. She could not make him deny the thing she had proof of, and without a denial she had nothing to strike against.

“What do you want from me?”

“A full account of your part in the northern communication.”

“I gave Berent an introduction to a carrier.”

“Why?”

“So he could find a horse or forward a packet if he needed to.”

“To whom?”

“You know whom he was looking for.”

“Tell me anyway.”

She folded her hands because she wanted to lift the papers and leave.

“Riarhin.”

“Did you read what he carried?”

“No.”

“Did you ask what it said?”

“No.”

Fayd waited. She could hear a clerk sharpening a pen in the adjoining room, the little scratch repeated three times.

“I asked whether Mara knew,” Lyra said. “He said she did.”

“That mattered to you.”

“Yes.”

“Did it tell you the communication was authorised?”

“It told me the wife in his house knew he had asked an old friend to visit.”

He looked at her for a moment, then made a note.

She wanted to seize the page and see whether he had written old friend.

When Elyas was brought to Valora she went to the gate of the building where he was held.

The building was a house near the east range that had belonged to a family who had left the city in the war, and the household had taken it, and put a guard at the door and bars on the lower windows, and it looked, from the street, like any other house with its shutters closed. Lyra stood across the lane from it for some time before she went to the door. She had a request written out, on the chancery’s paper, over Pellam’s mark, for leave to speak with the prisoner on the matter of dates.

The guard read it and gave it back.

“Household matter, mistress.”

“It’s from the chancellor.”

“I can see that. It’s still a household matter.” He repeated it slowly. “Apply to the household.”

“Where?”

He told her. It was the east range, the room where Aimeric had read her the entry. She went there. A clerk took the request and entered it in a book and said it would be considered. She asked when. He said it would be considered.

She went back to the lane in the evening and stood across from the house again, not for any reason. There was a light in one of the upper windows, very high up, a small square of yellow. She did not know whether it was his. She stood there until the guard at the door began to look at her, and then she went home.

The request was never answered.

The invitation to Jaennis’s supper lay at home beneath a book.

Lyra took it out that evening. She read the ordinary words again: the visitor, the arrangements for travel, the pleasure of her company. She remembered Maelis laughing before she left, the flowers, Vaelrie’s story about her brother. None of those things had been false.

She wrote a separate account of the conversation after the others departed. She described what she had intended to say and what she had said. At the end she began a sentence about a working, then stopped.

She did not know.

She could ask who had poured the wine. She could ask whether anyone else had felt ill. She could discover who had seen Maelis leave. She began with those questions the next day, while continuing to put the date of Elyas’s refusal before every office which would receive it.

The lady from the south with the three trunks was still in Valora, waiting for a carrier Lyra had recommended. Lyra found her at her lodging, in the middle of repacking the third trunk, which contained, it turned out, a great many small wrapped things that clinked, and which she still declined to identify.

“The supper?” She sat back on her heels. “Oh, it was lovely. I did so enjoy it. You were very helpful about the carriers. I’ve taken the man you said.”

“Did you feel well? Afterwards?”

The lady looked at her with surprise.

“Perfectly. A little too much wine. The dowager’s wine is very good.” She began wrapping a small thing in a stocking. “Why?”

“I wondered. I had a headache.”

“I didn’t. I slept like a child.” She tucked the stocking into a corner. “Vaelrie took me to find the shawl, you know, the one the dowager had told me about. It took an age. We couldn’t find it anywhere, and then when we came back down it was on the chair in the hall all along, and you’d gone.” She laughed. “The dowager said she must have sent a servant for it and forgotten. She said she’d been so taken up with talking to you.”

Lyra thanked her. She went down the stair and out into the street and walked for some time without noticing where. It meant nothing. A shawl on a chair. A servant sent and forgotten. It was the kind of thing that happened in every house with more than one servant in it, every day.

She wrote it down that night, with the date, on a separate sheet, under the heading Things that mean nothing. It was the first thing on the sheet.

Chapter Twenty-Nine — An Answer in His Own Name

Cedric let him correct the first account.

The examination took place at the first lodging, a manor house two days’ ride towards Valora that had been taken for the queen’s household under a protection and still had the previous owner’s hunting prints on the walls. The room had a hearth large enough to warm it badly. Elyas sat on one side of a table, Cedric on the other, a clerk between them at the end. The clerk had sand, spare sheets, and a knife whose handle was polished by use. Elyas watched him lay them out because there was little else to look at without appearing to wait upon Cedric.

“You summoned men to your house.”

“No.”

“They came at your invitation.”

“Some came after asking whether I would receive them. Some came without asking.”

“Which were invited?”

“For what purpose?”

Cedric glanced at the clerk.

“Keep the question as asked. Then his answer.”

Elyas leaned forward.

“If you ask whether I invited men to speak, the answer is yes. If you ask whether I invited men to serve, the answer is no.”

The clerk wrote. Cedric waited until he had finished.

“Did Vautrin offer service?”

“Yes.”

“You refused.”

“Yes.”

“He remained overnight.”

“He had nowhere to sleep.”

“There is an inn in the village.”

Elyas remembered Mara giving him the same answer and the shame of being unable to explain to either of them why it had not settled the matter.

“He slept in my house. I sent him home in the morning.”

“With a summons to return?”

“With leave to send news of the man whose detention brought him there.”

Cedric nodded to the clerk.

“Alter that passage.”

He was scrupulous. Elyas had expected that and was still unprepared for the extent of it. Cedric went through the year a month at a time. He asked who had come to the house in each month and on what business, and when Elyas named a man Cedric asked whether he had come alone, and armed, and whether he had left his weapon at the gate, and whether he had eaten, and slept, and where. He asked about the rules on the kitchen door and had the clerk write them down in Mara’s words, which Elyas gave him because he could not see how not to. He asked about Berent letting the armed men through and Elyas’s correction of it, and wrote that down too, and when Elyas said He mended it, Cedric said, “Yes. I have that from the man who gave up his sword,” and went on.

He asked about the northern journey last.

“You sent Sir Berent north.”

“I asked him to go.”

“To find a woman called Riarhin.”

“Yes.”

“For what purpose?”

“To ask her to visit.”

“The household has it from another witness that he was to find her quietly.”

“Yes.”

“Why quietly?”

“Because men from the queen’s household were asking my neighbours who came to my house,” Elyas said, “and I did not want them asking who came to my house from the north.”

The clerk’s pen stopped. Cedric looked at it, and then at Elyas.

“Write it,” he said. “As he said it.”

The clerk wrote it.

“And your wife knew.”

“She knew I had asked an old friend to visit. She wrote to her herself, about our pear tree. She did not know what I wrote.” Elyas held Cedric’s eyes. “Put that down exactly. She did not read my letter. I did not show it to her. There is a part of it she has never seen.”

Cedric looked at him for a moment longer than the question required. Then he turned to the clerk.

“Exactly,” he said.

The meal was brought after the first sitting. A piece of boiled meat, bread, a bowl of vegetables still warm at the centre. Elyas ate because the morning had left him hungry. He disliked discovering how readily his body accepted a routine his mind refused.

From the next room came a cough and the sound of someone moving a bench. He had not seen the other prisoner. He tried to judge age from the cough, then stopped. The man could be waiting over a theft, a debt, a quarrel with a neighbour. He listened for another cough.

Cedric returned with the amended account.

“You may read it before the afternoon.”

“Where is Mara?”

“At home when the last message left.”

“Can I write?”

“Yes. Your letter will be read.”

“By you?”

“By the officer responsible.”

Elyas looked at him. “Will you let her know where I am?”

“She has been given the lodging.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

Cedric set down the paper.

“I will have the acknowledgment brought.”

It came the next morning: a scrap in Mara’s hand, carried back by the rider who had taken the notice of lodging to the house. I have it. I have the list. The mare is lame again. M. Cedric brought it himself and laid it on the table in front of Elyas without comment and went out, and Elyas sat with it, reading the four short lines over and over as though they were a much longer letter.

On the second day he brought a fact Elyas had not supplied.

A man who had visited the house in the spring had purchased additional harness at the market in Aubrac the week after. Two of his relations had taken their horses out of a royal grazing arrangement at the Lesser Ford. There had been talk in a neighbouring parish that Elyas would eventually be obliged to lead.

“Did you hear that?” Cedric asked.

“I heard men say foolish things.”

“Which men?”

“I told them I would not lead them.”

“Which men did you tell?”

Elyas looked at the clerk. A little ridge had formed in the man’s forefinger where the pen pressed.

“They should be heard separately.”

“They can be, when they are named.”

“If you have an accusation, bring it against them.”

“This examination concerns your knowledge.”

“My knowledge is that I refused.”

“And what you refused.”

They returned to it after water was brought. They returned again after the clerk changed a sheet. Elyas began to hear his own answers grow shorter and understood why repetition was useful. He could either sound evasive or say more than he had decided to say.

Cedric never raised his voice. When he changed the ground, he did it quietly, as though he were only turning a page.

“You certify the carts at the Lesser Crossing,” he said. “Each quarter. By name.”

“Yes.”

“At the request of the house on the river. Your undertaking.”

“Yes.”

“In the spring a cart on your list carried wool belonging to a man not on it. Captain Wace reported it to you. You spoke to the carter. You did not report it to the house.”

Elyas felt the room shift under him, very slightly, like a boat when someone steps aboard.

“I spoke to him. He didn’t do it again.”

“You protected him.”

“I dealt with it.”

“You dealt with it yourself, under an undertaking you gave to the house, and did not tell the house. Because he was your man.” Cedric did not look at the clerk; the clerk was already writing. “I am not accusing you of the wool, Sir Elyas. I am asking you whether the men who came to you understood that you would deal with things yourself.”

It was the fairest question he had been asked, and the worst. Elyas did not answer it at once, and the silence went into the record too.

At last Elyas said, “You know what it is to have a whole life made out of the worst account of an afternoon.”

Cedric’s hand stopped beside the ink.

The clerk looked up. He knew enough to recognise a remark which did not belong in the form of the examination, though perhaps not enough to understand it.

Cedric moved the ink farther from the paper.

“Did anyone offer men after Vautrin?”

“Yes.”

“Name him.”

“No.”

The clerk wrote that down.

That afternoon Cedric brought a book.

He put it on the table between them without opening it. It was small and bound in green cloth, worn pale at the corners, and Elyas knew it before it touched the wood. It was his. It had stood on the shelf in the little room off the hall for five years, beside the weight shaped like a sleeping dog. It held the names of the men who had served under him at the Scar, with their villages written beside them in his own hand, and in a different ink, added later, the ones who had not come back.

“This was taken from your house,” Cedric said.

“It wasn’t on my wife’s list.”

Cedric looked at him.

“She read your clerk’s list against the one our housekeeper made, item by item,” said Elyas. “I watched her. There was no book.”

For a moment Cedric said nothing. Then he opened the book at a place marked with a slip of paper.

“Of the men known to have come to your house since the spring,” he said, “eleven are in this book. In your hand. With their villages. Vautrin is here. So is the carter from the Lesser Crossing, and his cousin. So are two of the men who were in your garden the day Sir Berent let them keep their swords.” He turned the book so that Elyas could see the page. “You didn’t only receive strangers with grievances, Sir Elyas. You received your own men. Men who had followed you once. The household finds that significant.”

Elyas looked at his own writing. Vautrin, son of Anselme, of Sainte-Ode. He remembered writing it, in a tent, by a lamp that smoked, with the pen that Mara would take out of his hand three nights later.

“They came to me because they knew me,” he said. “That is the whole of it. They knew me because they served with me. The book tells you nothing you couldn’t have learned by asking any of them.”

“The book tells me what you knew. When they came. Who they were.” Cedric closed it. “The men who came to you with grievances were not strangers to you. When they offered service, you knew exactly whose service it would be, and how good it was, because you had commanded it.” He put his hand flat on the green cloth. “That is what the household will say. I am telling you so that you can answer it.”

“And the list?”

Cedric’s hand stayed on the book.

“I will have it entered,” he said.

He did not say when. He did not say that it had been a mistake. He took the book with him when he went, and Elyas sat on in the room with the hunting prints.

He was taken to Valora before the next sitting.

They travelled in daylight. At one stop a woman came to the inn yard with a child who wanted to see the prince. The escort moved her away courteously. Elyas had already lifted a hand. He let it fall before the child saw the gesture and thought it meant something he could not provide.

His new room had a window too high to look out of while seated. He stood beneath it and could see a patch of sky and the corner of a roof. A swallow came into view, vanished, and returned along almost the same line.

There was work. He had not expected that. He had expected, if he had expected anything, to sit. Instead there were papers. The accusation came in a fair copy, three sheets long, and he was to answer it in writing, point by point, before the next examination. There was a request from Cedric for the names of witnesses he wished heard on his behalf, with a note that the household would consider each name and might decline any. There was a letter from Harald, opened and resealed, which said in four lines that the duke had been informed and was taking advice and that his brother should say nothing that would embarrass the house, and nothing else.

He answered the accusation first. It took two days. He had to begin again several times, because each time he began he found himself writing a defence of the bench, and the bench had not been accused. He had been accused. He wrote about himself. What he had done, where, when, and why. What he had refused, and how, and before whom.

The witnesses were harder.

He sat with the list for most of a day. He could name the priest who had witnessed the refusal at Vautrin’s parish; the priest had put his name to it already, and it was in the household’s hands. He could name Wace, who would say what the ford had been and what the company had been, and who was a queen’s captain and could not easily be touched. He could name Mara. He wrote her name and looked at it and crossed it out and wrote it again, because she would be asked whether he named her or not, and he would rather she were asked as his witness than as something else.

He could not name Vautrin. Or the carter, or his cousin, or the two men in the garden, or the widow on the Sallows road who had asked only whether the army was a good life. Every one of them could say that he had refused. Every one of them, named as his witness, would be a man who had come to his house and could be asked why.

He sent the list with four names on it. Cedric took it without looking at it.

When Cedric came to the new room, Elyas asked whether his uncle’s confinement had begun with a room like this.

“Valdais offered terms,” Cedric said.

“So might I.”

“Then say what you offer.”

“A full account of my own conduct. No men raised. No summons issued. No resistance to lawful examination of any charge brought against me.”

“And the names?”

“No.”

Cedric did not lay out a sheet this time. He sat down across the table, which he had not done before, and folded his hands.

“The Marshal has asked me to put something to you,” he said. “Not as an examiner. As a message.”

Elyas waited.

“If you give the household the names of the men who offered you service, and nothing else, the Marshal will ask the queen that the charge be amended to one that carries confinement. A house. In the south, on the approaches, where you know the ground. Your wife with you. Your household, what you want of it. Men to guard you who will be told to let you ride out.” Cedric’s voice was quite even. “He said to tell you that it is not the command. He said you would know what he meant by that.”

Elyas knew. He sat for a moment with his hands on the table.

“And the men?”

“Would be examined. Separately. As you asked.”

“And if they’re found to have done nothing but offer?”

“Then they’d be released.”

“After how long?”

Cedric did not answer.

“Anseau was held five months,” said Elyas, “for saying a foolish thing to an examiner about robbers. Because one office had his mother’s name and another had his father’s. I’ve seen the household be careful. I’ve seen it be slow.” He looked at Cedric. “You brought him back. You know how long five months is.”

“I know.”

“Then tell the Marshal thank you. Tell him I know what he means, and that I’m grateful, and that I love him for it. And tell him no.”

Cedric looked at him. Something moved in his face, under the scar, that Elyas could not read.

“He said you would say that,” Cedric said. “He asked me to ask you twice.”

“Then ask again.”

Cedric did. Elyas said no again. Cedric nodded, as if a thing had been completed, and stood.

“I’ll tell him,” he said. At the door he stopped. “For what it is worth, Sir Elyas. I would have taken the house.”

“I know you would,” said Elyas. “That’s why he sent you to ask.”

Cedric went out. The key turned. Elyas sat on under the high window until the patch of sky went from blue to grey to dark, and then he lit the candle he had been given and took out the fair copy of the accusation and began, again, on the third sheet.

Chapter Thirty — What the Refusal Proved

Lyra was admitted for the question of dates.

She had asked to speak about Elyas’s intention, the northern journey, the offer of men. She had asked in writing, through Pellam, on the chancery’s paper, three times. The answer, when it came, permitted her to identify when a particular refusal had reached the chancery and what authority had accompanied it. Nothing else.

“It’s what they’ll hear,” Pellam said. “It’s the only thing you know that nobody else can say.”

“I know he refused.”

“You know that a paper saying he refused came into this office on a certain day, under a certain seal, with a certain priest’s name on it. That’s evidence. What you know about his intention is an opinion, and you are the woman who arranged the carrier.” He took off his spectacles. “I’m sorry. They’ll say it if I don’t.”

She prepared the dates. She laid out the refusal as it had come to her in Elyas’s letter, and the entry of Elyas’s letter in her own correspondence book, and the entry of the priest’s witness in the chancery’s receiving book, which Pellam had had copied for her, and the date of the northern communication in the household’s entry, which Aimeric had read to her in the east range and which she had written down as soon as she was out of the room. The refusal came first. By a month. She prepared that evidence until she knew its order without looking, then found herself unable to eat breakfast.

At the palace a guard asked her to leave her little knife. She used it to cut paper and had forgotten she wore it. He put it on a shelf with two others and gave her a wooden marker. She held the marker throughout the wait.

Cedric came into the waiting room while she was there.

He had a tied packet under his arm and was going through to the inner room, and he stopped when he saw her, as though he had not expected anyone, and then came and stood in front of the bench. She had never spoken to him. She had read his report in two halves and kept the first page of it in her drawer ever since, and she knew the scar and the high collar from the passages, and that was all.

“Mistress Lyra.”

“Captain.”

“You’re here for the dates.”

“Yes.”

He looked at the papers on her knee.

“They’re correct,” he said. “I checked them against the household’s entries last night. The refusal came first. By a month. The priest’s witness is in order.” He shifted the packet under his arm. “I’ve said so in my report. I thought you might want to know that before you went in, in case you were wondering whether you would have to prove it.”

She had come ready to fight for the dates against everybody in the building, and the man who had arrested Elyas was telling her she need not.

“Thank you,” she said.

“It’s what the papers say.” He hesitated. “The book. The green one. It has been entered. I’ve had it put on the list of what was taken from the house, with the date it was taken. The prince’s wife may want to know that.”

“I’m not—” Lyra stopped. She did not know how to tell a stranger that the prince’s wife had asked her what use she was. “I’ll see that she knows.”

Cedric nodded. He looked at her a moment longer, as if there were something else, and she saw him decide that there was not.

“I read your rule,” he said. “About the levy rate for men taken on a Crown ford. The ten men from the Lesser Ford were paid under it. I thought it was well made.”

Then he went through into the inner room, and the door closed behind him, and Lyra sat on the bench with the wooden marker in her hand and found that it had printed its edge into her palm.

The room contained more chairs than people. Pellam sat beside the appointed examiner. Fayd stood near a window. Cedric’s report lay in a tied packet on the table. Veralyn came in with Tylan after Lyra had been told where to sit.

The queen wore green beneath black. She looked tired and attentive. Lyra found herself hoping for the woman who had paid the cradle repairer’s breakfast without making it difficult.

The examiner asked for the date. She gave it, supplied the entry, identified the original. The refusal preceded the accusation. The priest’s witness was independent. Berent had been instructed to ensure the refusal was understood.

Veralyn asked to see the copy.

She read it in silence. Lyra waited while her finger followed the line forbidding men to assemble in Elyas’s name.

“Yes,” the queen said. “He refused this.”

“He refused service,” Lyra said.

“Here.”

“The other offers also.”

“Were you there?”

“No. His account—”

“He will not name the others.”

The examiner opened Cedric’s report at a marked place. Lyra recognised Elyas in the distinction between being offered men and summoning them. She also recognised how much could be made of his refusal to say who had offered.

“He meant to protect people from an accusation merely for speaking,” she said.

“He has protected them from being asked,” Veralyn answered.

“Some came because the appointed officers would not hear them.”

“Some came because they had been heard and disliked the answer.”

“Yes.”

Tylan leaned forward.

“He could have sent the refusal with the names to the proper office. He could have accepted the command. He could still answer what he knows.”

“Would that release him?”

The question came before Lyra had decided to ask it. Pellam looked at her. The examiner began to speak, but Veralyn lifted a hand.

“It would allow us to know what we are releasing.”

She turned to Tylan. “You put the house to him.”

“Twice,” said Tylan.

“And?”

“Twice he said no.”

The queen looked at the refusal again, and then set it down on the table, square with the edge, as if putting it away.

“Then we have asked,” she said. She turned back to Lyra. “You arranged the carrier for Sir Berent.”

“Yes, your grace.”

“You knew it was to be done quietly.”

“Yes.”

“You told nobody. Not the chancellor. Not Master Fayd, who pays you. Not me, who has had you in my rooms with my daughter at my breast.” There was no anger in it. It was said as though she were reading down a list. “And then you told the dowager, at her table, after supper.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

Lyra had known the question would come here too. She had thought about it on the walk from her lodging, and at the gate with the wooden marker in her hand, and she had decided that she would not give the answer she had given herself for the first nine days, because it was not true, and she would not lie in this room.

“I don’t know, your grace.”

Veralyn looked at her as she had looked at Ferrand the day of It shall matter not.

“That isn’t an answer I can use,” said the queen.

“It’s the only one I have.”

“Then it will have to be recorded as such.” Veralyn turned to the examiner. “Is there anything else in the dates?”

There was nothing else in the dates. The examiner thanked Lyra and said she might go. As she rose she saw Fayd at the window, who had not spoken, and who was looking not at her but at the queen, with an expression she had never seen on him before and could not read; and she saw Tylan, who was looking at the table with his hands flat on either side of Cedric’s report, like a man at the end of a long march who has been told there is one more hill.

Outside, Lyra could not find the wooden marker.

The guard waited while she searched her bag. She emptied papers, a folded cloth, a little purse. Her hand caught the corner of the invitation from Jaennis and she pushed it back as though it were something shameful.

The marker was in her other hand.

The guard gave her the knife without comment. She thanked him too elaborately and hated herself for that as well.

Fayd caught up with her in the passage.

“You established the date.”

“She agrees he refused.”

“Yes.”

“Then what was I for?”

“To establish the date.”

She turned on him.

“Don’t.”

He stopped. People were moving behind them, obliged to go round. Lyra lowered her voice.

“I helped him find a carrier. I knew he was asking Riarhin to come. You have that from me. It does not make an army.”

“No.”

“Then why let them use it so?”

“Because he asks an outsider to intervene while declining to tell the authority he acknowledges what intervention his neighbours propose.”

“She is his friend.”

“She is capable of more than friendship.”

Lyra knew that too.

Fayd looked at the papers still half out of her bag.

“What happened at supper?”

She stared at him.

“You told the dowager something you had withheld elsewhere. I am asking why.”

“I meant not to.”

“Then what do you intend to do about the error?”

“Find out whether it was mine.”

He considered her for a moment. Then he stepped aside to let a servant pass and asked no more.

She saw Mara in the lower court as she was leaving.

Mara was standing by the door to the household’s offices with a folded paper in her hand and Nelle beside her, and she was talking to a clerk, and the clerk was shaking his head. She wore the grey she wore for travelling. She looked thinner. She looked, Lyra thought, exactly like herself at the station with a boy who would not drink from the jug: patient and immovable and entirely unwilling to be told no.

Lyra stopped on the steps.

Mara turned her head, the way people do when they feel themselves looked at. She saw Lyra. For a moment neither of them moved. The court was full of people going between them, a man with a basket of bread, two soldiers, a boy leading a horse, and through the gaps Lyra could see Mara’s face, and it did not change, and it did not turn away.

Then the clerk said something, and Mara turned back to him, and held out the folded paper again.

Lyra went down the steps and across the court and out through the gate without stopping. She did not know whether she had been refused or only not asked.

In the long gallery on the way to the street she met Jaennis.

Jaennis was walking with Vaelrie. They were arm in arm and Vaelrie was laughing at something, and when Jaennis saw Lyra she let go of Vaelrie’s arm and came forward at once with both hands out.

“Lyra. I heard you spoke for him. That was brave.”

She took Lyra’s hands. Her own were warm and dry. She held them the way she held everybody’s, lightly, as though she were offering something and would not mind if it were refused.

Lyra waited to feel something. She did not know what. Jaennis’s hands were warm and her voice was kind, and Lyra had liked both for years, and there was nothing in the gallery that had not been in a hundred other rooms where Jaennis had taken somebody’s hands and said she was sorry. She could not tell any more where the ordinary warmth stopped. She stood in the gallery being kindly spoken to and found that she was listening to herself, the way she would listen for a step on the stair at night, to see whether she was about to want to say something.

She took her hands away.

She did it too quickly. She saw Vaelrie see it, and Vaelrie’s face change. Jaennis’s did not. Jaennis let her hands fall to her sides as if she had meant to let go at that moment anyway, and smiled, and said that she was so sorry, that she had known Elyas since he was a boy, that it was all so terrible, and that Lyra must come to her if she needed anything, anything at all.

“Thank you,” said Lyra.

She went on down the gallery. At the end of it she stopped, and stood by the window with her hands pressed together in front of her, and looked at them.

They were only her hands. There was ink on the right one, from the dates.

She went home and took out the sheet headed Things that mean nothing and wrote beneath the shawl: In the gallery. Nothing happened. I took my hands away. She left the sheet open on the table.

Chapter Thirty-One — The Visit

Mara brought his better shirt.

He knew she had washed it herself because one cuff still held the faint mark she had once threatened to cut out of it. The guard examined the seams, shook it, and put it on the table. She watched him throughout. When he finished she picked it up and folded it again.

They were given a little time in the room where the examinations took place. The door remained open. Elyas had planned to tell her the questions, the corrections, the ways in which Cedric’s account might be challenged. At the sight of her he forgot the order of every argument.

“You’re thin,” she said.

“They feed me.”

“Do you eat?”

“Yes.”

“Then you’re sleeping badly.”

He smiled. She did not.

“Sit,” he said.

She sat opposite him and laid the shirt between them. He wanted her beside him, but moving the chairs would make the wish visible to the man in the doorway. After a moment he moved his own.

“How is the mare?”

“Sound.”

“Nelle?”

“Angry.”

“Good.”

“She’s counted everything they took. There’s a book missing from the list.”

“The green one.”

Mara looked at him.

“Cedric had it,” he said. “He brought it to the table. He’d found every man who came to the house since the spring, in my hand, with his village.” He watched her take that in. “He said he’d have it entered.”

“Has he?”

“I can ask.”

“Don’t ask. Tell them.” Her voice was very low. “Tell them your wife has a list in her bodice with Cedric Aeryl’s name on it, and the green book isn’t on it, and she will put that list in front of anybody who will read it.”

“Nelle’s sister’s floor,” he said.

“Nelle’s sister’s floor,” Mara agreed, and for a moment something passed between them that was almost a smile.

He looked at her hands. The little scar beside her thumb had faded. He could remember exactly how she had got it, trying to cut a piece of hardened leather while telling him she did not need a better knife.

She saw where he was looking and closed her hand.

“Lyra has put your refusal before them.”

“I know.”

“She was heard.”

“Yes.”

“So what do they still want?”

“The men whose names they don’t have.”

“Will you give them?”

He shook his head.

Mara leaned back. Beyond the door someone walked past carrying wood. A piece fell; the man swore and bent to retrieve it. They both listened until he had gone.

“Are they worth this?” she asked.

“I don’t know.”

She shut her eyes briefly.

“Thank you for saying that.”

“I can’t give them because they’re good. Some aren’t. I can’t give them because they’re innocent of everything anyone might ask. I don’t know that either.”

“Then why?”

“Because they came to ask me. Because I told them they could. If a man broke the law, let them name the act and bring him to answer. They want me to begin with the man and let them find the act.”

“And they have yours.”

“Yes.”

She looked at the shirt. The crease had begun to loosen where the guard shook it.

“I won’t call you foolish for letting people come. I won’t make that easier for them.”

“Mara—”

“But I am allowed to want you back.”

He took her hand. She let him hold it, then turned hers and gripped his hard enough to hurt.

“I know.”

“Do you?”

He could not answer that by saying it again. He put his forehead against their joined hands. For a moment she rested her other hand on his head.

When he lifted it she was looking at the door, where the guard stood with his back half turned, pretending to watch the passage.

“The branch is still on the path,” she said. “The one you cut. I haven’t moved it.”

“You’ll trip on it.”

“I step over it.” She looked back at him. “Berent isn’t home. There’s been nothing from the north. I don’t know whether he found her.”

“He’ll have found her.”

“You always say that.”

“He’s generally right to be found.” Elyas turned her hand over in his and looked at the palm, at the lines in it, at the small scar beside the thumb. “Mara. Whatever they decide. Don’t petition for me in a way that puts you in a room with them.”

She took her hand back.

“Don’t,” she said.

“I only mean—”

“I know what you mean. You mean you’d like to decide what I’m allowed to do after. You’ve been deciding things for both of us all year, love, very tenderly, and I’ve let you, because mostly I wanted what you wanted.” Her voice did not rise. “Not this. What I do after is mine.”

He looked at her.

“Yes,” he said. “It is.”

She nodded, once, and took his hand again.

Cedric brought the decision the following afternoon.

There had been a further examination before the appointed officers.

It was held in a long room in the palace with the shutters closed against the light and candles lit at noon. Three officers sat behind a table: a judge of the queen’s court whom Elyas did not know, an old soldier from the Marshal’s staff whom he did, and a man from the household in a dark coat without a badge. Cedric’s report lay in front of them, tied with a tape, and beside it the green book, and beside that a space.

Elyas stood. He was not offered a chair.

They read the accusation. They read his answer to it, the three sheets he had rewritten four times, and he listened to his own sentences in the judge’s dry voice and found that they sounded like somebody else’s. They read the refusal and the priest’s witness. They read the entry of the northern correspondence as the household had first made it, and then as it had been corrected, with quietly in it, and the prince’s wife knew that he had asked an old friend to visit, and did not know what he wrote. They read the four names of his witnesses. The household’s man said that the priest had been heard already and that his evidence was in the record; that Captain Wace could speak to the conduct of the ford but not to the prince’s house; that the lady was the prisoner’s wife. The judge asked whether the fourth witness would be called. The household’s man said he would be, if the officers wished. Elyas had named the carter’s reeve, Ermengarde, who could say what the ford had been. The officers did not wish.

Then the door opened and a clerk came in with a packet.

He was one of Fayd’s; Elyas knew the livery of the office by now, the grey with the narrow black cord. He laid the packet in the space beside the green book and bowed and went out, and the household’s man untied it, and it was full.

Elyas watched them read it. He could not see the pages. He could see their faces, and the old soldier’s hand going to his mouth once, and the judge turning back a sheet to read it again. When they had finished the judge asked him whether he knew a young man called Anseau son of Hugon.

“I know him.”

“He has made a statement to the household.” The judge found the place. “He names eleven persons who, to his knowledge, frequented your house in the months before your arrest.” He read the names. They were the names Anseau had told Elyas he had given, with his foot on the stool in the hall, crying. “Of these, the household finds that seven are entered in your hand in this book as having served under you at the Scar.”

“They did serve under me.”

“And two are the subject of statements, also in this packet, by their neighbours, to the effect that they had spoken in their parishes of the prince’s being obliged to lead.”

“I told them I would not.”

“So your refusal says.” The judge laid the sheet down. “The officers accept that you refused. The question before them is not whether you refused. It is whether, having refused, you continued to maintain in your house a following composed of men who had served under you, who had offered you their service, and who believed you would lead them, and whether you sought outside the Protector’s peace the countenance of a person able to assist you, while withholding from the Protector the names that would have allowed her to judge the danger for herself.”

The visitors, the offers, the northern letter: he recognised each occasion. But the judge had said maintain a following. He had sent those men home. The refusal lay on the table, within reach of the judge’s hand.

“They came to me,” he said. “I did not keep them. I did not maintain them. I opened a door.”

“The officers have your answer.”

The finding, when it came, distinguished neither his intention from the expectation of those who approached him nor his request to Riarhin from an attempt to gather support beyond the Protector’s knowledge. It gave more weight to his position than to his denial that he had used it.

He understood each part. He still expected confinement.

When Cedric read the sentence, Elyas looked towards the high window. The sky beyond it was pale. He found himself trying to determine whether the evening would be clear, as though some journey remained to be planned.

“When?”

Cedric told him.

“And Mara?”

“She will be informed.”

“No. What happens to her?”

“The sentence is yours.”

“The house?”

“I have no order against the household.”

“Get that written.”

Cedric laid down the warrant.

“I can request it.”

Elyas almost laughed. He had spent the whole peace learning the distance between those words and an answer.

“Please.”

Cedric nodded.

Tylan came that night.

He came alone, after dark, and the guard let him in and went away down the passage out of hearing without being told. He had a candle in one hand and a jug in the other, and he set them both on the table and sat down on the other stool without asking, the way he had sat down on the ends of beds at Tarlington when they were boys and one of them was in trouble.

“It’s wine,” he said. “It’s not very good. I didn’t want to ask for the good.”

Elyas poured it. It was not very good.

They drank for a while without speaking. The candle made the room smaller.

“I asked her for the house,” Tylan said. “After you said no. I asked her anyway. I said you’d go to it without giving the names and stay in it and never trouble anybody again.”

“And?”

“And she asked me whether I believed that.” Tylan turned the cup in his fingers. “And I said no. Because I don’t. You’d sit in a house on the southern approach and the first week a man would come to the gate with a grievance and you’d let him in.”

Elyas said nothing.

“She asked me what I recommended, then,” said Tylan. “If not the house.”

“What did you tell her?”

Tylan looked at the candle.

“I told her the truth,” he said. “That I’d done what I could. That I couldn’t find another way that she would take. That it should be quick, and clean, and done in the open, and that you should be let to speak if you asked.” He drank. “I’m the Marshal, Elyas. I’ve put my name to it. I’m not going to sit here and tell you I didn’t.”

“I know.”

“I’m not asking you to forgive me.”

“I know that too.” Elyas put down the cup. “You tried twice. You sent Cedric to ask twice. I know what you were doing.”

“And it didn’t work.”

“No.”

They sat. Somewhere in the building a door closed, and footsteps went away along a stone floor.

“Do you remember,” Tylan said, “the winter at Tarlington when you fell through the ice on the mill pond, and Veralyn went in after you, and I stood on the bank holding the rope and shouting at you both?”

“You didn’t shout. You told us very calmly what to do.”

“I was shouting inside.” Tylan stood up. He left the jug. “I’m still holding the rope, Elyas. I’m just not allowed to pull.”

He went out. The candle burned down beside the jug. Elyas sat with it until it guttered, and did not drink any more of the wine, and did not sleep.

He asked to speak publicly.

Cedric took the request. The answer came with the priest the following morning, conveyed by the officer who would conduct him to the place, and this time Cedric came too, and had it in his hand.

It was a half-sheet. It was not in a clerk’s hand. Elyas knew the writing before Cedric had unfolded it far enough to read: the long, impatient loops, the stroke across the top of the capital that cut through it like a sword through a rope. He had seen it on notes passed under the table at lessons. He had seen it on orders at the Scar.

He may speak. He is to be allowed to finish. No one is to interrupt him. V.

“She wrote it herself,” Cedric said. “At table. The Marshal had put your request to her. She sent for paper and wrote it and gave it to me.” He paused. “She read it over before she gave it me. Then she said, He’ll want to be heard. Let him be heard.”

Elyas held the half-sheet. The officer who would conduct him was watching from the door, and the priest beside him, and Cedric in front of him, and none of them said anything.

“Did she say anything else?”

Cedric considered. Elyas saw him decide to answer exactly.

“She said, He was always better at that than any of us.”

Elyas looked at the loops of the V. He could not tell, reading it, whether it was a gift, or a courtesy, or something she did because it cost her nothing and she could afford to be generous with a man she was going to kill. She had written He may speak in her own hand, and he was going to.

He gave the half-sheet back to Cedric.

“Keep it with the papers,” he said. “So that afterwards somebody can see it was granted.”

“I will.”

“And enter the book.”

Cedric met his eyes.

“It was entered this morning,” he said, and went out.

The priest waited until they were alone before asking what Elyas wanted from him.

“I’m expected to know, aren’t I?”

“Some do.”

“Do they find it?”

“Sometimes they ask for somebody else.”

Elyas looked at the shirt on the bed. He had put it on the night before to see whether the collar still fitted, then taken it off because he wanted it clean.

“Can you take a message?”

“If it is permitted.”

“For my wife.”

“I will ask.”

He gave the priest an account of the money still due to the roof repairer, the mare’s habit at the left gate, the place he had put a letter from Ouen which Mara had wanted to answer. Halfway through he stopped.

“She knows most of this.”

“Then she will know you were thinking of home.”

Elyas sat. He could hear his own breathing. It seemed too loud for a room containing another person.

“I don’t want her to be comforted by being told I was ready.”

“Are you?”

“No.”

The priest drew a chair beside him.

“They will ask you afterwards,” Elyas said. “What I said to you. Whether I was sorry.”

“Some will.”

“What will you tell them?”

The priest considered. He was a thin, grey man from the chapel in the lower town, not one of the palace clergy; Elyas had asked for him because he had buried two of Wace’s men from the ford and Wace had said he did it properly.

“I’ll tell them what I saw,” he said. “That you ate two mouthfuls. That you were afraid. That you asked about a roof.” He folded his hands. “If you tell me you repent of something, I’ll hear it, and it will be between you and God and me, and I won’t carry it out of this room to make anybody easier about tomorrow. And if you don’t, I won’t say you did.”

“There are people who’d like me to have been sorry.”

“There are always people who’d like that,” said the priest. “It lets them go home.” He looked at Elyas. “Are you?”

Elyas thought about it honestly. He owed the man that.

“For Mara,” he said. “For Berent, who’ll come home and find it done. For Anseau, who told them the names and thinks it’s his fault. For some of the men on the bench, who’ll be asked now what they came for.” He stopped. “Not for the door.”

“Then that’s what I’ll know,” said the priest, “and what I’ll say, if I’m asked, is that you made your peace and that it was yours to make.” He was quiet a moment. “What shall we pray for?”

For time, Elyas thought. For a mistake discovered at a desk. For the next man through the door to say the queen had reconsidered, for a horse to be brought, for the branch in the garden to require cutting again because years had passed and he had neglected it.

He asked for courage. The priest began quietly, and after a little while Elyas found he could answer.

Chapter Thirty-Two — The Open Door

He woke before it was light and did not know, for a moment, where he was.

He had been dreaming about the ford. Not the flood; the ordinary ford, low, in autumn, with the water clear over the stones and Wace beside him and nothing to be done. He lay on his back on the narrow bed and looked at the high window, which was grey, and the knowledge of the day came into the room slowly, the way cold comes in under a door.

His body knew before he did. His hands were cold and his stomach was tight and there was a pulse going in his throat that he could hear against the pillow. He lay and listened to it. He had been afraid before. He had been afraid at the Scar, and in the river at the Lesser Ford with the water coming up, and in Acasia more times than he had ever told Mara. It had always been fear of something that might happen. This was fear of a thing that was going to happen at a known hour, and his body did not seem to understand the difference, and went on preparing him to run.

He got up and used the pot and washed his face in the cold water in the basin. That helped a little.

They brought him breakfast at first light.

Bread, and a bowl of broth, and a piece of cheese, and a small pear, which he looked at. It was yellow and freckled and not quite ripe. He did not know who had thought to send a pear. It might have been the kitchen, which sent what it had. It might not.

He ate two mouthfuls of the bread and drank some of the broth. His throat would not take more. He put the pear inside his shirt, against his skin, where it was cool and hard, and did not know why he had done it.

The shirt was the one Mara had brought. Now his fingers were clumsy with the buttons at the wrist. He did the left one wrong and had to undo it. While he fastened it again he thought about the roof over the back of the stable. Mara had asked him to have it mended the first week they came back from Valora, five years ago. There was a man in the village who did roofs. Elyas owed him for the kitchen chimney. He had told the priest about the money yesterday, but he had not told him about the stable roof, and the rain would come in on the mare, and she would go lame again from standing wet, and Mara would have to find somebody.

He sat down on the bed with one button done and one undone and thought about the stable roof for some time. It seemed very important. He could not think why he had never mended it. There had always been something else.

When the priest came in he was still sitting there.

“The stable,” Elyas said. “At home. The roof at the back. Will you tell her—”

“I’ll tell her.”

“There’s a man in the village. He did the chimney. I haven’t paid him.”

“You told me yesterday.”

“I didn’t tell you about the stable.”

The priest sat down beside him and did the other button.

“I’ll tell her about the stable,” he said.

The square smelled of bread.

A baker had opened before dawn. Elyas could not see the shop, but the smell reached him as they turned into the space before the fountain, warm flour and the scorched edge of a crust. People stood close enough together to keep their cloaks dry where the shoulders touched. It had rained during the night. Water lay in the joints of the stone.

He had eaten two mouthfuls at breakfast. The bread now made him hungry, which seemed so absurd that he nearly smiled. The pear was still inside his shirt. He could feel it against his ribs as he walked.

The priest walked beside him. The officer ahead moved a man out of the way with an open hand. Nobody struck anyone. A woman raised herself on her toes to see. Near the fountain a man fastened his belt, having loosened it while he waited.

Elyas climbed the steps.

From there the crowd became faces. He looked for Mara before he could prevent himself, then stopped looking because finding her might make the rest impossible. He had been allowed to write. The letter would reach her. He tried to hold to that smaller certainty.

The proclamation was read. His name, his rank, the finding. The words carried well across the square. Some people moved forward at the mention of the northern correspondence. Others seemed chiefly interested in getting a view past the shoulders in front of them.

When it ended, the officer turned to Elyas. He had a half-sheet in his hand. Elyas saw the loops of the V.

“You may speak,” the officer said. “You are to be allowed to finish.”

He looked at the priest. The man nodded once and stepped back.

“I have been granted leave to speak,” Elyas said. “I thank the queen for it. I ask you to hear what I say, and afterwards to remember which words were mine.”

He had begun too quietly. A man at the front told those behind him to be still. Elyas waited. The sound of the fountain came through the thinning voices.

“When I was fifteen I knelt in the chapel at Tarlington and swore an oath. Some of you have sworn it. You know the words. I swore to serve the crown of Vhasia and its people, to defend those who could not defend themselves, to keep faith with those who kept faith with me. I was a boy. I did not know what half of it meant. I have spent every year since finding out.”

He could hear his voice now, coming back to him off the stone of the houses across the square. It steadied him.

“I have served Vhasia since before Veralyn was its Protector. I served beside men who died believing that their service placed a duty upon those who sent them. I have sent such men. I have stood where their wives asked what I meant to do now that the man who kept the roof sound was beneath the earth. I have made promises there. Some I kept. Some I have failed to keep. You may ask my wife how often another person’s necessity came home with me, and how readily I expected her to find room for it.”

A little movement passed through the front of the crowd. He heard a woman’s brief laugh and was grateful for it.

“The queen has kept promises too. You know it. Men are paid who once went home with a paper and a curse. I stood in a receiving yard in this city five years ago with a woman who had brought her husband’s good boots, and the money was not there, and I put my name to it, and a fortnight later the money came, on the day, through a house on the river where a man kept a shelf. That was the queen’s doing. There are roads upon which your daughters can travel more safely than they did. There is a tower at the Lesser Ford where the watch is paid on the day and the villages sleep. There is a bridge at Varenne. There are officers who can be made to answer for taking twice what was due once. I have asked for those answers. I have received them. I will not spend this morning denying help because the hand which gave it has condemned me.”

He could see the officer listening now. The man’s eyes remained on him, his face composed.

“But I swore to defend people.
