I did not swear to defend the peace from them.” He let that stand for a moment. “And there are people in this duchy who have been told that their own debts are a foreign matter. That the house on the river holds them and the queen cannot touch them. That the tolls are pledged and the mill is scheduled and the road is surveyed and the examiner has counted and found them able to spare a son. They have been told this by men who were not lying. Every paper was in order. I read them. I helped write some of them.

“A thing does not cease to be ours because another has made it prosper. A holding is still a home when a surveyor finds it useful. A son is still a son when the examiner finds that a household can spare him. There is a girl on the Sallows road who weaves, and her loom paid a third of her father’s debt, and the examiner did not count it because he does not assess looms. She wanted to be a weaver. She was told, very kindly, by a man doing his work well, that she could be spared. There is a woman at the Lesser Ford whose village has taken its cheese to market by the old ford since before there was a market, and one morning the ford was closed because the tolls were pledged, and she walked eleven miles to my gate to tell me, and she did not ask me to open it. She knew I couldn’t. She wanted me to know what it cost. These are the people the peace was made for. They must be permitted to speak of what the peace has done to them.”

His mouth had dried. He paused, swallowed, and went on.

“When they spoke to me, I had no remedy for many of them. I could not rebuild every ferry. I could not pay every debt. Some brought claims which would have injured their neighbours if granted. Some wanted the return of privileges under which other people had suffered. I heard those men too. Hearing them did not make them right. Refusing them did not require me to close the gate against the next man.”

He put a hand on the rail.

“They came to my door. I opened it.”

“There were other doors!” a voice called near the fountain.

Elyas looked towards it. The man had already lowered his head behind another shoulder. Elyas let the silence hold long enough that people could hear the water again.

“Yes,” he said. “There were. Some had tried them. Some had no right to pass them. Some feared what would be required in exchange for being heard. Some were impatient, and I told them so. If you have never gone from one door to another with an answer that sends you back to the first, I hope you never shall. If you have, you know why a man may knock where he believes he is known.”

The priest stood beyond the officer, hands folded. Elyas could feel him without looking directly at him.

“I was told that certain debts were a foreign matter, beyond the reach of the person to whom I appealed. Yet the road they secured was the queen’s business, and the men they furnished were the queen’s soldiers, and the peace which required payment was under the queen’s protection. I could not tell the people who stood before me where their own affairs ended and that protection began. I asked to be shown. For that question I offer no apology.”

A gust moved over the square. He felt the damp at his collar, where Mara’s washing had softened the cloth.

“Men offered me service. I refused it. I forbade an assembly in my name. That refusal was written, carried, and witnessed. It has been read to those who judged me. You should know that. You should also know that I continued to receive the people who made the offers. That is true. I will not make my defence depend upon a lie.”

He saw several heads turn towards one another. At the edge of the square a child began complaining and was lifted against a man’s shoulder.

“I have been asked for names. I have answered for what I did, what I said, where I went, what I refused. Where another man has committed an offence, let him be charged with it and heard in his own name. I will not purchase my safety by giving up everybody who believed he might speak to me before his words were made a charge. Some of those men would not do as much for me. I know it. I did not first promise to protect only men who would deserve me.”

The sentence took more from him than he expected. He drew breath slowly. His hand on the rail had begun to tremble. He took it away.

“I asked an old friend to come. I asked her because I was afraid that I had grown certain in the company of people who wanted my certainty. I wanted an answer I had not prepared for. That request has been made part of the case against me. I cannot make it smaller to save myself. If an honest request to be contradicted is danger to the peace, the peace has become very easily endangered.”

Somebody said his name. He could not tell from where.

“There is a difference,” Elyas said, and his voice had risen now without his meaning it to, and he let it, “between a man who comes to a gate and offers his service, and a household that is told how many of its children it can spare. I have seen both. I have received both. The first I refused, because it was not mine to take. The second I could not refuse, because it was never offered to me. It was taken, by rule, on the day stated, with a clerk’s writing to say what it was for. I have helped to write that rule. I have been glad of what it bought. I am not glad now.

“Service is a thing a man gives. It is the best thing he has. I have seen men give it at the Scar and at the ford and on the road, and I have seen what it costs them and I have seen them give it anyway, freely, because they were asked and because they believed that the asking put a duty on the one who asked. That is the whole of what I swore at fifteen, and I did not understand it until this year. The one who asks has a duty. The one who asks does not own what he is given.”

He looked beyond the fountain, over the heads, towards the narrow opening through which he had entered. The smell of bread had gone. There was wet wool, stone, the smoke of a fire somewhere sheltered from the wind.

“I will ask one thing of those who have heard me. When you leave this square, do not lend me a summons I have not given. Do not tell a man that he must take up arms because Elyas said so. I have said no such thing. Go home to the people who have a claim upon you. Keep the promises you can keep. If you remember me, remember also the persons for whom I asked, and do not require them to be innocent of every failing before you think them worth hearing.”

He turned, for the first time, towards the palace end of the square, where the windows were. He did not know whether anybody was behind them. He spoke to them anyway.

“To the queen I say this: I have wished your works to prosper. I have helped where I could. We were children together at Tarlington. You are my kinswoman and I have loved you as kin all my life, and I say it here because I will not have another chance. Your success does not release you from hearing those whom it has hurt. They are yours to govern. They are not yours to spend until the remainder satisfies you.”

He let his eyes rest on the faces nearest him.

“To my wife, if any word of this reaches her before my own, I ask forgiveness for what she must now carry. I have no instruction for her grief. What she does after is hers.”

He stopped. There had been one more passage, in the night, something about Tarlington and the years before the war. It was unnecessary now. He could hear how much the square was waiting for a conclusion. He found that he had one, and that it was the same words.

“They came to my door,” he said. “I opened it.”

For a little while nobody moved.

Then the officer stepped forward. Elyas turned towards the priest. He had used the time granted him and had no wish left to make another argument. He asked the man to stay close.

“I am here,” the priest said.

The last preparations were explained. Elyas listened carefully, grateful for directions about where to put his feet. When his collar was opened he felt cold air against his throat. He thought of Mara folding the shirt after the guard had shaken it out.

The pear was still inside his shirt. He felt it move against his ribs as he knelt, and put his hand over it without thinking, as a man puts his hand over something he has been given to carry.

The wood beneath his other hand was rougher than he had expected. He moved his palm once to find a smooth place, heard the priest begin again, and followed the words as far as he could.

Chapter Thirty-Three — The Words Afterwards

Lyra heard that Elyas had called for rebellion before she heard that he had finished speaking.

The report came from Jehan, returning from the square. He had stood too far away to catch the speech but had heard other people explain it. He wanted to know whether the chancery would remain open if Istovar rose.

“Who heard him?” Lyra asked.

“The men at the fountain.”

“Which men?”

“I didn’t ask their names.”

“Then don’t give their words his.”

Jehan looked hurt. He had brought her something he thought she would need. She made herself thank him for coming back and asked him to find a person who could repeat what he had heard without first explaining what it meant.

When he had gone she sat at the desk. The page before her concerned a lease on a wharf. A woman had paid the fee twice because the first office’s acknowledgment had never reached the second. Lyra had both acknowledgments now. She put them together, wrote the request for repayment, and discovered at the signature that she had used the wrong date.

She started again.

Jehan came back at noon with a baker’s boy.

The boy was about twelve and had been delivering loaves to a house on the square when the crowd filled it, and had climbed onto the house’s mounting block to see, and had stayed there with his basket through the whole of it because he could not get down. He was frightened of the chancery and more frightened of Lyra, and he stood in front of her desk turning his cap in his floury hands.

“Tell me what he said,” Lyra said. “Only what you heard. If you didn’t hear a part, tell me you didn’t.”

The boy thought. He had a good memory, the kind children have for things that frightened them, and he gave her what he had in pieces, out of order. That the prince had thanked the queen. That he had talked about a woman with boots. That he had said the queen had done good things and he wouldn’t pretend she hadn’t. That he had talked about a girl who wove and a woman who walked eleven miles. That somebody had shouted about other doors. That the prince had said a thing about service being a thing a man gives.

“And about rising?” said Jehan, behind him.

The boy looked at him with scorn.

“He said don’t,” he said. “He said don’t say he said so. He said go home.” He turned back to Lyra. “And then he said the door thing again. The same words. They came to my door. I opened it. He said it twice. The second time everybody went quiet.”

Lyra wrote it all down. When she had finished she read it back to him and he corrected her twice. She gave him a coin and Jehan took him down to the kitchens for something to eat, and she sat looking at the page.

She had it. She had it from somebody who had stood on a mounting block with a basket of bread and heard it. She knew that by nightfall there would be a dozen other versions in the city, and that some of them would have Elyas calling Istovar to arms, and some would have him weeping and begging for mercy, and some would have him cursing the queen by name. She could not stop any of them. She could put the baker’s boy’s words on a page and keep the page.

She made two copies. One she put in the drawer. The other she put in an envelope addressed to Mara, and held it, and did not send it.

The instruction about the body went to Mara’s house. Lyra heard about it from Pellam, who had seen the order: four days, and the widow might make arrangements.

She meant to go.

She meant to go the whole of the four days. She thought about hiring a horse, and about what she would say at the door, and about Mara’s letter in the drawer with What use are you in it in Mara’s steady hand. She thought about Mara standing in the lower court with the folded paper, not turning away. She did not know whether that had been an invitation. She did not know whether she would be let in.

On the fifth day she hired the horse.

The burial place was a small church with a walled yard on the road above the pear-tree house. She reached it late, because the horse was slow and she had let it be slow, and when she came to the gate the service had already begun. She could hear the minister’s voice from the yard. There were people inside, more than she had expected, standing in the wet grass between the graves. There were people outside too, at the gate and along the wall: men in working clothes with their caps in their hands, a woman with a child on her hip, two soldiers in the queen’s coats who stood a little apart from the rest and did not look at anybody.

Lyra stood among them. Nobody asked who she was.

She could see Mara from the gate, at the front, beside the minister. She wore black. Nelle was on one side of her. On the other side was nobody. Lyra looked at the empty place beside Mara and thought that she could walk through the gate now and across the grass and stand in it, and that Mara would either let her or not, and that either way it would be over.

She did not move. She stood at the gate and listened to the prayers, and when the minister reached the part where the people answered she answered with them, under her breath, and when it was finished and the people inside began to move she turned before anybody could turn towards the gate, and went back to where she had tied the horse.

A woman was standing beside it.

It was Perrine. She had a parcel in her arms wrapped in a cloth, and she was looking at Lyra’s horse with the expression of somebody who has been told to wait by a particular horse and is not sure it is the right one.

“You’re not going in,” said Perrine.

“No.”

Perrine did not ask why. She looked at the parcel.

“I’ve brought bread. And cheese. I don’t know where to put it.”

“There’ll be a kitchen at the house. Nelle will take it.”

Perrine nodded but did not move.

“Aline has her place,” she said. “At the weaver’s. In the town. A year now. The weaver says she’s the best he’s taken in ten years.”

“Lucan told me. He has a scarf.”

“He wrote to the weaver for her. Elyas. Last year. To say she was worth taking.” Perrine moved the fold of the cloth to check that it had not come loose. Lyra could smell the bread. “Lucan couldn’t come. They wouldn’t give him leave.”

“He needn’t answer to anybody for that.”

“I know.” Perrine looked at her. “You wrote down his pay. In the yard. With the boots.”

“Yes.”

“He said you were the one who made him read the sum.” Perrine settled the parcel against her hip. “He said it to Matthis, after. He said, She made me read it, and I was angry, and she was right.” She looked towards the church. “I thought you’d want to know he said it.”

Lyra could not answer. Perrine did not seem to need her to. She nodded, once, the way she had nodded at the kitchen table at the Sallows over the clause, and walked away towards the gate with the bread and the cheese, and went in.

Lyra untied the horse and rode back to Valora in the rain.

News of Harald’s death reached Valora before Lyra had returned to her ordinary hours.

A fall on a stair. An accident, someone said. A convenient accident, someone else answered. A man at an inn named the Bloody Table with the confidence of one who had heard an explanation difficult to disprove. Lyra asked whether he knew anyone in the duke’s household. He called her innocent. She left before giving him something more useful to repeat.

She did not know what the Bloody Table was. She had heard the name before, twice, in the passages outside the east range, spoken in the lowered voice people used for things they wanted to be overheard saying. She did not know whether it was a thing or a name for a fear. She went to Pellam and asked him, and he took off his spectacles and said that he did not know either, and that he would thank her not to ask anybody else in the chancery, and that she should go home.

The roads stayed open. The feared army did not come. Men who had postponed journeys began travelling again, relieved and embarrassed by the relief. The new authorities in Istovar assured the public that existing undertakings would be honoured. Lyra read the proclamation on the chancery wall. It was well written. It had been drafted, she thought, by somebody in Fayd’s office; she knew the turn of the sentences. It named the protection and the schedule and the tolls and the roads. It did not name Elyas.

Harald’s death made nothing easier to ask. She had thought, when she first heard of it, that it might crack something open: that a duke falling on a stair a month after his brother was beheaded in the square would make somebody somewhere ask a question. It made everybody stop asking.

She met Fayd a week later, in the passage outside the treasury, by the window where he had once offered her a room.

She had not meant to speak to him. She had meant to deliver a return to Ferrand’s clerk and go. But he was standing at the window looking down at the court, where carts were unloading, and he turned when he heard her step, and she found that she had stopped.

“Mistress Lyra.”

“The page,” she said. “Anseau’s. With both names. When did you send it?”

He did not pretend not to understand her. She gave him that.

“At the beginning of the month in which he was released.”

“I gave it to you in the early summer.”

“You did.”

“You held it.”

Fayd looked at her. Below, a cart lost a wheel-pin and a man shouted, and another man shouted back.

“I sent it,” he said, “when it would do the most good. In the summer the household would have taken it and asked me what else I knew, and I would have had to say that I knew nothing, because you had given me nothing, and they would have held him until they found out for themselves. By the autumn they had found out for themselves. Then the page let them close the matter and send him home, and let Cedric find him, and let the household say it had been an error in the names, which it was.” He turned back to the window. “If I had sent it in the summer he would have been held just as long, and you would have been the person who had tried and failed. I did not think that would be useful to anybody.”

“It would have been true.”

“Many things are true, Mistress Lyra.” He watched the cart being righted. “I didn’t use what you refused me. I want you to know that. Somebody else found it. I waited until they had, and then I used what you gave me, exactly as you gave it. That was our agreement.”

She stood beside him at the window and could not find the place where he was lying. She thought perhaps there was none.

“I won’t be taking your commissions for a while,” she said.

“I thought not.” He did not look round. “They’ll be there when you want them. I shall go on needing somebody who tells me what she won’t do.”

She left him at the window. At the end of the passage she looked back, and he was still there, watching the carts, a neat grey man in a good coat with his hands behind his back.

A letter arrived from the missing soldier’s sister.

Hawise. Lyra had not thought of her in a year. The lease had been settled long since, and she had married, and she was writing now because she had heard, all the way out on the western edge of the Scar country, what had happened in Valora, and wanted Lyra to know two things. The first was that her brother’s name had been kept in the agreement as directed, and that if he came home there would be a place for him, and she wanted to be sure that the agreement still stood now that the prince was dead. The second was a postscript, for Elyas, thanking him for writing to the lord.

Lyra put the letter down.

That afternoon she wrote to Hawise herself. She explained that Elyas had died and that the arrangement remained valid. She gave the office to which any dispute should be brought and the reference under which the earlier agreement would be found.

She read the letter three times before sending it. The practical part was clear. She could not improve the other sentence.

Then she took the envelope addressed to Mara out of the drawer, with the baker’s boy’s words inside it, and looked at it, and put it back.

Chapter Thirty-Four — The Sentence Unfinished

Master Ridow took the grammar back because the binding needed repair.

Lyra had grown accustomed to its weight in her bag. He had let her take the second volume home at last in the spring, after five years of refusing, on the grounds that she was now the only pupil he had ever had who could be trusted not to correct it, and she had carried it everywhere since. She brought it to his room intending to ask about a passage, set it down, and saw the strip of cloth at the spine lift away beneath her fingers. He made a wounded noise, took the book, and examined it with the reproach he usually reserved for her translations.

“You’ve been opening it flat.”

“It is a book.”

“It is an old book.”

“I am beginning to understand its complaints.”

He glanced up. He smiled, then laid the volume closed on his desk.

“I’ll have it looked at.”

“I was in the middle of a sentence.”

“It will remain there.”

“Will it?”

He looked at her again. This time she turned away before he could decide what kindness was required.

Outside, the garden had been cut back. The disputed pear tree carried only a few late leaves. A woman raked the leaves into a basket while the gardener complained that they would fall again tomorrow. She told him to speak to the tree about it.

Lyra stood beneath the arcade and laughed. It surprised her enough that she stayed there until she could trust her face.

Deirdre brought the news, a week after Harald’s death, with a packet of carriers’ answers and a stone in her shoe.

Deirdre ran carts on the northern roads and had carried for Lyra for three years, and was the only carrier in Valora who read every direction before she took a packet and would argue about it if it was wrong. She sat on Lyra’s second chair and took off her shoe and shook it out, and said, while she was doing it, that Ewen’s wagons had come in from the north on the Tuesday with two families and a load of cloth and three guards he had taken on at Glenruan, and that one of the guards was a knight with a bad temper who had asked at every halt for news of the prince, and one was a big man with a limp who talked to children, and one was a woman with a bow.

“They went off the road before the gate,” Deirdre said. “South. Where the prince’s lady lives.”

Lyra did not say anything. Deirdre put her shoe back on.

“I thought you’d want to know,” she said. “Since you sent the knight to my cousin’s stopping house in the first place.”

When she had gone Lyra sat at her table. Then she took a clean half-sheet and wrote on it the name of the room above Oudine’s shop, and an hour, the day after next, and one line: I can come alone, or you can come here. She did not sign it. She took it to the woman who sold thread at the corner, who went south to the villages every week with her pack, and asked her to carry it to the house with the pear tree and give it to whoever answered the door, but only if there were travellers staying there. If there were not, she was to bring it back.

The woman did not bring it back.

Afterwards she could remember too much of it.

The street had been blocked by a delivery wagon, and Riarhin had come early and been made to wait in the shop while Oudine fitted a boy for a coat for his sister’s wedding. Lyra, upstairs, had heard the boy complaining that the coat would fit more easily if he were not wearing it, and Oudine saying that a coat which fitted a chair would be of very little use at a wedding, and under both voices a silence that she knew was Riarhin standing by the window. She had picked up a coat that needed its inner pocket moved and gone down with it, because it gave her something to carry.

Riarhin had been standing by the window in the clothes she had travelled in. She had looked, for a moment, like the woman outside the baker’s five years before, and then not like her at all.

Lyra had said, “Come up.”

Upstairs she had closed the door and stood with her hand on the latch, and then crossed the room and held her. She had meant to be composed. She had shaken, and stopped, and shaken again, and Riarhin had put a hand at the back of her head and waited.

“I meant to come,” Lyra had said into her coat.

“To Mara?”

“Yes.”

“She’ll hear you.”

“That isn’t what I asked her.”

And Riarhin had said she was sorry, for that answer, and Lyra had laughed, and fetched water, and spilled it, and Riarhin had moved a sheet out of the wet, and Lyra had said Leave that too quickly, because the sheet was about a wharf and a woman who had paid twice, and the woman had not agreed to be part of this.

“Neither had Elyas,” Riarhin had said.

Lyra remembered that more exactly than anything else. She remembered Riarhin’s face when she heard herself say it, wanting it back.

Then she had told her. The carrier and the stopping house. Berent saying quietly. Asking whether Mara knew. The invitation, which she had taken out of the case and laid on the table: Jaennis’s name, the visitor from the south, the carriers. The supper. The other guests leaving. Staying. What Jaennis had asked, and what Lyra had answered, and the moment she remembered most clearly, which was the moment of deciding not to say more.

“What happened?”

“She asked whether he’d sent for anyone who might reassure him.”

“And?”

“I told her he had sent Berent north.”

“Why?”

“Because I wanted to tell her.”

She had said it almost angrily. Riarhin had not moved. Then Riarhin had asked the questions Lyra had been asking herself for six weeks: the wine, whether she had felt ill, whether she had been confused. And Lyra had answered: the same wine the others had, she had asked; not ill; not confused. Relieved.

It had sounded, said aloud to somebody who was listening, like an ordinary weakness. Lyra had heard it sound like that and had hated it and been grateful for it at once.

“Do you think there was a working?” Riarhin had asked.

“I think I know when I’m being persuaded. I think I’ve wanted people’s approval before and kept my mouth shut while wanting it.”

“That isn’t certainty.”

“No.”

And then Maelis. Lyra had not meant to say Maelis’s name. She had said that Jaennis had been learning small things, things that made an evening go more easily, and that she did not know from whom, and Riarhin had asked whom she did know, and she had said Maelis, who served Mab and had been at Jaennis’s for years and altered cloth and light for entertainments and sometimes more than that; who had been at the supper, and left before Lyra; who had not touched her or given her anything; who would understand the difference between being made comfortable and being made to confide, and who would take any question Lyra asked her straight back to Jaennis before Lyra had got home.

She had given Riarhin two of the places Maelis went. Not the linen-seller’s; the rehearsal house and the hired room. She had told her that the servants at both knew nothing. She had asked what Riarhin meant to do, and Riarhin had said speak to her, and would not say how, and Lyra had said Don’t make me wish I hadn’t told you, and heard in her own voice exactly what she was afraid of.

“Has Fayd heard this?” Riarhin had asked.

“Part of it.”

“Which part?”

“Enough to know I made an error. He asked me what I meant to do about it. I said I meant to find out whether it was mine.”

“Are you working for him?”

“When we have business together.”

“Is that safe?”

“No occupation answers that by its name.”

Riarhin had almost smiled. Lyra had not. She had told her about the clients, the people whose affairs had to stay theirs, the things she could give Fayd and the things she would not, and the people who went to him anyway. And when Riarhin had looked at the damp sheet on the table she had moved it out of reach and said: “I won’t give you their names because you’re angry. I won’t give them to Mara because she’s right to be angry. I have done harm already.”

“You think I’d use them.”

“I think you’d decide they were necessary.”

And Riarhin had said All right, and Lyra had believed her.

At the end Riarhin had asked to take the invitation, and Lyra had said she could copy the date and the names, and Riarhin had said she could remember them, and Lyra had said, “Yes. You can,” and heard the tiredness in it, and seen Riarhin stop reaching for her coat.

They had sat for a long time after that. The shop below had emptied. Lyra had turned her cup between her palms.

“He liked the house,” she had said at last. “He kept asking me whether Mara liked it. I told him to ask her.”

“She’d have told him.”

“She did. He thought she might be sparing him.”

And Riarhin had smiled, despite herself, and Lyra had looked at the smile and begun to cry again, more quietly, without trying to talk through it. Riarhin had stayed until she could.

Afterwards she remembered Riarhin staying.

Riarhin carried word back three days later, in a note left with Oudine: Mara will hear what you have. It was not an invitation. Lyra read it several times and put it in the drawer, and did not go.

She told herself there was work. There was. A supplier had received a rejection for cloth already cut to the measurements in his contract; the measurements had been amended after the cutting. Lyra found the earlier direction, obtained a hearing, and made certain the cutter’s apprentice was paid while the adults settled who would bear the waste. The supplier wanted her to tell Fayd about a rival who had benefited from the alteration. He offered names freely. When she asked what he had seen himself, he admitted that most of it came from a man who had been refused work by the rival.

“I can take the complaint about your cloth,” she said. “I cannot swear to the rest.”

“You needn’t swear. Just tell him.”

“He will ask why I believe it.”

“Does he?”

She took her fee and went. On the road home she wondered whether he had meant the question or wanted to make her ask it later, alone.

At her desk a note awaited her from Fayd’s office. He would pay for a comparison of two transport arrangements. The work was lawful, useful, and within her competence. It would take three days. It was the first word from his office since the passage outside the treasury. She left it on her table for a day, under the whetstone, and then she accepted, limiting the return to the carriers’ undertakings and their performance. No private letters would be copied. The condition was agreed without alteration. The fee arrived when promised. She did not know whether that meant he had forgiven her the hearing, or had never thought there was anything to forgive, or wished her to go on taking his money until she could not afford to stop.

She heard nothing from Riarhin for a month. Then, in the first week of the frost, a note came up the stair in a hand she did not know, very small and very neat, on paper that smelled faintly of some flower she could not name.

A woman called Aveline Marr has lately come to court from the north. You have met her before, under another name. When you meet her again, please do not know her. She asks me to say that she is sorry it must be done this way. M.

Lyra read it standing at the window. She read it again. Then she held it over the candle and watched it burn down to her fingers, and dropped the last corner in the basin, and stood looking at the ash on the water.

She did not know what it meant. She knew enough about Maelis to guess at what kind of thing it might mean, and she did not let herself guess further. Riarhin had gone somewhere Lyra could not follow, and had sent to say so, kindly, through the one person Lyra could least bear to owe anything.

The next morning she hired the slow horse and rode south to Mara.

The garden had been tidied. The cut branch was gone from the path. On the path a boy knelt beside a broken fastening on the gate, turning it over with a concentration that reminded Lyra of Berent years before. Nelle came out of the kitchen and saw her and stood still for a moment, and then went back in without speaking, and a moment later Mara came out with a towel across her arm.

They looked at each other across the garden.

“You came,” said Mara.

“You said you’d hear what I had.”

“I did say that.” Mara looked at the horse, and at Lyra’s face, and at the bag on Lyra’s shoulder with the papers in it. “Have you eaten?”

“No.”

“Then you’ll eat first.”

They ate at the table where Elyas had received visitors. The little weight shaped like a sleeping dog stood on a shelf. Mara had left one chair against the wall because she needed space for a basket of mending. Lyra took another without looking at it again. Neither of them said anything about the letter, or the gate at the burial place, or the lower court. They talked about the mare, who was lame, and the stable roof, which leaked, and the man in the village who was going to mend it and had been going to for a fortnight. They talked about Riarhin, carefully, and about Berent, who was sleeping most of the day and eating the rest and would not talk to anybody about the road.

When the bowls were cleared Mara brought out the petition.

It was a fair copy, several sheets. The dates of the arrest and the examinations. A request for the depositions on which the sentence rested. An account of what had been taken from the house, with Cedric’s list and Nelle’s slate side by side, and the green book, which had not been on the list and had later been entered. Statements from people who had come to the house. Some of the statements were useful. One man had written three pages about a conversation he had not attended. Mara wanted to keep it because the account was favourable. Lyra asked her to keep it separately.

“I won’t throw it away.”

“Neither would I. But don’t put it where they can use it to discredit what you saw.”

Mara set it aside with visible reluctance.

“And this?”

It was her own account of the search: the book omitted from the list, Cedric’s promise to record what was taken, Elyas’s refusal spoken at the door. Lyra read it slowly.

“Keep every word.”

Then Mara laid a last sheet on the table. It was blank except for a heading, in her hand: Persons who can say what he asked of them.

“I want the names,” she said. “The ones who came. Vautrin. The carter. The cousin. The two in the garden. All of them. They can say he refused. They can say he told them to go home.” She put her hand flat on the empty sheet. “You have them. He wrote to you about half of them. You kept his letters.”

“Yes.”

“Then give them to me.”

Lyra looked at the blank sheet.

“I’ll give you the dates,” she said. “Everything I gave the hearing, and more. The chancery entries. The priest’s witness. The household’s entry of the northern communication, which I have from the examiner in his own words. I’ll give you the baker’s boy.” She took the envelope out of her bag, the one addressed to Mara that she had not sent, and laid it on the table. “He was on a mounting block in the square with a basket of bread. He heard all of it. He says Elyas said go home. He says he said the door twice. I’ll put my name to that. I’ll put my name to all of it, as the person who took it down, and they’ll know that I’m working for you, and Fayd will know, and I expect I’ll lose his commissions, and some others.” She met Mara’s eyes. “That’s what I can give.”

“And the names.”

“No.”

Mara’s hand closed on the blank sheet.

“You’re protecting them.”

“Yes.”

“From what? They’ll say he refused. It can only help them.”

“They’ll say he refused,” said Lyra, “and then they’ll be asked why they came, and what they wanted, and who else was there, and who else they know, the way Anseau was asked, every day for a week, until he told. The household already knows who went to this house. It has the green book. It has Anseau. It doesn’t have them standing up in a room saying yes, I offered, and he said no. If you put their names on that sheet, you give it that.”

“He wouldn’t give their names either,” said Mara.

“I know.”

“He died for it.”

“I know.”

Mara looked at her across the table. Her face was very still. It was, Lyra thought, the face Elyas must have seen in the room with the open door, when he said he could not give them.

“You’re just like him,” Mara said.

“I’m not. He opened the door. I just won’t tell them who came through it.”

Neither of them said anything. Outside, the boy at the gate had got the fastening working and was swinging the gate back and forth to hear it click.

Then Mara took the blank sheet off the table and folded it and put it with the favourable account she had set aside.

“I’ll keep it,” she said. “In case.”

“Keep it.”

“I’m still angry with you.”

“I know.”

“About the supper. About the names. About coming to the gate and not coming in.” Lyra looked up sharply, and Mara said, “Nelle saw you. At the burial place. She told me last week. She hadn’t said before because she thought I’d be angry, and she was right, I was.” She put the folded sheet in the chest. “I’d have wanted you to come in. I don’t know if I’d have let you stand beside me. I’d have wanted you to come in.”

Lyra could not speak.

“Help me with the dates,” Mara said. “Please.”

They worked until the light went. At the end Mara asked whether Lyra could help obtain a clearer account of where the first examination had been held and who had witnessed the corrections. Lyra said she would ask what could be released.

“Don’t promise more.”

“I won’t.”

In the garden, when Lyra was leaving, Mara stopped by the tree and put a hand on the rough place where the branch had been cut. The exposed wood had begun to darken.

“I thought about selling,” she said.

“Where would you go?”

“I hadn’t got that far.” Mara looked at the kitchen door. “I can’t keep it exactly as it was. I don’t want a visitor to think I have forgotten him because I needed room to mend a shirt.”

“They can think what they like.”

Mara gave her a tired smile.

“You’ve never believed that.”

“No.”

Mara pressed her arm. The pressure was brief and strong. Then she went in, and Lyra untied the slow horse and rode home in the dark, and did not mind that it was slow.

Chapter Thirty-Five — The Wrong Door

Deirdre was angry because she had carried the wrong paper forty miles.

She said so under the arch of the lesser palace gate, with the packet in one hand and the other pointing at the writing on its cover, while servants carrying baskets went round them on both sides. It was a claim for freight. Deirdre had carried a load of dyed wool for the queen’s household from the northern mills in the autumn and had not been paid for it, because the household’s clerk said the claim had to be supported by the mill’s account of what was loaded, and Deirdre had brought the mill’s account, and the clerk had looked at it that morning and said it was the account for a different load. He had given the whole packet back to her. There was a bond posted against the freight, released now that the queen’s officer had signed, and it was to pay the claim, and it could not pay the claim until the right account was in the packet.

“It’s the same mill,” Deirdre said. “The same wool. The same week. I loaded it myself.”

“Let me see.”

Lyra took the packet by its cord and held it flat against her palm and opened it along the fold. The claim was there, and the released bond with the queen’s officer’s mark on it, and the mill’s account. She read the account. It was for forty bales of red. The claim was for forty bales of blue.

“They dyed the red first,” said Deirdre. “Then the blue. The same week. The clerk at the mill gave me the wrong sheet.”

“Then the right sheet is at the mill.”

“Forty miles.”

“Forty miles.” Lyra took the pencil she kept in her sleeve and made a small mark in the margin of the claim, beside the line where the account should have been referred to, and wrote beneath it in very small letters: Account for the blue to be supplied; red enclosed in error. “Show him this. When you bring the right one, he’ll see where it goes. He won’t have to send it back again.”

Deirdre looked at the mark. She folded the packet shut.

“You’re a good woman,” she said. She put her hand on Lyra’s wrist for a moment in thanks, and went off through the gate towards the street with the packet under her arm.

The cord had left a pale mark across Lyra’s palm. She looked at it and rubbed it with her thumb, and when she looked up there was a woman standing in the archway watching her.

She was tall, in a brown gown with a narrow cuff. Her hair was red, and dressed high, and there was more of it than Lyra would have known what to do with. The face under it was a face Lyra had never seen: broad across the cheekbones, generous in the mouth, older than Riarhin by some years, a face that had laughed a great deal and did not look as though it had been doing so lately.

The eyes were Riarhin’s.

Lyra stood among the people going past in both directions and looked at the gown, and the hair, and the face, and came back to the eyes, and the burned note on the water in her basin came back to her, very small and neat, smelling of a flower she could not name.

“Aveline,” she said.

The woman inclined her head.

“Have we been introduced?” Lyra asked. Her own voice sounded quite ordinary to her, which surprised her.

“Maelis told me to expect you.”

Lyra came nearer.

“Is it difficult?” she asked quietly.

“Yes.”

“Good to know something is.”

She had not meant it to come out like that. She saw it land, and saw Riarhin, inside the other woman’s face, accept it without flinching, and was ashamed and not sorry.

“Have you seen Mara?” Riarhin asked.

“Yesterday.”

“How was it?”

“We spoke.”

“Did she—”

“Riarhin.”

The name was barely breathed. A man carrying a folded carpet passed behind them, and Lyra waited until he had cleared the arch.

“You needn’t arrange us. I know you want to. We can be unhappy with each other and still go back.”

“I wasn’t—”

“Yes, you were.”

Riarhin let the denial go. She looked at Lyra’s hand, where the cord had been.

“Can I help with that?”

“The packet? No. It has reached the right person.”

“And you?”

Lyra did not know what to do with the question. She had a claim for blue wool in somebody else’s hand and a burned note in her basin and a friend in front of her wearing a stranger’s face.

“I’m going home,” she said. “To eat something I didn’t have to discuss with anyone before choosing it.”

“That sounds pleasant.”

“It may be. You could try it.”

Riarhin glanced past her towards the lighted windows of the palace. Lyra followed the glance and looked back, and found she was smiling, a little, tiredly.

“Go,” she said. “You’re expected.”

Riarhin touched her hand. It was an awkward place for such a gesture, under the arch, among the servants, and Lyra almost withdrew before she understood what was being offered. She let the contact last a moment.

“Another day,” said Riarhin.

“Yes.”

Lyra went out through the gate. She did not look back. She walked home through the early dark and bought a piece of fish from the stall at the corner because she wanted fish and nobody could tell her not to, and cooked it badly over her little fire, and ate it at her table with the whetstone beside the plate, and thought about the other woman’s face, and about the eyes inside it, and about what it must cost, and did not let herself think about how it had been done.

The next day was ordinary.

She delivered Fayd’s transport comparison to his office in the morning and was paid. She spent the afternoon with a widow whose husband’s back pay had been sent to the wrong village and found the village, which was the right name in the wrong duchy, and wrote to it. In the evening she went to Isaut’s and ate supper with the cooper, who said four words in the course of it, all of them kind. She walked home under a clear sky. She slept well.

The man came shortly after breakfast.

He was polite and young and had a cold, and he stood at the top of Oudine’s stair holding a folded paper, and said that the keeper of the military correspondence would be grateful if Mistress Lyra would attend at the west office that morning to explain a discrepancy.

“What discrepancy?”

“I don’t know, mistress. I was only told to ask.”

Lyra looked at the paper. It was a summons of the ordinary kind, under the seal of the office that kept the Marshal’s correspondence. She had received a dozen like it in five years about dates in returns and the pay of men in companies whose captains had sent the wrong list. She had gone to all of them. They had all been about dates.

“I have somebody coming at ten,” she said.

“It shouldn’t take long, mistress.”

She took her cloak. On her table were two letters she had been answering, one from Hawise about a new roof on the house that might one day be her brother’s, one from the widow’s wrong village. She put them both in the drawer, because she did not like to leave letters on a table in an empty room, and locked it. Then she knocked on the door across the landing, where Mistress Joye lived, who was old and deaf in one ear and made lace for Oudine, and asked her, if a woman came asking at ten, to tell her that Lyra had been called to the west office and would be back before noon.

“The west office,” said Mistress Joye. “Before noon.”

“Yes.”

“I’ll tell her.”

Lyra went down the stair and out into the street with the young man, who sneezed, and apologised, and sneezed again.

The west office was a long low room with stools in a row along one wall and a desk at the end. A boy was arranging the stools. A clerk at the desk took her name and wrote it down and asked her to wait. She waited on the end stool. After a while a man came out of an inner door and asked her to come in.

He was not the keeper of the military correspondence. He was an examiner. He said so, courteously, and asked her to sit, and there was another man in the room with a board, and she understood before she sat down that it was not about dates.

“You were at the lesser palace gate two afternoons ago.”

“Yes.”

“You met a carrier called Deirdre there.”

“Yes.”

“She handed you a packet.”

“Yes.”

“You opened it.”

“Yes.”

“And passed something back.”

“The packet. With a mark on it.” Lyra looked at him. “It was a claim for freight. She’d brought the wrong account from the mill. I marked where the right one should go.”

The examiner took a sheet from the table.

“The household has information,” he said, “that at the lesser gate two afternoons ago you communicated to the carrier Deirdre a copy of protected military instructions, namely the muster order for the horse companies of the queen’s army, with its direction for their approach through Cecily Orme’s orchard.”

Lyra sat very still.

She had received a packet from Deirdre. She had given it back with a pencil mark in the margin. No military direction had been in it. She knew this as exactly as she had ever known anything. The very clearness of the recollection made the accusation feel, for a moment, like a mistake which would be corrected as soon as she explained it.

“No,” she said.

“You can answer the particulars.”

“Show me the particulars.”

He read them again. A muster order. A direction for the horse companies. Cecily Orme’s orchard. She did not know who Cecily Orme was. She did not know where the orchard was, or what the horse companies were doing there, or why anybody would want a copy of an order about it.

“My correspondence is at my lodging,” she said. “My own record of the day. What I wrote on the claim. The claim itself is with Deirdre. The mill’s account is with Deirdre. Ask her for them. Ask the household’s clerk who gave the packet back to her that morning. Ask the mill. All of it existed before anybody came to my door.”

The man with the board wrote the requests down. The examiner was listening. She needed him to listen, and found herself speaking more carefully, giving him the route by which he could discover the thing she knew already.

“May I see the alleged enclosure?”

“Later.”

“Is the original preserved?”

“It is held.”

“Where?”

He looked up.

“You are answering questions.”

“Yes,” Lyra said. “I would like one answered.”

He did not answer it. He asked her instead about Deirdre, and how long she had known her, and what else Deirdre carried and for whom, and whether Lyra had ever given Deirdre a packet to carry north. Lyra said yes, many, for clients, for carriers’ business, and once, years ago, a letter to a friend in Ubral. The examiner asked the friend’s name. Lyra looked at him and said it. He wrote it down.

At the end he said that she would be asked to remain while the particulars were examined.

“How long?”

“Until they are examined.”

“I have somebody coming at ten.”

He looked at her with something that might have been sympathy.

“It’s past eleven, mistress,” he said.

At the lodging they took her knife.

It was the little knife she used to cut paper and had forgotten she wore. There was no wooden marker this time. Her purse was counted in front of her. The coins were wrapped and sealed; she was asked to confirm the sum. She did, because a missing coin would still be missing tomorrow and she had no wish to spend tomorrow proving it.

A clerk came through while they were doing it and spoke to the examiner in a low voice, and Lyra heard papers and the seamstress’s and an order, and understood that somebody had gone to her room. She thought of the drawer, locked, with Hawise’s roof in it, and the widow’s wrong village, and the sheet headed Things that mean nothing. Mistress Joye would tell them she was due back before noon.

She kept her next question to herself.

She was brought to a room with a bed, a basin, and a shutter which opened only partway.

The woman who brought water showed her how to fasten the shutter against rain. There was a trick to it: lift before pulling. Lyra tried and trapped her finger. The woman took her hand without asking, inspected the nail, and said it would bruise.

“Have you eaten?”

“No.”

“There’s broth.”

“Thank you.”

When the woman had gone, Lyra stood beside the bed. She had not thought to ask whether she might send a message. She had asked for the enclosure, the original, the witness, and had forgotten to tell anybody that somebody would need to know where she slept.

She sat down. Her finger throbbed.

On the other side of the door a key turned. The sound was small enough to have belonged to a cupboard at home.

Chapter Thirty-Six — The Second Volume

In the morning she asked for someone she knew.

The request was written down. She named no one at first, then understood that this made it easier to leave the answer indefinite. She thought of Riarhin, and did not say her name, because Riarhin was somebody else now and Lyra did not know what it would cost her to be asked for. She named Pellam. The officer wrote the chancellor with a small lift of his eyebrows and said the request required approval.

“Will you record when I made it?”

He put down the date.

At the next examination she asked when the orchard direction had been made. The examiner answered that the date would be established by comparison with the military office’s papers.

“Then establish it.”

“We intend to.”

“Before you require Deirdre to agree she received it.”

He looked at her. She had spoken too sharply to a man who had not yet decided whether she was helping him or obstructing him. She made herself begin again.

“She received a freight claim with the wrong mill account in it. The right account is at the mill, and the claim went back to the household’s clerk the same afternoon. If a sheet was added to it afterwards, the order of the additions matters.”

“That will be examined.”

She wanted to believe him. The wanting frightened her more than the refusal at the gate.

“The carrier,” she said. “Deirdre. Is she held?”

The examiner looked at his papers for longer than he needed to.

“She has been examined,” he said. “She was shown a copy of the sheet. She says she never saw it before. She says she opened every fold of the freight claim herself at the gate, in front of you, and took the whole of it back to the household’s clerk, and that there was no such sheet in it, and she has asked that the clerk be examined as to what he received.” He turned a page. “She has been instructed to remain available. She is at the wool store where she keeps her carts.”

Lyra breathed out.

“She’ll remain available,” she said. “She has carts to run.”

“So she told us,” said the examiner, and for the first time something that was almost a smile crossed his face. “At length.”

He showed her, then, what he had not shown her at the door: a copy of the sheet. It was a fair copy in a clerk’s hand of an order for the muster of the horse companies, and beneath it, in the same hand, the direction for their approach, through the orchard strip belonging to Cecily Orme, rather than by the lower lane. It meant nothing to her. It was the kind of thing she had copied a hundred times for the Marshal’s office about a hundred approaches, and she had never seen this one.

“If the part about the orchard was made after the gate—” she began.

“That will be established, mistress.”

She looked at the copy, at the name of a woman she did not know and an orchard she had never seen, and could not make it tell her anything more.

The following afternoon she was brought bread.

It came in the afternoon, eight small rolls wrapped in a cloth, and the woman who carried things into the rooms put it on the table and said that a lady had left it at the west office for her.

“Which lady?”

The woman looked at the slip tied to the cloth.

“Aveline Marr.”

Lyra looked at the bread. Then she laughed. She had not expected to, and it hurt somewhere under her ribs, and she could not stop for some time, and the woman stood by the door with an expression of great patience until she had finished.

“Is it funny?” the woman asked.

“No,” said Lyra, wiping her eyes. “Not at all. Thank her, if you can.”

“I can’t. She’s gone.”

“Then thank the bread.”

The woman looked at her as though she might be ill, and went out. Lyra ate one of the rolls. It was very good. Somebody had chosen the baker with care.

The letter came already opened.

The seal was broken and somebody had written across the outer fold Read and passed and a mark she did not know. The writing inside was small and slanting and she knew it at once, as she had known it on a note under her door five years before that said the writer had not wanted anyone to stand in the cold on her account.

Riarhin had spoken to Jaennis.

She did not say how, or where, or under what name. She said that Jaennis had acknowledged using a charm at the supper, to encourage confidence, without asking Lyra’s leave. She put down Jaennis’s words as closely as she remembered them. Lyra read them three times. They were very like Jaennis. They were reasonable and a little sad and did not apologise for anything except the lack of asking.

Riarhin did not say what it meant. She did not say that Lyra had done nothing wrong. She did not say that Lyra had done something wrong.

Below that she had written about a melon.

A man called Orrel, whose melon Riarhin had enjoyed at supper, had lost another to a squirrel. It had got into the growing frame through the opening left for air and hollowed out his best fruit from underneath. He had admired it all morning before lifting it. When Riarhin next visited, he had told her about it before either of them sat down.

Lyra sat on the bed with the letter on her knee.

She read the part about Jaennis again. A charm. To encourage confidence. It was the first time anybody had given the thing a name. She had spent two months looking for the moment in her memory where not wanting to say it had become wanting to, and there had been no such moment, and now somebody had told her why. She waited to feel the relief of it. It did not come. She tried to remember the gladness at the table without looking for the charm in it.

She read about the melon again. She laughed again, less painfully, and turned the sheet towards the light to make out the last cramped words beneath it.

With her acknowledgment of the letter she asked for a comb and a woollen wrap, without pins, because the room was cold in the mornings. The woman wrote it at the bottom of the entry for the letter.

When they came, she was waiting by the shutter. The wrap was grey and thick and smelled of somebody else’s chest. The comb was bone, with a tooth missing near one end.

Lyra held it. She had borrowed it years ago, on the road in Acasia, when her own comb had broken in the cold, and she had complained about the missing tooth every morning for a week and gone on using it, and given it back, and Riarhin had kept it. It was not a very good comb. It was the only thing in the room she recognised.

That afternoon she was permitted a scrap of paper and a pencil, and she wrote:

The tooth is still missing. Tell Orrel the squirrel has excellent judgement.

Then, beneath, because the woman had said she might ask for what she needed: that the room was cold in the morning and warm after the kitchen fires had been lit, and that she would like clean linen if it could be sent.

There was a little space left at the bottom. She looked at it for some time. Then she wrote, smaller:

I would like to see somebody I know.

She gave it to the woman who carried things. The woman read it, slowly, moving her lips over the longer words, and at the end looked up at Lyra and then down again and folded it without saying anything.

The linen came later. It was a shift and two shirts, plain and clean and well mended. One of them had a patch at the elbow in a thread that did not quite match, set in with small, hard, even stitches that Lyra had seen on a dozen things in a kitchen with a pear tree outside the door.

She put it on. It was a little too long in the sleeve. She turned the cuffs back and sat on the bed in it, and did not cry, and after a while did.

Nobody came.

They kept her.

She learned the noises of the passage. A man cleared his throat before unlocking a door. Someone else always struck a heel against a loose board. One afternoon food came late because a pot had cracked and the woman who brought it was angry about having warned them.

Lyra listened to the complaint. The woman apologised, then grew irritated at having apologised and said the broth was no worse for the wait.

“It isn’t,” Lyra said.

“You haven’t tasted it.”

She took a spoonful.

“It isn’t.”

The woman smiled despite herself. She had a little burn on her wrist. Lyra recognised the narrow red shape made by touching the rim of a hot pan and nearly told her to put something on it. Then she remembered how unwelcome advice could be when a person had come carrying help.

“Is there someone who collects your requests?” she asked instead.

“What sort?”

“For things allowed in the room.”

“I can ask.”

“A book.”

The woman glanced towards the shutter.

“You can read in that light?”

“For part of the day.”

“What book?”

Lyra almost named the clerk who could find the documents she needed. She almost asked for paper enough to reconstruct every meeting of the last fortnight. She had begun doing that in her head. Each repetition altered what she thought she remembered, and she had no wish to make a fresh accusation out of her own fear.

“A grammar.”

That afternoon she was permitted a little paper.

It was a long time after the linen. The frost had come and gone twice. No letter had come since Riarhin’s; no bread since the eight rolls. The woman with the burn on her wrist still came, and the man who cleared his throat before he unlocked the door, and the examiner, twice, with the same questions and the answer that the date would be established. Lyra did not know where Riarhin was, or Mara, or whether either of them knew where she was. She knew the shape of the shutter and the trick of it and the exact hour the kitchen fires were lit.

She wrote a request for the original alleged enclosure to be preserved with the receiving entry and any later additions. She asked again to see someone she knew. She identified Deirdre’s freight claim and the mill account for the wrong load, and described the correction in the margin. She asked for the mill’s loading book to be checked against them. She read the page until she was satisfied it asked for what could be proved.

There was enough room below it for the book.

She sat with the pen lifted, remembering Riarhin crouched beside the garden channel, listening to Master Ridow dispute the name of a plant. She had intended to go north. She had meant to learn enough of the language to surprise her, to be a visitor who could bring something besides another request. She had started learning Kahfagian so that she could read the name of a town on a letter from a boat. The thought was so ordinary that she had to put the pen down.

She thought of Ridow’s desk, the torn spine of the grammar, the garden beyond his window. Would he be questioned if he sent it? She could name the binding and the damaged spine. He need not know anything about the case to find the right volume.

After a while she picked it up again.

The second volume of the Kahfagian grammar. Brown cloth binding, the spine torn. Master Ridow at the University has it. He will know the one.

She left a space between that and the requests above, so a person could copy only what it was safe to carry.

When the woman returned for the bowl, Lyra showed her the lower lines.

“Only this part, if the rest cannot go.”

“I don’t promise they’ll send it.”

“I know.”

The woman read the description twice. She could read slowly, and Lyra waited without supplying a word. At the bottom the woman stopped.

“Do you need to say why?”

Lyra looked at the empty space beneath the description. She wrote one more line.

I was in the middle of a sentence.
